# Irie v1: design and implementation plan

Date: 2026-10-26. Status: v1 plan. Read with [AGENTS.md](../AGENTS.md). Every source is listed in [PROVENANCE.md](../PROVENANCE.md).

Irie runs coding agents from any supplier, cloud or local, side by side in one calm view: a narrow sidebar on the left and the selected agent's live terminal on the right. v1 does this end to end for Claude Code and gives fine control over every agent. You can see who needs you, pause an orchestrator or one of its subagents at its next tool call, freeze a session instantly, raise a task's priority on the fly, and undo what you did. The seams for every other harness also ship in v1: adapters, a normalised event envelope and a socket API.

**Glossary.**
- **Session:** one agent conversation in its own tmux window. It has an orchestrator (the main agent) and any subagents the orchestrator starts.
- **Tool boundary:** the moment just before a tool call runs, or just after one finishes.
- **Hold:** a hook that does not answer until the user releases it.
- **Freeze:** SIGSTOP of a session's whole process tree.
- **Slot:** the right-hand pane where an agent's live terminal is swapped in.
- **Mirror:** a read-only pane that shows one subagent.
- **Profile:** a named launch recipe (harness, model, effort, permission mode, args, env).
- **Context note:** text that Irie puts in front of an agent at a tool boundary or prompt.

---

## 1. Goal and non-goals

### 1.1 Requests and where v1 answers them

| Request | v1 answer | Where |

| Different options for closing tabs and sub tabs, and moving tabs and workspaces, your way | Pin a card to a Pinned group at the top, which no bulk close reaches; close the others, those below, all finished or a group's, or cards picked with ⌘ and ⇧, each as one step one undo takes back; a × on a hovered card and a middle click; keys to move a card; `ui.new_tabs` for where a new card goes; a helper or teammate hidden from its card, or stopped. Your groups, spaces and a workspace pane beside each agent come next ([workspaces.md](proposals/workspaces.md)) | §4.1, §4.5 |
| A copy of Irie a company runs as its own product: rename it in one place, leave the personal plugins out, use the company's approved VPN, fit a long name, tips in the bottom bar, the core on its servers | `internal/brand` holds the names and everything takes them from there; `Tags = "noprivacy,nowriting,nomarkets,nomail,noterms,noextension,noyoink,nojammin"` builds without the plugins' code and keeps the VPN plugin, which `VPNs` pins to the company's VPN; names up to 30 characters fit; `ui.bar = "tips"`. Folded, it shows its size and how many of its sessions need you. A parked session is out of the way: it leaves the *to verify* count. A parked card goes there at once, last in Parked, and an unparked one goes last among the rest, just above Parked. Every note carries `trod.rules` and `trod.guidelines`; `irie ctl trod` says what it did | §4.10 |
| Arch agents, the first of which is Mikael: a helper to every Irie user, against evil and for the good they want to see in the world | Every Claude Code session hears Mikael's word as it starts, and so does each subagent; any agent can ask the `mikael` agent whether something is right, a scam or a trap; before a call that could do harm that can't be taken back runs, he weighs it and stops one that would do evil, telling the agent why; `+mikael` starts a session with him; `arch.mikael.good` holds the good the user wants to see, in their words | §4.11 |
| Learning resources and a tutor mode, configurable, with pictures | `learn.tutor` runs Claude Code sessions in its Explanatory or Learning output style, and `+explain` or `+teach` sets it for one task; in a tutor mode an agent draws small diagrams where a picture helps (`learn.visual`); a teach-mode turn that leaves a part for you to write is your turn; Irie's tips under Claude Code's spinner; the `learn` op serves the Help menu's resources and glossary ([learning.md](proposals/learning.md)) | §3.3, §4.2, §4.8, §5.5 |
| Anonymous research: Mullvad's relays as exits, private search with SearXNG, Tor | `+anon` and `+tor` tasks run in compartments, each with an exit of its own that nothing else of the user's uses; Irie's own SearXNG and Tor; search, fetch and evidence through the exit, with WebFetch, WebSearch and other MCP servers shut off; off until `irie setup anon` | §4.16 |

| Other VPNs than Mullvad, chosen in the config, and in a company's copy the VPN the company approves | The VPN plugin follows the VPN `vpn.provider` names: Mullvad VPN, Tailscale, NetBird, Cloudflare WARP, NordVPN, Cisco Secure Client, GlobalProtect, FortiClient, the system's own VPNs, or any other through commands of the person's own; a copy's brand names the VPNs it offers and what they connect to, and keeps the VPN plugin without the privacy plugin; `irie setup vpn`, `:vpn use` | §4.18 |
| Bitwarden as a plugin, filling accounts and passwords from it | The Bitwarden plugin fills the sign-in pages of your web panes from your vault. The Mac app unlocks it with your master password, keeps only the panes' logins, in its own memory, and lets them go when the Mac locks or sleeps; no agent can ask for one | §4.17 |
| A chaos monkey agent for stress testing products and features, whatever we point it at | `+chaos @repo TASK` starts a session on `/irie:chaos`, Irie's chaos monkey skill: it learns what the target must keep doing, then breaks its inputs, timing, processes, disk, network and load on purpose, in a copy it owns, never anything live, shared or another's, and reports each failure with a seed that replays it; any agent can run the skill on what it built; `profiles.<name>.skill` starts any profile's sessions on a skill of Irie's | §4.19 |
| Learn how Higgsfield grew, and bring its ways to Irie | A launch kit for every release, as each of Higgsfield's feature launches came with a campaign. `docs/demo/kit.sh` writes what to show and what to say from the changelog, the commits and the reels, with the phone cuts to attach, and `kit.sh check` goes over a kit before anything is posted, as Higgsfield reviews each submission. Pay for reach, secret rates and demos better than the product are left out ([launch-kits.md](proposals/launch-kits.md)) | docs/demo |

### 1.2 Not in v1

| Not in v1 | Why |
|---|---|
| Ghostty native splits or tabs as the engine | Agents would die when Ghostty quits, and scripting cannot move a live terminal or read its screen. tmux keeps agents alive with no window open and can swap, capture and steer panes. |
| Embedding libghostty | It would mean rewriting Irie as a native app, and it is macOS-only. The UI already talks to the core over a socket (§2.4), so a native front-end can come later without touching the core. |
| Full adapters for harnesses other than Claude Code | In v1 they run in the generic tier: launch, freeze, alive/exited, typed notes. Full adapters follow v1 (§3.2). |
| Budgets beyond the plan's windows, cross-model review | These need usage oracles and adapters for other suppliers. v1 routes by cost per success (§6.4) on profiles with what each costs, the plan's use from the status line, the local preflight with its memory check, and the event envelope (§6.2, §6.3). |
| Cost tracking via OpenTelemetry; driving agents over ACP | After v1. Cost comes from Claude Code's status line instead (§6.3), and the event envelope maps onto both. |
| Editing the user's agent config files | Hooks, settings and env are injected per launch, so there is nothing to clean up. |
| Screen-scraping state detectors | They break whenever a vendor changes its UI. Irie reads hooks first; its only screen read is the screen tail it shows when a spawn stalls. |
| Deleting branches, or worktrees that hold work | Risk of data loss. v1 never deletes a branch. It removes only a closed session's worktree whose branch has landed with nothing else in it or running in it, and reopen adds it back (§4.5). |
| Windows without WSL | Irie needs tmux and POSIX signals, so on Windows it runs inside WSL 2, with Irie for Windows as its window ([WINDOWS.md](WINDOWS.md)). |

### 1.3 After v1, in order

1. Harness adapters: Codex CLI first, then opencode, Gemini CLI and pi, then goose, hermes-agent, Amp and crush (§3.2).
2. Routing, and agents working in tandem (§6.2).
3. Pop an agent out into its own native Ghostty tab (§7.4).
4. Sibling awareness (which agents work in the same repo, and on which branches), context-window gauges, and a focus mode (prioritise one session and pause the rest at their next tool call).
5. Cost for harnesses other than Claude Code, `irie gc` for old worktrees, and desktop notifications when a session turns red.

---

## 2. Architecture

### 2.1 Principles

1. **tmux is the engine.** It keeps agents alive with no window open, swaps panes into view, reports process facts and carries typed input. Ghostty is the window around it.
2. **One writer.** `irie core` owns all state and every rule, and is the only process that drives tmux. The sidebar, the Mac app, `irie ctl`, the hook shim, the subagent mirrors and any other front-end are all clients of its socket API, which [CORE.md](CORE.md) describes for whoever writes one: irie-core. A client keeps no rules of its own, not even what the words in its task bar mean (`input`).
3. **State is derived, not stored.** The core records facts in an append-only journal and computes each row's state from them.
4. **Inject, never edit.** Hooks, settings, env, Irie's own skills and its MCP servers go in per launch, through `--settings`, `--plugin-dir`, `--mcp-config`, argv and env. User config files are not touched.
5. **Fail open, except for a pause.** If Irie is down, hooks return at once and agents keep working. An agent the user paused stays held through its gate file.
6. **Restart without disturbing.** A hard requirement: restarting or replacing any part of Irie (the core, the sidebar, the placeholder, Irie.app, an install) never stops, restarts or signals an agent or any process an agent started. Only the user's own close, pause, freeze, promote or move acts on an agent. `tools/live.sh` checks every agent's process after an install and fails loudly if one changed.
7. **Responsive whatever the load.** The windows answer the user at once however busy the machine is and whatever other programs do to it: an app that takes the memory or the CPU, a stray signal, a killed process. Irie keeps its own processes running and out of the background, never waits on tmux past a deadline, and draws the core's news a batch at a time so a click never waits behind it (§2.9).

### 2.2 Processes

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  launcher(["Irie.app or irie"])
  subgraph term["Ghostty window (any terminal on Linux)"]
    client["tmux client: irie attach"]
  end
  subgraph srv["private tmux server: tmux -L irie"]
    subgraph s1["session irie (what you see)"]
      ui["pane 0: irie ui (sidebar)"]
      slot["pane 1: viewport slot"]
    end
    subgraph s2["session irie-agents (never attached)"]
      core["window core: irie core"]
      host["agent window: irie host"]
      agent["claude, own process group"]
      mirror["subagent window: irie mirror (read-only)"]
    end
  end
  shim["irie hook (shim)"]
  ctl["irie ctl and scripts"]
  files[("state dir: journal, state.json, gate files")]
  tr[("Claude transcripts, JSONL")]

  launcher -->|"AppleScript: open or focus"| term
  launcher -->|"irie up"| core
  client --> s1
  host -->|"fork, setpgid, foreground"| agent
  agent -->|"runs on each hook event"| shim
  shim -->|"unix socket, NDJSON"| core
  ui -->|"socket API"| core
  mirror -->|"socket API"| core
  ctl -->|"socket API"| core
  core -->|"new-window, swap-pane, capture-pane"| srv
  core -.->|"polling now, control mode in M11"| srv
  core -->|"freeze: SIGSTOP, thaw: SIGCONT"| agent
  core --> files
  core -->|"tail"| tr
  mirror -->|"tail"| tr
  shim -.->|"gate file when core is down"| files
```

| Process | Runs in | Role | Talks to |
|---|---|---|---|
| `irie` | the user's terminal, or Irie.app | Runs `irie up`, then opens or focuses the Irie window in Ghostty (macOS, from M9) or attaches in the current terminal | tmux, `osascript` |
| `irie up` | any terminal | Idempotent. Starts the server and the core if the socket does not answer `hello`, and waits up to 5 s for it. If the running core or UI is a different version, respawns those panes (`respawn-pane -k`); agents are untouched | tmux, socket |
| `irie attach` | any terminal | `exec tmux -L irie attach -t irie`. Inside another tmux it refuses unless given `--nested`. Inside Irie's own server it runs `switch-client` | tmux |
| `irie core --supervise` | `irie-agents:core` | Holds `core.lock` (flock). Owns the registry, state derivation, gates, notes, undo log, journal, worktrees, spawning, the swap-in and observation, and runs the freeze dog, Raphael's watch, which restarts a frozen Irie.app or sidebar (§2.4, §4.11), keeps Irie's own processes running (§2.9) and has the core leave when its own loop stands still for `arch.raphael.core` (§4.11). The supervisor restarts it with backoff (0.1, 0.2, 0.4 … 5 s), continues it when something stops it and keeps it out of the background (§2.9) | socket (server), tmux, hosts (signals), transcripts |
| `irie ui --supervise` | `irie:0.0` | The sidebar, a pure client of the socket. It never calls tmux | socket |
| `irie host --spec <file>` | the first process of every agent pane | Owns the agent's process group; continues stops it did not cause, but leaves a frozen agent stopped; says who it is in `host.json`; records the exit status | the agent (fork/exec), socket (best effort) |
| `irie hook` | started by the agent for each hook event | Forwards the event, waits while held, prints the harness's reply | socket, gate file |
| `irie statusline` | started by Claude Code for each status line update (§6.3) | Forwards the session's cost, context and plan use, then runs the user's own status line | socket |
| `irie team-tmux` | the `tmux` an agent with agent teams finds first on its PATH (§2.3) | Takes Claude Code's teammate panes to the team server, a tmux session each; sends every other tmux call where it would go without Irie | tmux (exec) |
| `irie browser` | started by Claude Code for a session with Irie's browser (§4.15) | An MCP server on stdio. Starts Mullvad Browser on the first call that needs a page, drives it over WebDriver BiDi, and closes it when idle or when the session ends | the agent (stdio), its browser (WebDriver BiDi on loopback) |
| `irie anon mcp` | started by Claude Code for an anonymous research session (§4.16) | An MCP server on stdio: search through Irie's SearXNG, and fetch and the exit's check through the session's compartment's exit | the agent (stdio), SearXNG (loopback), the exit (SOCKS5) |
| Tor, SearXNG | the core, under `irie runtime` in the windows `irie-tor` and `irie-searxng` of irie-agents, as compartments need them (§4.16) | Irie's own Tor client and metasearch engine, on 127.0.0.1 only. A restart of the core leaves them running | the core (Tor's SIGHUP), sessions' servers (loopback) |
| `irie runtime -- PROGRAM` | `irie-agents:irie-helper`, a window each, while their plugin is on (§4.17) | A helper of yours from a plugin's manifest, such as a listener for what you text yourself. The core starts it, starts it again when it exits, at most every 30 s, and stops it when its plugin goes off; an install leaves it running | whatever it is written to; its status file, which the core reads |
| `irie remote serve --supervise` | `irie-agents:irie-remote`, while `remote.on` ([PHONE.md](PHONE.md)) | The phone app's gateway: HTTPS on the local network, a client of the socket that relays a phone's ops and subscriptions, and passes the requests of the panes in `remote.panes` on to their apps on this machine (PHONE.md §2.2). The core starts it, and starts it again after an install or a change of its settings | the core (socket), phones (HTTPS), the panes' apps (HTTP on 127.0.0.1) |
| `irie runtime -- ollama serve` | `irie-agents:irie-ollama`, started on first need (§6.3) | Irie's own Ollama server, in a process group of its own, so its model runners stop with it | the agents (HTTP on 127.0.0.1) |
| `irie mirror` | subagent panes (M8) | Read-only live view of one subagent | socket, transcript |
| `irie ctl` | anywhere | Scripting and tests: `list`, `spawn`, `show`, `watch`, `pause`, and so on | socket |
| `irie placeholder` | the slot when nothing is shown | Shows the keys | none |

### 2.3 tmux layout and swap-in

| Object | Contents | Options |
|---|---|---|
| Server | `tmux -L irie -f $XDG_STATE_HOME/irie/tmux.conf`, generated by the core. The user's own tmux.conf is never read. The core runs every tmux command with `-u`: without a UTF-8 locale, as when Irie.app starts the server, tmux would print a tab, or any non-ASCII character of a name, as `_` | Socket name from `tmux.socket` |
| Session `irie`, window 0 | Pane 0: `irie ui --supervise`, `ui.sidebar_width` columns wide. Pane 1: the slot | `window-size latest` |
| Session `irie-agents` | Window `core`. One window per agent, each with a single pane running `irie host`. One window per subagent mirror (M8). Window `irie-ollama` for Irie's own Ollama server, once a local profile needs it (§6.3) | Agent windows use `window-size manual`, resized to the slot's size, and `remain-on-exit on` |
| Session `irie-view` | Only while the Mac app splits: a dead first pane, and the window of the session shown beside its teammates, linked from `irie-agents` and current (see Agent teams below) | That window takes `window-size latest`, so it follows the app's client of this session |
| Pane user options | `@irie_session`, `@irie_agent` (`main` or `sub:<id>`), `@irie_role` (`agent`, `mirror`, `placeholder` or `runtime`), `@irie_name`, `@irie_named`, `@irie_created`, `@irie_vendor_id` | These travel with the pane through swaps. A restarted core rebuilds its registry from them, and finds its Ollama server by `runtime`. `@irie_name` is the session's name, which `rename` and Claude Code's titles change (§4.2); `@irie_named` is `1` when the user chose it, else `0`. The agent's window keeps the name it was born with, since nothing reads it |

Generated tmux.conf, essentials:

| Setting | Reason |
|---|---|
| `status off`, `prefix None` | The sidebar is the status line, and there are no stray tmux commands |
| `bind -n C-] if -F '#{==:#{pane_index},0}' 'select-pane -t irie:0.1' 'select-pane -t irie:0.0'` | Focus toggle between sidebar and slot (`ui.focus_key`) |
| `mouse on` | Click to focus, wheel to scroll |
| `base-index 0`, `pane-base-index 0` | `irie:0.0` is always the sidebar and `irie:0.1` always the slot |
| `remain-on-exit on`, `history-limit 50000` | A dead agent keeps its exit status and last screen |
| `allow-passthrough on`, `focus-events on`, `extended-keys on`, `terminal-features 'xterm*:extkeys'` | What Claude Code's terminal docs ask for (Shift+Enter, progress) |
| `set-titles on`, `set-titles-string Irie` | Sets the Ghostty window title, which is used to find the window (§7.1) |
| `escape-time 10` | Esc reaches agents promptly |
| `set-hook -g window-resized "if -F -t irie:0.1 '#{window_zoomed_flag}' '' 'resize-pane -t irie:0.0 -x <width>'"` | Keeps the sidebar's width when a client resizes `irie:0`. The hook fires for every window, an agent's too, so it asks `irie:0` whether it is zoomed: resizing the sidebar would end the Mac app's zoom |

**Swap-in.** The slot holds either the placeholder P or one agent pane A. P also draws the conversation of the session you picked, so in the conversation view a shown session's own pane stays home and P stays in the slot (§4.7). While A is shown, A's own window holds P. To show B, the core sends one command list, so no client ever draws the in-between state:

```
swap-pane -d -s irie:0.1 -t <P>     # only if an agent A is shown: A goes home, P returns to the slot
swap-pane -d -s <B> -t irie:0.1     # B moves into the slot, P into B's window
select-pane -t irie:0.1             # only when the user asked for focus
```

On every tick the core checks these invariants and repairs any it finds broken:
- `irie:0` has exactly two panes, and pane 0 is the sidebar.
- The slot holds P or exactly one agent or mirror pane.
- Every agent pane is either in the slot or in its own window.
- The slot is zoomed exactly while the Mac app is open ([MAC.md](MAC.md)), and swaps keep the zoom.
- The sidebar is `ui.sidebar_width` wide, except while the slot is zoomed.
- While the Mac app splits a session, its pane is out of the slot, and `irie-view` holds its window and no other agent's, current and at `window-size latest`. With no split there is no `irie-view`.
- Every agent window but the split one has the slot's size, so a swap never resizes an agent: no SIGWINCH and no redraw jump. While the Mac app is open it says the size it shows each session's terminal at (the `sizes` op), narrower beside the session's own panes, and each agent window has its own session's size instead; the shown agent's way home keeps the slot's.
- `irie:0` follows its latest client (`window-size latest`), except for a moment after a swap: while the app is open, a swap first gives `irie:0` the size of the window coming in, as the placeholder fills the slot, so that agent comes in at its own size, and `irie:0` holds that size (`window-size manual`) for 1.5 s, until the app's terminal has taken it too.

The core never kills a pane while it is in the slot; it swaps the pane home first.

**Agent teams.** With `spawn.teammates = "panes"`, Claude Code sessions may start agent teams, whose teammates are Claude Code sessions of their own, each in a terminal pane (`teammateMode: "tmux"`). They run on the team server, `tmux -L irie-teams` with its own generated `tmux-teams.conf`, never on Irie's: a teammate's pane is not Irie's to place, and no agent of Irie's is resized for it.

| Object | Contents |
|---|---|
| Anchor | Per session, a tmux session named after it whose one pane is dead (`true` under `remain-on-exit on`). The agent starts with `TMUX` naming the team server and `TMUX_PANE` the anchor, so Claude Code takes the anchor for its own pane |
| Shim | `sessions/<id>/bin/tmux`, first on the agent's `PATH` (spec `path`), runs `irie team-tmux`. Claude Code's calls on the team server (`-S` its socket) go on, but `split-window` makes a new session `<id>-<n>`, detached, with the environment in `sessions/<id>/team.env`, and `list-panes` of the anchor's window lists the anchor and its teammates, as if they shared it. Claude Code's questions about its terminal (`display-message` on its pane, `show-options`) go there too. Any other call, such as the agent's own use of tmux, goes where it would without Irie: `TMUX` and `TMUX_PANE` are taken out. The shim is never Irie's own tmux: tmux starts a pane with the PATH of the client that asks, so an `irie up` from an agent's shell once handed the core that agent's PATH, and the core ran every tmux command through the shim, and through the shim of each session before it, five deep and about 50 ms a command. So no Irie process takes a shim for tmux (`tmux.IsShim`), `irie up` and a starting core leave the shims' dirs out of PATH, and a shim whose tmux is another shim runs the real one |
| Teammate | One tmux session each, which a view shows by attaching to it. Its environment is the agent's without `IRIE_SESSION`, `TMUX` and `TMUX_PANE`, and with `IRIE_TEAM_OF` naming the session. So the hooks and status line it inherits through `--settings` never speak for its session; they only hold its tool calls while the session is frozen (§4.3). tmux continues a pane's first process whenever it stops, and a teammate's pane has no host, so that hold is how a teammate pauses. The team server itself starts with only what tmux and a login need (`HOME`, `PATH`, `LANG` and the like) |
| Row | `team`: each teammate's session, name, colour and agent type (from its command line), title and whether it works (from Claude Code's spinner in it), and whether it is dead |
| End | The core never stops the team server, so a restart leaves every teammate running. Once a session is gone, its anchor and teammates' sessions go, which ends them; Claude Code itself kills its teammates' panes when it exits |

The Mac app shows a session that has teammates beside them in its terminal view, as cmux shows a team: the session's terminal on the left and its teammates' in a column on the right, one under another at equal heights, each a live terminal you click into and type to ([MAC.md](MAC.md)). The session's own terminal there is a second client of Irie's server, attached to `irie-view` (`show` with `split`), so only that agent takes the split's size. The slot meanwhile shows the session's conversation, and every other agent keeps the slot's size.

**The Dock.** A session's panes in the Mac app have a Dock card of controls, as cmux's Dock has (docs/proposals/right-dock.md §5.2); it was a tab of the right sidebar until that became the same for every session. A terminal control's command runs on the dock server, `tmux -L irie-dock` with its own generated `tmux-dock.conf`, never on Irie's, in a tmux session of its own: one copy per session for a control of scope `session` (the default), started in the session's worktree or folder; one per repo for `repo`, in the repo's own folder; one in all for `global`. It runs in the user's login shell (`$SHELL -lc`), in the core's environment plus the control's `env`. The app's Dock is a client of the copy, which takes its size, so no agent is resized; a copy that ends keeps its last screen (`remain-on-exit`). A copy carries the session it is for (`@irie_dock_session`) or its repo's key (`@irie_dock_repo`): it ends when its session closes, and the core's sweep, every 30 s, ends any whose session or repo is gone. The core never stops the dock server, so restarts leave the controls running.

### 2.4 Socket API

The socket is a unix stream socket at `<runtime>/irie/core.sock`, mode 0600, in a 0700 directory. The core has no TCP listener; the phone app reaches it through its own gateway, `irie remote serve`, a client of this socket that lets a phone use only a phone's ops ([PHONE.md](PHONE.md)). Messages are NDJSON, one object per line:
- Request: `{"id":1,"op":"spawn","args":{…}}`.
- Reply: `{"id":1,"ok":true,"result":{…}}` or `{"id":1,"ok":false,"error":{"code":"…","msg":"…"}}`.
- `subscribe` replies once, then streams frames until the client closes:
  - `{"t":"snapshot","sessions":[…],"header":{…}}`, every row, once, as the first frame
  - `{"t":"upsert","session":{…}}` and `{"t":"remove","id":"…"}`, one for each row that changed, however many changed at once: the removes first, then the upserts in the list's order
  - `{"t":"history","undo":"…","redo":"…"}`: the labels of what `undo` and `redo` would do next, empty when nothing (§4.5), after the first snapshot and whenever they change. A subscription to one session gets none.
  - `{"t":"header","header":{…}}` when the plan's use, the machine's memory (§6.3) or how busy it is changes. A snapshot carries the header too. `load` is how busy the machine is: its CPUs, the work waiting to run over the last minute (to a whole CPU), the kernel's memory pressure (1, 2 or 4) and the ptys in use (to 8) of the most there can be, read every 5 s. A core advertises it to the cores it is paired with ([multi-host](proposals/multi-host.md) §4.2). The header also says `red_on_done` when `ui.red_on_done` is set, so every sidebar draws done in red (§5.3), and, once the Mac app has named its palette (`theme`), `theme`: its name and its `light` and `dark` sets, each colour `#RRGGBB` by role, which the terminal sidebar, the slot and the phone app draw in from then on instead of their own.
  - `{"t":"event","event":{…}}`, sent only when `events:true` was requested.
  - `{"t":"outside","outside":[…],"problem":"…"}`: the Claude Code sessions outside Irie (§4.6), after the first snapshot and whenever they or the problem change. A subscription to one session gets none.
  - `{"t":"closed","closed":[…]}`: the closed sessions that `reopen` can bring back, the last closed first (§4.5), after the first snapshot and whenever they change. A subscription to one session gets none.
  - `{"t":"notepad","notepad":{…}}`: the notepad, as `notepad` answers (§4.20), after the first snapshot and whenever it changes. A subscription to one session gets none.
  - `{"t":"conversation","conversation":{…}}`, sent only when `conversation:true` was requested for one session: its whole conversation first (`reset`), then the items that changed and what it waits on you for (§4.7).
  - `{"t":"screen","screen":{…}}`, sent only when `screen:true` was requested for one session: what its terminal shows, `cols` by `rows`, with the `cursor` (null while hidden) and while it waits on you the `options` of its prompt, as `screen`'s. The first frame, and one after a change of size, has `reset` and every line as `capture-pane -e` writes it, SGR escapes and written trailing spaces kept, the escapes' state carrying from line to line; the next ones have `set`, each line that changed by its number `n` and its `text`. The core reads the terminal four times a second while someone watches, and at once after `keys` or `send`, and sends a frame only when it changed ([PHONE.md](PHONE.md)).
  - `{"t":"ping","ping":N}`: the freeze dog's ping, the only frame of a `view: "watch"` subscription (below).
- Frames to the UI are coalesced to at most 10 rounds a second: between two rounds the core only notes which rows changed, and a round carries each one's latest state once. A snapshot for several changes would send every row again; on a busy machine that came every few seconds ([optimisation](proposals/optimisation.md) §2.1).
- **The freeze dog, Raphael's watch (§4.11).** A window of Irie's own, the Mac app or a sidebar, asks to be watched with `subscribe`, `view: "watch"`, its `pid` and, for the Mac app, its `bundle`, on a connection of its own. The core pings it once a second, one ping at a time, and the window answers each on the same connection from its main loop, `{"pong":N,"front":…,"hidden":…}`: whether its app is the active one, and whether it is hidden. A ping left unanswered for `ui.freeze_dog` (10 s), by a sidebar for a minute at least, means the window no longer answers its user. The core then has `sample` write down where the process is stuck, in a report in `<state>/freezes/`, named for the time, the kind (`app` or `sidebar`, an `irie` process) and the pid (the newest ten are kept); force quits it with SIGKILL once the stacks are read, about 1.3 s later; waits for it to go; and, for a window with a bundle, opens the bundle again as it stood, with `/usr/bin/open`, `open -g` or `open -j` and the arguments `--freeze-dog <seconds>`, which the app says in a notice, and `--background` when it was not in front. When `open` fails, as it does with procNotFound (-600) while Launch Services still takes the old app for running, the dog tries again after 1, 2 and 4 s. A sidebar's supervisor starts a sidebar again by itself. Agents are untouched (§2.1, principle 6). The dog's clock waits while the core itself is held up, since a late look restarts every unanswered ping's time; a process under a debugger is left be; and the dog restarts windows of each kind three times in ten minutes at most, so an app that freezes as it opens is not opened again and again. With `arch.raphael.on` off it restarts nothing. The dog has a lock of its own, so a busy core never holds a ping or an answer up. `hello` lists `watch` when the core has the dog.
- The API version is `1`. Additions keep backwards compatibility.
- Each op is marked by where its effect goes, inside Irie or outside it (§4.5, Inside and outside).

| Op | Args | Result | From |
|---|---|---|---|
| `hello` | `client`, `api` | `version`, `api`, `features[]`, which name the ops served, `move` among them | M1 |
| `list` | none | `sessions[]`, `outside[]`, `outside_problem?` | M1 (`outside`: §4.6) |
| `subscribe` | `events?`, `session?`, `agent?`, `view?`, `conversation?`, `screen?`, `pid?`, `bundle?` | frames (above). `view: "app"` is the Mac app: the slot stays zoomed while it is subscribed. `view: "watch"`, with `pid` and `bundle`, asks the freeze dog to watch a window (above). `conversation` needs `session`; with `agent: "sub:<id>"` it follows that helper's own conversation (§4.4). `screen` needs `session`: its terminal, live, which `hello` names `screen-live` | M1 (`view`: Mac app; `conversation`: §4.7; `watch`: the freeze dog; `screen`: the phone app's Term view) |
| `spawn` | `task`, `cwd?`, `repo?`, `in_place?`, `profile?` (`auto` routes it), `name?` (the user's, §4.2), `force?`, `delegate?`, `parent?`, `permission_mode?`, `continues?`, `jot?` (typed in a task bar: it may add to the jot before), `apart?` (a session a jot was added to, to take it back from and start it apart), `tutor?` (`off`, `explain` or `teach`; else `learn.tutor`, and off for a delegated subtask) | `session`, which carries `tutor` while it is in a tutor mode; for a jot added to the one before, `added`, the session it went to and `until`, when it is typed; for `apart`, `late` when that agent had it already | M1 (`repo`, `profile` M3; `force`, `delegate`, `parent`, `permission_mode`, `continues` M10; `jot`, `apart`: "More for the last jot", §4.2; `tutor`: [learning.md](proposals/learning.md), with `spawn-tutor` in `hello`) |
| `input` | `text`, as typed; `session?`, the selected session; `outside?`, the selected session outside Irie; `repo?`, `in_place?`, `profile?`, `model?`, `tutor?`, the client's menus, which the words win over; `pastes[]?`, each `label` and `text`; `apart?`, a session a jot was added to, to start it apart instead; `note?`, the note of the notepad the text came from, which the core ticks off once the input went through (§4.20) | `did`: `spawned`, `added` (a jot that went to the agent of the jot before, §4.2), `answered`, `ran` or `asked`; `op`, the op it ran; `text`, to show as it is; `session?`; `show?`; `choice?`, as `undo`'s; `until?`, for `added`, when it is typed in. Reads what a person typed, a task, a status question or a `:` command, and runs the op it means through this table (§4.2, [CORE.md](CORE.md) §3) | irie-core |
| `show` | `session`, `agent?`, `focus` | none | M1 |
| `complete` | `text`, an `@` or `+` token | `items[]`; for `+`, `notes[]`: what each profile runs and costs (§6.3) | M3 (`notes` M10) |
| `hook` | `harness`, `session`, `payload` | `context?`, `deny?`. Waits while the agent is held | M2 |
| `status` | `session`, `payload`: Claude Code's status line JSON | `state`, `subagents[]`: the session's, for Irie's own status line; `color?`, the session's own colour while it works | M10 (§6.3) |
| `theme` | `theme`: one of `ui.theme`'s palettes, or the name of a palette of the user's own with `light` and `dark`, its sets as `#RRGGBB` by role, a set left out rosewater's ([MAC.md](MAC.md) §1) | none. The Mac app's palette, which `color` follows from then on instead of `ui.theme`, and which the header carries as `theme` (its name and both sets) to every subscriber: the terminal sidebar, the slot and the phone app draw in it. The core also writes its dark grounds as Irie's theme of Claude Code's (`themes/irie.json` in Claude Code's config folder), which a Claude Code session started from then on names in its settings (`"theme": "custom:irie"`) while the user's own theme is Claude Code's default | §6.3 |
| `local` | `start?` | the local server, the machine's memory, and each model weighed against it | M10 (§6.3) |
| `route` | `task`, `parent?` | where `+auto` would run it: class, choice, reason, every profile weighed | M10 (§6.4) |
| `rate` | `session`, `verdict`: `good` or `bad` | none | M10 (§6.4) |
| `bench` | `task?`, `profile?`, `repo?`, `host?`, `runs?`, `judge?`; with no task it lists | `bench`, the bench started; or `benches`, every one, the newest first | §6.6 |
| `result` | `session` | `text`: what its agent said at the end of its last turn | M10 (§6.4) |
| `tool` | `name`: `setup` or `models` | `session`, running `irie setup local` or `irie ctl local` | M10 (§6.4) |
| `rename` | `session`, `name` | `session`, with the name it got: whitespace and control characters made single spaces, cut at 200 bytes, numbered (`-2`) when another row has it. The name is then the user's, which Claude Code's titles leave be (§4.2). Refuses an empty name with `bad_args`. Records `ui.renamed` when the name changed | M4 |
| `park`, `unpark` | `session`; `before`, a session, for a drag across Parked's edge | none. Park puts the session last in the Parked group at the end of the list, and `unpark` last among the rest, just above Parked: both move it to the end of the order, or with `before` just before that session; each row says `parked`. One that is that way already changes nothing and moves nothing. When it changed, it records `ui.parked` or `ui.unparked` and a step of the history, which `undo` takes back, to the place it left (§4.5) | M4 (§4.1) |
| `pin`, `unpin` | `session`; `before`, a session, for a drag across Pinned's edge | none. Pin puts the session last in the Pinned group at the top of the list, and `unpin` first among the rest, just under Pinned, or with `before` just before that session; each row says `pinned`. Pinning a parked session unparks it, and parking a pinned one unpins it. One that is that way already changes nothing. When it changed, it records `ui.pinned` or `ui.unpinned` and a step of the history (§4.5) | §4.1 |
| `spaces` | `do`: none (list), `new` (`name?`), `rename` (`space`, `name`), `move` (`space`, `before?`) or `remove` (`space`) | `spaces[]`, each `id` and `name`, in their order; for `new`, `space`, the one made. Names are unique in any case; an empty one gets a name of its own, `Space 2`. Removing a space puts its sessions in the first; the last space stays. The header carries `spaces` too | §4.1 |
| `space` | `sessions[]`, `groups[]?` (ids, names or paths), `space` (`""` for the first) | `sessions[]`, those it moved: those named, and every session of a group named, its groups' too, pinned and parked ones included, so the group goes whole as the core has it then. Records `ui.spaced` for each, and one step of the history, which one undo takes back, named for a group sent alone (`move "Steak" to another space`) | §4.1 |
| `group` | `do`: none (list), `new` (`sessions[]`, `name?`), `add` (`sessions[]`, `group` or `name`, `before?` or `after?`), `remove` (`sessions[]`, `before?` or `after?`), `rename` (`group`, `name`) or `ungroup` (`group`) | `groups[]`, each `id` and `name`; for `new` and `add`, `group`, the one the sessions went in; `sessions[]`, those it moved. `new` makes a group of the sessions where the first of them is, with the others gathered just after it; `add` puts them last in the group, or where a drag dropped them, and by `name` makes the group when none has that name; `remove` puts each just after the rest of its group, or where it was dropped; `ungroup` removes the group and leaves its sessions where they are. Joining a group unpins and unparks a session. Names are unique in any case; an empty one gets a name of its own, `Group 2`. Each session that `new`, `add`, `remove` or `ungroup` moves records `ui.grouped`, and together they are one step of the history; a rename is not. Rows say `group`, and the header carries `groups` | §4.1 |
| `notepad` | `do`: none (list), `add` (`text`, `id?`, the client's own, `after?`), `edit` (`id`, `text`), `check` (`id`, `done`), `move` (`id`, `before?`), `delete` or `restore` (`id` or `ids[]`) | `notes[]` in their order and `deleted[]`, the last deleted first, each `id`, `text`, `done`, `created`, `changed`, `sent?`, `session?` and `deleted?`; `rev`, the file's changes; `path`; `problem?` and `read_only?` when the file had trouble; `note`, the note an add or an edit left. Every change is written before it answers, and each is the same however often it is sent: an add of an id the notepad has, or had, changes nothing; an edit of a deleted note brings it back, and of one it never had adds it. A note holds at most 64 KB and the notepad 5,000 notes, past which a change is refused, never cut short; while the file is read only every change is refused with `busy`. While the notepad is kept in the Obsidian vault too, `vault` names its note (`path`, `synced`, `problem`, §4.17) | §4.20 |
| `materialize` | `id`, `prompt`, `context?`, `inputs?` (the context's digest when left out), `every?`, `gap?`, `wait?`; or `id` and `forget`; or `session` (and `wait?`) for a session's own card, where it stands, which the core makes from its row and conversation while `materialize.sessions` is on | `id`, `state` (`cold`, `fresh`, `stale` or `refreshing`), `spec?` (the card, in the catalog of `internal/genui`; none while cold), `made?`, `by?` (the local model), `err?` (why the last remake failed), `began?`. The card a local model makes for `prompt` from `context`, kept in `cards.json` (§5.4): given at once from what is kept, and made again, one at a time and never on a cloud model, only when its inputs changed or it is as old as `every`, never sooner than `gap`; a new `prompt` leaves it cold. `wait` waits for a remake under way, up to 5 minutes (docs/proposals/materialized-cards.md) |
| `stop_sub` | `session`, and `agent` (a helper, `sub:<id>`) or `teammate` (its tmux session on the team server) | none. Ends one of a session's subs at your word: a helper's tool calls are turned away from then on, each with word to end at once, until it finishes; a teammate's terminal ends. Records `ui.sub_stopped` | §4.1 |
| `close`, `reopen` | `session`; for `close`, or `sessions[]`, several, and `finished`, every session whose work is through but the pinned, the parked and another machine's; for `reopen` it may be left out, which reopens the session closed last | `close`: `sessions[]`, those it closed, in the list's order; `reopen`: `session`. Sessions closed together are one step of the history, which one undo takes back (§4.5) | M4 (§4.5) |
| `undo`, `redo` | none; `confirm` to be asked first even when the step is certain (hello's `undo-confirm`); to answer a question, `choice_id` and `choice` (its option's key) | `label`, `did`: `undone`, `redone`, `settled`, `skipped`, `cancelled` or `asked`; when `asked`, `choice`: `id`, `question`, `options[]` (`key`, `label`, `detail`, `recommended?`), and `confirm` when it is the question `confirm` asked. Nothing to do is `not_found`; an answer to a question that no longer fits the history's head is `stale` (§4.5) | M4 |
| `pause`, `resume` | `session` or `all`; `scope`: `main`, `sub:<id>` or `tree` | none | M5 |
| `freeze`, `thaw` | `session` or `all` | `sessions[]`, those it froze or thawed; for a thaw of several, `queued[]`, those that thaw in turn after them, one every 30 s (§4.3) | M6 |
| `priority` | `session`, `level` (0 or 1), `reason?` | none. Level 1 also puts the session at the top of the list | M7 |
| `reorder` | `session`, `before?` | none. Puts the session just before `before`, or last. Not `move`, which brings a session in from outside Irie | §4.1 |
| `note` | `session`, `agent?`, `text` | `note` | M7 |
| `preflight` | `profile`, `model?`, `remember?`, `force?` | `ok`, `problems[]`, `warnings[]`, the context and memory the model got | M10 |
| `attach-info` | none | tmux binary, server socket and the session that holds the slot, for a terminal outside Irie | Mac app ([MAC.md](MAC.md)); per-session targets after v1 |
| `move` | `vendor_id`, `profile?` | `session`. Refuses with `busy` unless the session is listed outside Irie and idle. A local profile is checked as for a spawn (§6.1) before the outside copy ends | Sessions outside Irie (§4.6) |
| `dog` | none | `jots[]`: the jots that are not through, each with its `loose` (below), looked at afresh: the open ones in the list's order, then those that closed on their own (`spawn.close_when_done`) in the last day as `closed`; a jot you closed is done and never listed | The jot dog (§4.8) |
| `rebuilds` | none | `on` (`usage.rebuilds`), then `today` and `week`, the last seven days: each `from`, `count`, `tokens`, `usd`, and `sessions[]` and `causes[]` ranked by what their rebuilds cost, each `name`, `words`, `count`, `tokens` and `usd`, a session also `id` and `open` | The prompt-cache meter (§6.3) |
| `lock` | `session?`, `check?` | `sessions[]`, the open ones then the peers' rows, or the one named (an open session's id or name, or a closed one's id), each `id`, `name`, `host?`, `open`, `lock?` (the whole lock), `packages[]` and `none?` (why it has no lock); `packages[]`, each version the locks name with `checked`, `advisories[]` (`id`, `summary`, `fixed`, `modified`) and `sessions[]`; `checked`, `osv`, `asked`, `daily`. With `check` it first asks OSV.dev, and says why it could not with `internal` | Agent locks (§4.21) |
| `worktrees` | `drop?` | `trees[]`, each closed session's worktree that is there, those that can go first: `session`, `name`, `path`, `branch`, `commit`, `bytes`, and `keep`, why it stays, with `busy` when what keeps it is something that runs there; `can` and `bytes`, how many can go and the room they take. A dry run; with `drop` it removes each that can go, every check made again, and says `dropped`. A `drop` from inside an agent is refused | §4.5 |
| `learn` | `session?` | `resources[]`: the user's own links (`learn.links`), then with a session the tutorials of the languages its project is written in, read from the files at its top (`go.mod`, `Package.swift`, …), then the free resources Irie links to, each with its group, title, link, what it teaches and a note; `glossary[]`: the words Irie and its agents use, in plain words. Both come from `internal/learn/learn.toml` | The Help menu ([learning.md](proposals/learning.md)) |
| `config` | `set[]?`, each a `key`, dotted as in the file (`spawn.default_profile`), and a `value` of the type the key takes: a duration as a string such as `"30s"`, and null to take the key out, so its default applies | `path`, `config`: every key in effect, defaults too, by the file's names, and `problem?`: why the file would not load as it stands. With `set` it first edits the file in place and runs on the result (§5.5); what would not load is refused with `bad_args` and changes nothing | The Mac app's Settings window ([MAC.md](MAC.md)) |
| `flip` | none | `terminal`: flips the slot between the picked session's conversation and its raw terminal, and focuses the slot (§4.7) | Conversation view (§4.7) |
| `conversation` | `session`, `agent?`, `limit?` | `session`, `agent?`, `transcript?`, `items[]`, `needs?`, `pending[]`, `more?`: the session's conversation, read from its Claude Code transcript (§4.7), or with `agent: "sub:<id>"` that helper's, read from its own (§4.4) | Conversation view (§4.7) |
| `send` | `session`, `text` | none. Types the text to the agent as a person would; refuses with `busy` while it waits on you in its terminal, before it is ready, once it has ended, and a message of several lines while the agent takes no bracketed paste (§4.7) | Conversation view (§4.7) |
| `continue` | `session` | none. Types "continue" and Enter to the agent as `send` does, but only while its own prompt shows with nothing typed in it, and so after a turn that failed too; Enter waits until the prompt shows the word. Refuses with `busy` when the prompt has text or none shows, while its pane is scrolled back, and as `send` does (§4.7) | The Mac app's double ↓ ([MAC.md](MAC.md) §4) |
| `context` | `action`: `log`, `sync`, `resolve`, `tag`, `clean` or `where`; `session` (an open session's id or name, a closed one's id, or any branch or tag of the store), `at?` (a point: a turn `N`, `-N`, a tag, a commit or an entry's uuid), `name?` (the tag's), `limit?` | `repo`, the store's path; `session` and `branch`; `commit` and `cut?` the point resolves to; `turns[]`, newest first, each with its `commit`, `turn`, `prompt`, `said`, `session`, `leaf`, `model`, `code`, `dirty`, `partial`, `cut`; for `clean`, `removed[]`, the sessions whose contexts went, and `keep_days`. An open session's newest turns are stored first | Contexts in git (§4.13) |
| `fork` | `session` (open), `at?`, `task?`, `name?`, `profile?`, `model?`, `here?` | `session`, the fork; `from`, the commit it carries on; `turn` | Contexts in git (§4.13) |
| `screen` | `session` | `lines[]`: what its terminal shows now, as plain text, without the blank lines under the last; `options[]`, each `key`, `label` and `selected`: while it waits on you, the numbered choices of the prompt at the bottom, as Claude Code draws a permission, a question or a plan, and none otherwise | The phone app ([PHONE.md](PHONE.md)) |
| `keys` | `session`, `keys[]`, `text?` | none. Presses each key in its terminal, a letter or digit or one of Enter, Escape, Up, Down, Left, Right, Tab, BTab, Space, BSpace, PageUp, PageDown, Home and End, never Ctrl+C, then types `text`, with no control character, without Enter. Takes its turn with `send`; refuses with `busy` once the session has ended or while it is frozen | The phone app ([PHONE.md](PHONE.md)) |
| `pair` | `minutes?`, 10 by default, a week at most | `on`, `name`, `urls[]`, `code`, `until`: opens a six-digit code that pairs a phone with this machine, as `irie remote pair` does, and the setup page's addresses, the Bonjour name first. The gateway takes the code from the state folder; a phone can't open one | The Mac app ([PHONE.md](PHONE.md)) |
| `dock` | `session`; `start?`, `stop?` or `restart?`, a control's `key`; `ensure?`; `trust?`, a repo file's hash | `controls[]`, each `key` (`repo:<id>` or `mine:<id>`), `id`, `title`, `type` (`terminal` or `browser`), `url?`, `chrome`, `height?`, `source` (`repo`, `cmux` or `mine`), `scope`, `state` (`untrusted`, `stopped`, `running`, `exited` with `exit`, or `ready` for a page), `target?`, the copy's tmux session, `cwd`, `command`; `tmux` and `socket` to attach a client while the dock server runs; `files[]`, each `path`, `source`, `trusted`, `problem?`; `untrusted?`: the repo file that waits for the user's trust, its `path`, `hash` and `commands[]`; `mine`, where the user's own file goes. The repo's file is `.irie/dock.json`, or else cmux's `.cmux/dock.json`, in the session's folder or the nearest above it up to the repo's top, with cmux's fields and Irie's `scope`; the user's own is `dock.json` in Irie's config folder. A repo file runs nothing until `trust` names its hash, and a changed file is untrusted again; the user's own runs at once. `ensure` starts each trusted terminal control with no copy; a copy that ended stays until it is started again (§2.3) | Right sidebar ([MAC.md](MAC.md), docs/proposals/right-dock.md) |
| `peers` | `reload?` | `name`, this machine's, and `peers[]`, each `name`, `url`, `up`, `since`, `problem?`, `version?`, `sessions`, `load?`, `memory?`: the other machines' cores this one is paired with, as it last heard from each; `reload` first reads `peers.json` again, as `irie peer add` and `remove` ask (§2.8) | `irie peer` ([multi-host](proposals/multi-host.md)) |
| `perf` | none | `since`, `cpu_us`, `children_cpu_us`, `ops` (each op's count and time to answer: `n`, `p50_ns`, `p99_ns`, `max_ns` and the buckets they come from), `times` (`tick`, `tmux.snapshot`, `changes`, `sample`, `outside.run`), `counts` (`frame.<type>` for each frame sent, `status.unchanged`, `header.changed`, `outside.<why>`), `lock` (how often `c.mu` was taken and waited for, the waits and the holds), `status` (each session's status line updates) and `subscribers` by view, all since the core started. `irie ctl perf` prints them and `tools/perf` reports one window of them | The optimisation plan's yardstick ([optimisation](proposals/optimisation.md), iteration 1) |
| `variorum` | `action`: `status` (the default), `connect` with `setup`, as Variorum copies it, `forget`, `notes`, `read` with `note` or else `session`, or `show` with `note`, `block?` and `target?` (`note` or `takes`) | `state`: `ready`, `setup` (a setup is missing), `needs` (Variorum is missing, or a kept setup's program fails its checks) or `off` (the Variorum tab is off, by its own switch `variorum.tab` or with the writing plugin, or not in this build: the op is the tab's); `text`, where things stand and what fixes it, which after `connect` starts with the setup it kept; `sessions` and `tab`, which setups are kept; `installed`, a Variorum.app in an Applications folder, on every answer, as the Mac app shows the tab only when one of the three is set; `error?`, why the action failed, in the door's own words when it said; `notes[]` (`id`, `title`, `edited`), `[]` when none is shared; `note` (`id`, `title`, `blocks[]`, each `id`, `name`, `shown`, `takes[]` without the archived, `comments[]`, and `more`, `read`); `shown`: `shown` or `offered`. `read` with only `session` reads the note its agent named last in a call to Variorum's door, which the core sees in its hooks. `notes`, `read` and `show` call the door with the tab's setup, one at a time, only when asked; a process inside an agent gets only `status`. No answer ever holds a key. `hello` lists it only in a build with the writing plugin | The Variorum tab ([writers.md](proposals/writers.md)) |
| `obsidian` | none | `state` (`ready`, `needs` or `plugin_off`), `text`, `vault`, the vault's folder, `name`, its name, `opened`, whether Obsidian has opened it, and `notes`, how the notepad's note in it stands (`path`, `synced`, `problem`): it only reads; the vault is chosen with `config`'s `obsidian.vault` | The obsidian plugin (§4.17) |

### 2.5 Observation

| Rank | Source | Gives | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Host and tmux | Alive or exited, exit code, frozen, pane sizes | Process truth. "Exited" and "frozen" override everything else |
| 2 | Hooks (via the shim) | Prompt submitted, tool calls, needs you, turn ended, subagents | The authoritative semantic signal |
| 3 | Transcripts (JSONL) | Catch-up after core downtime; subagent content | Tailing only |
| 4 | Timers | Startup stall (`spawn.ready_timeout`), shown with a `capture-pane` tail | The only screen read |

A hook event older than the last host or tmux fact cannot revive an exited session.

Until M11 the core polls `list-panes -a -F …` once per second for pane ids, options, `pane_pid`, `pane_dead`, `pane_dead_status` and sizes. M11 adds a control-mode client (`tmux -u -C attach -t irie -f ignore-size,no-output`) for push events and keeps a 10 s reconcile as the fallback.

| `core/` | irie-core's contract for Go clients: the wire types and op names, generated from `internal/ipc`, the socket's place, a client, and the language a person types (`Read`, `Said`) ([CORE.md](CORE.md)) | irie-core |
| `internal/paths/` | XDG and runtime dirs, socket path | M1 |
| `internal/brand/`, `tools/brand.sh` | The product's names in one place, for a copy under another name; the script gives them to the build scripts and writes the Mac app's `Brand.swift` (§2.10) | §2.10 |
| `internal/ctxgit/` | The context store: git objects written in git's own format, transcripts read into turns, forks' transcripts (§4.13) | §4.13 |

| `internal/mcp/` | The MCP server over stdio that Irie's own servers share: the handshake, ping and tools (§4.15, §4.16) | after M12 |
| `internal/anon/` | Anonymous research (§4.16): compartments and their exits, Mullvad's relay list, the checks of an exit, Tor's config, SearXNG's install and settings, and the MCP server `irie anon mcp` | after M12 |

A full run took 83 s where `go test ./...` took 595 s, which `internal/core`'s tests, one after another, filled; the slowest test, 70 s, is now the floor. Without cargo, or with a flag only go test takes, it is `go test` | Tests |

| `internal/variorum/` | Variorum's door for the writing plugin: the two setups Irie keeps, told apart by their shape, in `<data>/variorum/door.json`; the checks on the program they name; one call over stdio for the Variorum tab; and `irie variorum door`, a writing session's own MCP server ([writers.md](proposals/writers.md)) | Plugins |
| `e2e/` | Opt-in tests against the real Claude Code | M11 |

### 2.7 Install and first run

Getting from nothing to a first finished task takes one line in a terminal, or on a Mac one line and Spotlight, and no knowledge of what Irie needs: Irie says what is missing, in plain words, and offers each fix, which runs only after a yes.

```
curl -fsSL https://irie-people.github.io/irie/install.sh | sh
```

| Part | Design |
|---|---|
| Downloads | `tools/release.sh` builds them on a Mac: `irie` for darwin and linux on arm64 and amd64 (no cgo, `-trimpath`), each as `irie-<os>-<arch>.tar.gz` with LICENSE; Irie.app for Apple silicon and Intel as `Irie-macos.zip` (`IRIE_MAC_UNIVERSAL=1 mac/build.sh`: the Intel slice draws its terminals with SwiftTerm, since GhosttyKit builds for the Mac's own processor only, and `lipo` joins the two), its version stamped from the tag, with a universal `irie` inside in `Contents/Helpers` (not `MacOS`, where on a disk that ignores case `irie` and the app's own `Irie` would be one file), signed ad hoc from the inside out; and `SHA256SUMS`. The names carry no version, so `releases/latest/download/<name>` finds the latest without an API call. The release workflow's `downloads` job runs it on macos-15 when release-please publishes a release, and attaches the files to it; run by hand with a tag, it builds them again. A release made with `GITHUB_TOKEN` starts no other workflow, so the job lives in release-please's own |
| The installer | `site/install.sh`, served as `https://irie-people.github.io/irie/install.sh`. POSIX sh, with everything in `main`, so a download cut short runs nothing. It picks the download from `uname` (a shell under Rosetta counts as arm64), checks its SHA-256 against `SHA256SUMS` before anything moves, puts `irie` in `$XDG_DATA_HOME/irie/bin`, where `tools/live.sh` keeps it too, with one rename, so running processes keep their file, and links `~/.local/bin/irie` to it (an older plain file there becomes `irie.old`). On macOS 14 or later, the app's least, it replaces `~/Applications/Irie.app`, or `/Applications/Irie.app` when that is where it is and can be written, registers it with Launch Services and Spotlight, and reopens it only when that copy was open. curl marks no download for quarantine, so Gatekeeper never stops the unsigned app. When a release has no download for the system it builds with `go install …@<tag>`, given Go. Last it runs `irie setup` on `/dev/tty`, since its own input is curl's, or `irie setup --check` with no terminal. `IRIE_VERSION`, `IRIE_NO_APP`, `IRIE_NO_SETUP` and `IRIE_DOWNLOADS` (a mirror, and the tests) change it |
| `irie setup` | `internal/ready` checks five needs, in the order a newcomer meets them: tmux, 3.2 or later (the first with `-N`); Claude Code, the claude profile's command, where a version older than `MinVersion` warns; signed in, from `claude auth status --json`, of which only `loggedIn` and `authMethod` are read, since the rest names the person; git, where on a Mac `xcode-select -p` comes first, because macOS's own `/usr/bin/git` opens Apple's installer when the Command Line Tools are missing; and, from a terminal only, `irie` on the PATH. Each is ok, missing, warn or unknown. tmux, Claude Code and the sign-in block agents; git blocks only tasks in a repo. Each problem carries a fix: a command, run after a yes (Homebrew, or Homebrew's own installer and then Homebrew on a Mac without it; apt, dnf, yum, pacman, zypper or apk; Claude Code's official installer; `claude auth login`; `xcode-select --install`), or a line added once to the shell's startup file, after `# Added by irie setup`. `irie setup` offers the fixes in order and checks again after each, so installing Claude Code brings the offer to sign in. `--check` and `--json` change nothing, and exit 1 while something blocks |
| The first `irie` | When no core answers, outside tmux, `irie` runs the check first. With something missing it shows the list and, in a terminal, offers the fixes of what blocks. Only a missing tmux stops it: an agent on another profile may need nothing more. The check runs once a server start and takes about 0.3 s |
| The server's PATH | A new server gets `~/.local/bin` at the end of its PATH when that folder exists and the PATH lacks it (`ready.PathEnv`). The installers of Irie and of Claude Code put their programs there, and a terminal finds them only once a startup file says so, which `irie setup` offers. A running server keeps its environment |
| From the app | Opened with no Irie running, the app runs `irie up --login-env` (§7.2), once a launch and only before it has ever reached a core, so a core that restarts is left to the supervisor. It asks the core's `ready` op, the same check in the server's environment, which is the agents', every 4 s while something blocks. A fix runs as the `ready` tool: a session running `irie setup`, whose terminal the app shows ([MAC.md](MAC.md)) |
| A first task | Both sidebars offer `+haiku Write a haiku about my city. Ask me which city first.` with no sessions. It makes the agent ask before it works, so a newcomer sees a session start, ask and finish in about a minute, on Claude's quickest model, in a scratch folder of its own. The Mac app follows it with a card of steps, the walkthrough of [learning.md](proposals/learning.md) §5.1 in brief |
| Windows | Irie runs inside WSL 2, Windows' own Linux, as on any Linux ([WINDOWS.md](WINDOWS.md)). `site/install.ps1`, run as `powershell -ExecutionPolicy ByPass -c "irm https://irie-people.github.io/irie/install.ps1 \| iex"`, downloads `irie-windows-<arch>.zip`, checks it against `SHA256SUMS`, puts Irie for Windows, `Irie.exe` (`cmd/windows-app`, `internal/winapp`), in `%LOCALAPPDATA%\irie` with an entry in the Start menu, and the wrapper `irie.exe` (`cmd/windows`, `internal/wsl`) in `%LOCALAPPDATA%\irie\bin` and on the user's PATH, runs `irie setup`, and opens the app. The app is Irie's window on Windows, as Irie.app is on a Mac: a page in WebView2 that reaches the core and the slot's terminal through one `wsl.exe` running `irie bridge`, whose standard input and output carry each stream (`internal/mux`, `internal/bridge`), and shows the setup's steps itself while WSL or Irie in it is missing. On Windows that installs WSL (an administrator's OK, then a restart), Ubuntu, and Irie inside it by `install.sh`, whose own `irie setup` brings tmux, Claude Code and the sign-in; then it offers `.wslconfig`'s `instanceIdleTimeout=-1`, so agents keep working once the last window closes, a Start menu shortcut with a Windows Terminal profile, and a start at sign-in (`irie up`). Every other command runs the Linux `irie` in the distribution through a login shell, Windows paths among its arguments made WSL's. It finds WSL by its files rather than by asking `wsl.exe`, which on a Windows without WSL may offer to install it |
| Not yet | A signed and notarized app, and so a direct download of it that opens without a warning; Ghostty in the Intel slice; a Homebrew tap; and a tmux of Irie's own for a Mac without Homebrew, which needs a terminfo of its own, as macOS's lacks `tmux-256color` |

---

### 2.8 Peers: Irie on more than one machine

A core can be paired with another machine's, which then is its peer ([multi-host](proposals/multi-host.md)). `irie peer add` pairs through that machine's phone gateway ([PHONE.md](PHONE.md)) as a phone does, with a pairing link that `irie remote pair --link` prints there, and keeps the token and the fingerprint of its Irie authority in `peers.json` (mode 0600), so the core trusts no other certificate for it. The core then follows the peer's sessions over HTTPS and shows them after its own, each as a row of its own: `host` names the machine, its id is the machine's name, a slash and that core's own id (`Asahi/s38…`), and its detail starts with where it runs (`on Asahi · running go test`), so every client shows it. The sidebars and `ctl list` draw each machine's rows in a group of its own, `On Asahi`, after Parked (§4.1), whose cards leave that start out, since the header says it; a parked one goes into Parked with the rest and keeps it. A client sends that id back as it is, and the core passes the call on: `send`, `close`, `rename`, `park`, `unpark`, `priority`, `freeze`, `thaw`, `rate`, `result`, `screen`, `keys` and `conversation`, and a subscription to that session. `reorder` refuses, since a peer's rows keep that machine's order. `show` picks the row and the slot draws its conversation, since its terminal is on its own machine. `^name` among the first line's words starts the task on that machine, which reads its own `@` and `+` words; `^` and a word that names no machine stay in the task as typed.

**Where a task goes.** Each core advertises its machine in its header: `load` and `memory`, and `machine`: whether Claude Code is signed in, the profiles, the free disk, and the user's own Claude Code hooks (`~/.claude/settings.json`), each as the SHA-256 of its event, matcher and command without the program's folder or the home folder, with any program they start that the machine lacks. A task goes to a peer only where those hooks are all there and all run, so it never runs without a hook it would have had here, such as a repo's guard: `^name` says so and starts nothing anywhere. With `place.mode = "auto"`, a task typed with no `^` word goes to a peer only while this machine is busy (fewer than `headroom_cpus` free, less than `headroom_gib` free, memory pressure, or fewer than 64 ptys), only to a peer where Claude Code is signed in, the profile exists, and the `@repo` is one `peers.<name>.repos` lists, and only to one with at least 2 more CPUs and 2 GiB more free than this machine; each task placed there counts as a CPU and a GiB against it for 2 minutes. A task that reads as more for the jot before it stays with that jot's agent, and one the peer refuses starts here. The result says why it went.

**Work comes back.** A task in a repo runs on the peer in its own checkout, which the owner keeps there, with its hooks and guard, under the same `@name` as here. Once its turn ends (done, idle, exited or failed), this core fetches its branch over ssh into the repo of that name here, as `refs/remotes/<peer>/<branch>`, so it never moves a branch of this machine's, and the row says `fetched as Asahi/irie/…`, or why not. The ssh name is the one `irie peer add --ssh` paired over, or `peers.<name>.ssh`; `irie peer fetch [SESSION]` fetches at once. A peer's frame can't stop this core: one that goes wrong ends that peer's stream, which marks it down until it is followed again.

The peer's sessions run on under its own core whatever happens to this one. When its stream ends, or brings nothing for 40 s (its gateway pings every 15 s), its rows stay with `host_down`, the time it went, and calls about them say `peer_down`. The core follows it again after 1, 2, 4 … 30 s. A phone hears none of the peers' rows: it pairs with each machine itself.

### 2.9 Responsive under load and chaos

Principle 7. What the user does goes first: the views answer a click or a key at once, and only the news from the core waits its turn. macOS lets a process slow another of its user's down and undo it, but not raise one above normal, and gives no way to keep memory for one. So Irie keeps its own work small, keeps its own processes running and out of the background, steps its other sessions back while the machine is busy (Gabriel, §4.11), and never lets one stuck part hold another.

| Threat | What Irie does | Test |
|---|---|---|
| Many agents at work, so news many times a second | The core sends rows at most 10 times a second (§2.4). The app gathers frames off the main thread and takes them in a batch at a time: at once after a quiet spell, then at most every 100 ms, 250 ms while the machine is busy (by the header's `load`, as Gabriel counts it) and 500 ms while another app is in front. A batch is drawn once, and a snapshot in it drops the rows before it. A conversation's updates go in the same way, and only what changed is set | Measured on the Mac app with `sample(1)` |
| The plan's use read differently by each session | Each session reads the account's use when its own requests come back, so a quiet one reports an older figure. The core keeps each window's figure from the window that resets last, the highest within it (§6.3), and the header no longer changes with every status update. What the agents take in memory is rounded up to 100 MB, as the free memory is rounded | `TestThePlanKeepsTheNewestWindow` |
| A tmux server that is stopped, swapped out or wedged | Every tmux command has a deadline (10 s; 30 s to start the server). The client is killed at it, which leaves the server as it was; the core answers from what it knows and tries again at its next tick | `TestACommandToAStoppedServerGivesUpAtItsDeadline` |
| A stray SIGSTOP, a debugger, a chaos monkey | Twice a second the freeze dog (§2.4), the core's one watchdog, continues the tmux server, the core's process group (its supervisor and what the core runs) and the sidebar's if any is stopped; that look takes no lock, starts no process and asks tmux nothing. It finds the server as the core's first forebear that runs tmux. A supervisor continues its own child at once, the core included, from `wait4` with `WUNTRACED`; it reads the stop from the status bits itself, as the host does, because Go's `WaitStatus.Stopped` takes a stop by SIGSTOP on macOS for a continue | `TestIrieKeepsItsOwnProcessesRunning`, `TestWaitRunningContinuesAStoppedChild` |
| A restart, a power cut or a tmux server that ends with agents in it, which takes every row with it | The next core records each session it ended as closed on its own, `lost`: history lists it, and reopen, or `irie ctl reopen --lost` for them all, brings it back as it was, its conversation carried on, pinned, parked or frozen again (§4.5) | `TestSessionsLostWithTheServerComeBackAsTheyWere` |
| A killed core or sidebar | The supervisor starts it again after 0.1 s, then 0.2, 0.4 … 5 s while it keeps dying; a run of a minute resets the wait. Agents are untouched (principle 6) | `TestIrieKeepsItsOwnProcessesRunning`, `TestCoreRestartGivesTheSameRows` |
| Irie's own processes in the background, as when a shell in the background state started the server and every pane inherited it | The freeze dog takes the server and both process groups out twice a second. macOS keeps the state another process set apart from the one a process set itself, and a process can clear only its own, so the core cannot take itself out: its supervisor does | `TestIrieTakesItsOwnProcessesOutOfTheBackground` |
| Another program takes the CPU or the memory | While the user is at the computer and it is busy, Gabriel puts every session out of their focus in the background, and with `arch.gabriel.outside` their heavy apps outside Irie (§4.11); their disk reads, paging included, are throttled too. Irie's own processes stay as they are | §4.11's tests |
| Irie.app's main thread or a sidebar's loop stuck or stopped | The freeze dog, Raphael's watch, force quits a window that leaves a ping unanswered for `ui.freeze_dog`, a sidebar for a minute at least, and opens the app again as it stood; a sidebar's supervisor starts it again (§2.4, §4.11) | `TestTheFreezeDogRestartsAWindowThatStopsAnswering`, `TestTheFreezeDogTriesAgainToOpenTheApp`, `TestRaphaelForceQuitsASidebarThatStopsAnswering` |
| Iries left running by tests and checks: a `go test` killed on a branch without testutil's reaper, or an Irie an agent started for a check and left, each with its core, sidebars, hosts and agents, for days, holding memory and ptys | Once a minute the core stops such an Irie's tmux server with all it runs: a test run's (`irie-test-*`) once no Go test binary that started before it still runs; one an agent of this core started (its server's environment holds the session's `IRIE_SESSION` and this core's `IRIE_SOCKET`) once that session is closed; either once it has run a day. Its team and dock servers go with it. The server gets SIGTERM, which hangs up its panes, and what of its tree still runs 6 s later gets SIGKILL; `core.log` names each. The core's own servers, the user's, and an agent's that run no Irie are left be, and a test core does none of it (`IRIE_LEFTOVERS=off`) | `TestLeftoverWhyStopsOnlyIriesWhoseRunIsOver`, `TestALeftoverTestServerStopsWithWhatItRuns` |
| The core's own loop stuck: a lock held for ever, a call that never returns | Every 5 s the freeze dog looks at when the core's loop last went round, by the monotonic clock, which stops while the computer sleeps, so a sleep never counts. Once it has stood still for `arch.raphael.core` (2 min), the core writes where every goroutine is in `<state>/freezes/` and leaves, and its supervisor starts it again; that look takes no lock. A late look starts the loop's time again, and a core restarted three times in half an hour is left be (§4.11) | `TestRaphaelRestartsACoreWhoseLoopStandsStill`, `TestTheCoresBeatSkipsTheComputersSleep` |

**Not done, and why.** Raising Irie's own priority: a process without an app role cannot raise a thread above the default priority, whatever quality of service it asks for (checked on macOS 14.1: a command-line process stays at 31). `task_policy_set` with `TASK_FOREGROUND_APPLICATION` lifts only the main thread to 47, and the Go runtime runs its work on any thread, so it would buy little. Keeping memory for Irie: macOS has no way to set memory aside for a process, and wiring the core's pages would take them from everything else. Restarting Irie.app when it quits or dies: the user owns the app, and quits it on purpose too. A window that is stopped or hung stops answering, which is the freeze dog's (§2.4).

### 2.10 Another name: copies under their own brand

A company may run its own copy of Irie under its own name. `internal/brand` keeps the product's names in one place, and everything else takes them from there, so a copy changes that one file. Its constants:

| Constant | Irie's | What takes it |
|---|---|---|
| `Name` | `Irie` | Everything a person reads: messages, the help, the sidebars, the Mac app's windows, menus and Info.plist, the phone app, the skills and the arch agents' words, the tmux window title |
| `Slug` | `irie` | Everything a computer looks up: the command, the folder in each XDG directory, the runtime folder and its socket, the tmux server and its sessions and windows, the default branch prefix, a repo's `.irie/dock.json`, the skills' namespace (`/irie:…`), the MCP servers' names, the pairing link's scheme |
| `EnvPrefix` | `IRIE` | The environment variables agents and scripts see: `IRIE_SESSION`, `IRIE_SOCKET`, `IRIE_BIN` and the rest (`brand.Env`). Tests' hooks, such as `IRIE_FAKE_MEMORY`, keep their names |
| `BundleID` | `io.github.irie-hq.irie` | The Mac app's identifier, the core's look for it, and the lid's launchd job |
| `Site`, `Repo` | the guide and the source | The Help menu, the install command the app shows, the skills' author, the push service's contact |
| `Tagline` | `Everything irie.` | The line under the name on the Mac app's welcome |
| `Bar` | `cheer` | `ui.bar`'s default: what the strip under the terminal shows, phrases of encouragement (`cheer`) or tips for those new to it (`tips`), one at a time; a company's copy may want tips |
| `Tags` | none | The go build tags `tools/live.sh` and `tools/release.sh` build with: `noprivacy,nowriting,nomarkets,nomail,noterms,noextension,noyoink,nojammin` leaves those plugins' code out, `novpn` the VPN's and `nobitwarden` the Bitwarden plugin's (§4.17); by hand, `go build -tags "$(tools/brand.sh --tags)" ./cmd/irie` |
| `VPNs` | none: all | The VPNs a copy drives (§4.18), comma-separated, the first `vpn.provider`'s default: a company names the VPN it approves, such as `cisco`, `globalprotect` or `forticlient`, and its people can choose no other. A copy that names any shows its VPN from the start (`vpn.on`) |
| `VPNServer` | none | `vpn.server`'s default: the host, portal or VPN by name that the company's VPN connects to, so its people set nothing |

Made from them: `App` (`Irie.app`) and `Exe`, the Mac app's executable and so its process's name, `Name`'s ASCII letters and digits, 15 at most, as macOS keeps only a process name's first bytes and `pgrep -x` matches only those.

**Renaming a copy.** Change the constants, run `tools/brand.sh --swift >mac/Sources/Irie/Brand.swift` and build. Go code uses the constants; the scripts that build and install (`mac/build.sh`, `tools/live.sh`, `tools/hot.sh`, `tools/release.sh`, `mac/snapshot.sh`) read them through `tools/brand.sh`; the Mac app has them in `Brand.swift`, which `mac/build.sh` also brings up to date. Texts that ship in the binary, the skills, the arch agents' words, the tips and the phone app's pages, keep Irie's names in the source and are rewritten as they load (`brand.Text`). Since the names are constants, a message made from them is a constant too, and `go vet` still checks its format. Two copies under different names run side by side on one computer: no folder, socket, server or variable of one is the other's.

**What stays Irie's.** Data whose form must not change between copies: the context store's git trailers (`Irie-Turn`, §4.13; a trailer's key can't hold a space), journal values (an event's source, a closed session's harness), tmux's own `@irie_*` options inside the private server, the Go module's path and the Mac app's source folder. None of them is shown as the name.

**A long name.** `Name` may have up to 30 letters, digits, spaces, hyphens and full stops (`brand.MaxName`), which every place that shows it fits: the Mac app's title bar shrinks it a little and then trails it off with the whole name on hover, the theme maker's preview likewise, a tight button leaves it out (Move In for Move into Irie), the terminal sidebar's title bar gives it half the width at most so the counts keep theirs, the slot's placeholder cuts it to the pane, the phone app's title shrinks it and trails it off, and tmux.conf quotes it. Only those characters, since the name goes as it is into shell scripts, tmux.conf, XML and HTML.

**What a copy changes by hand.** The icon (`tools/mkicon` draws Irie's), `site/` and `docs/`, `site/install.sh`, the iOS app (`ios/`), the arch agents' charges and the default preferences (`spawn.preferences`), and the tags of a build it runs by hand rather than through the scripts.

**Tests.** `internal/brand`: the names' form, `tools/brand.sh` agrees with the package, `Brand.swift` is current, `Exe` for long and non-Latin names, and two guards that fail on any string outside the package, in Go or in the Mac app, that names the product itself rather than through it. `internal/config`: `ui.bar`.

### 2.11 The design library

How Irie looks and behaves is kept in one place for all six clients (the Mac and iPhone apps, the phone's web app, the Windows app, the terminal sidebar and the site): Roots, Irie People's design library, named for roots reggae, a repository of its own: [irie-people/roots](https://github.com/irie-people/roots). It holds the four palettes, one file each in the form of a theme file of one's own (§5.5, `ui.theme`), and `tokens.json`: the palettes' order and default, the contrast floors, the terminal's colours and the numbers of the agents' colours (§4.1). Irie takes it as a Go module, `github.com/irie-people/roots`, and the Go clients read it: the sidebar's palettes, the status line's agent colours and `ui.theme`'s names.
