  - *Memory:* the kernel's memory pressure and the swap in use, with what takes the most, by program (Irie's sessions together), and any program that takes a fifth of the RAM.
  - *The disk:* nearly full under 10% or 15 GB free, getting full under twice that.
  - *Caches:* the biggest over 500 MB. Go's build cache, which `go clean -cache` clears once no Go build or test runs. Homebrew's, which `brew cleanup` clears of what `brew cleanup -n` lists; the dry run can take minutes, on a busy Mac more than an hour, so only a look the user asked for has it, for ten minutes at most. The cache of an app that is not open, which goes to the Trash. Caches of tools he can't tell are idle, such as Yarn's and pip's, get advice only; the system's, Irie's own, test browsers' and a folder whose app he can't tell are left be.
  - *Startup:* each launchd job in `~/Library/LaunchAgents`, `/Library/LaunchAgents` and `/Library/LaunchDaemons`, the user's crontab, login hooks and old startup items. A job's program is weighed by `codesign -dv` and `--verify`, and, when it is part of an app, by Gatekeeper's `spctl -a -t exec` of the app, which says whether it is notarized; a program outside an app is judged by its signature alone, since only asking Apple would tell, which he does not. Then come the signs public write-ups of Mac malware name (MITRE ATT&CK T1543.001, T1543.004, T1053.003, T1037.002): no signature or a broken one, a program in a temporary, shared, downloads or hidden folder, code written into its plist or fetched from elsewhere, Apple's name on code Apple did not sign, a file anyone can change, a change this week. Good programs show some of these too, so the signs add up: three are worth a look, five look suspicious. Each comes with its evidence and how sure he is, never certain. A job whose program is gone is a leftover; an empty plist does nothing. The user marks one as theirs (It's mine, `arch.raphael.known`), and he no longer flags it.
  - *Protection:* System Integrity Protection, Gatekeeper, XProtect's rules and their age, whether macOS installs its security updates by itself, the updates waiting, FileVault and the firewall. These are advice only: turning one on is the user's to do, in System Settings, often with an administrator's password.
- *Fixing:* he fixes a thing only on the user's OK, one at a time, and shows first what he will run: `go clean -cache`; `brew cleanup`; an app's cache moved to the Trash, so it can be put back; `kill -TERM` of a program of the user's own that runs hot; `launchctl bootout` and `launchctl disable` of a launch agent of their own, whose file stays, with how to turn it back on. Right before, he checks again: the process's start time, that the folder is still a cache and its app still closed, that Go is idle (he waits up to 30 minutes for it). He never deletes the user's own files, never runs what needs an administrator, and never takes an agent's word for the user's: op `raphael` refuses a fix or a mark from inside an agent's process tree, and a fix without `"ok": true`, which a client adds only once the person says OK.
- *Asking him:* `irie ctl raphael` prints his report, numbered, with each fix's command; `look` has him look now; `fix N` shows what N runs and asks at the terminal (`--yes` once read); `mine N` marks a startup item as the user's; `on` and `off` turn him on or off. His row in the arch op says when he looked and how many things need care, offers Look over my Mac now, and has an item per finding, with its fix, which asks first, and It's mine. What Irie and its agents leave behind, such as old worktrees and the servers of tests cut short, is Azrael's to clear.

*Not yet:* watching the slot and the placeholder as the sidebar is; a core whose loop goes round but that answers no socket; login items that open apps (System Settings > General > Login Items, which only an administrator's `sfltool dumpbtm` lists), configuration profiles, browser extensions and shell startup files; on Linux, systemd units and the system's own protections (there he looks at processes, memory, the disk and Go's cache only); telling the user of a finding at once, rather than in his report; a session that runs as him, to look closer at one finding. For the owner to decide: whether Yarn's and pip's caches should get a fix too, and whether an app's cache should be deleted outright rather than moved to the Trash, which frees nothing until the Trash is emptied.

**Tests:** Raphael's parsers of ps, plists, codesign, spctl, lsappinfo, crontabs and Homebrew; the sampler, which finds a process hot for ten minutes and starts afresh for a pid used again or across a gap; what each finding advises and which offer a fix; the signs of a startup item, on a Mac's folders under a test's root; each fix with its tools faked, its checks right before (Go busy, an app opened meanwhile, a pid used again, a folder outside the caches); the op, which runs a fix only with OK, never from an agent and one at a time, and keeps what it did; his arch row; the core watch, which restarts a core whose loop stood still, not one still starting or held up, not across a sleep, and not a fourth time in half an hour; the sidebar force quit and not opened, with its own restarts; the app opened again when `open` fails at first; nothing restarted with him off.

**Every arch agent reports through the core.** An arch agent is three things: his file in `internal/arch/agents` (name, `nickname`, `icon`, rank, `title`, description, and `call: no` when nobody calls on him), his work in the core, and his row in the core's arch table (internal/core/archop.go), which says what he does now, what a person can do with him and his settings, by config key and kind. The op `arch` (`irie ctl arch`) gives all of it, with each setting's value, to every client, which draws the arch agents as they come and knows none of them by name ([CORE.md](CORE.md) §2): an action is an op called with its args, a setting is a key of the `config` op. The Mac app's Arch Agents tab is drawn that way, so a new arch agent shows in the Mac app, the sidebar or the phone with no change to them. An arch agent may also list `items`, what he found or holds, a line each with its level (`ok`, `note` or `warn`), his advice and its own actions; and an action with `ask` is one the person must say OK to first: a client shows `ask`, such as the commands a fix runs, and calls the op only on their OK, with `"ok": true` added to its args, which the op otherwise refuses, so a client that does not know `ask` never runs one unasked. Their icons are emoji, which every client can draw: Mikael ⚔️🛡️, Gabriel 🪽, and the rest with nicknames as they come: Raffi (Raphael) ❤️‍🩹, Uri (Uriel) 📚, Ragu (Raguel) 🤝, Sari (Sariel) 🦉, Remi (Remiel) 🌱, Raz (Raziel) 🔑, Azra (Azrael) ♻️, Izzy (Israfil) 🔔 and Barry (Barachiel) 🥩.

**What a session took.** The core asks for the arch agents of each Claude Code session it starts or resumes; the adapter gives what its CLI can take and writes that to `arch.json` in the session's directory (who is with it, whom it runs as, the user's good, whether they are on call and keep the door), which the core reads to say the word. A session that took nothing has no file, and hears nothing.

**Not yet.** The cards and the conversation do not yet show a call Mikael stopped: a PreToolUse hook's denial fires no further hook, so Irie would read it from the transcript. Other harnesses get neither word nor door. The next arch agents, with their own work, follow as the user names them.

**Tests:** the arch agents read from their files, and broken files are refused; Mikael's word with and without the door, his agent and the user's good; `--agents` with his charge and the good, and `--agent` for a session that runs as him or a spawn that fails; the door's prompt hooks by rule and tool, and none on an old Claude Code or with the door off; with the fake agent, which takes `--agents` and `--agent` and checks prompt hooks as Claude Code does: the word at SessionStart and SubagentStart (not told to call on him), none to a session that runs as him, and nothing with Mikael off.

### 4.12 The lid

macOS sleeps a MacBook the moment its lid closes, and every agent at work stops with it: the API calls in flight fail, and nothing goes on until the lid opens. A power assertion, such as Claude Code's own `caffeinate -i` or the trod bot's, keeps only idle sleep away. An assertion that keeps a closed lid awake takes an entitlement Apple keeps for its own tools (`AppliesOnLidClose` in powerd), and the kernel's own lid switch (`kPMSetClamshellSleepState`) is one powerd sets back each time its lid state changes, as at a power source change, which sleeps a Mac whose lid is closed at once. What holds is the Mac's sleep turned off altogether: `pmset -a disablesleep 1`, the `SleepDisabled` system setting, which only root may change and which lasts across restarts.

**Setting it up.** `irie setup lid` asks for the user's password once, through sudo in a terminal or macOS's own password dialog, and installs `/etc/sudoers.d/irie-lid`: a rule that lets the user's account run `/usr/bin/pmset -a disablesleep 1` and `… 0` as root without a password, and nothing else. It writes the rule beside its place under a name sudo skips (`irie-lid.new`), has `visudo -c` check it, and only then moves it into place, so a broken rule never reaches sudo. It also installs a launchd agent, `io.github.irie-hq.irie.lid`, that runs `irie lid check` at login and every two minutes. `irie setup lid --remove` turns sleep back on if Irie had it off, and removes both.

**Awake while there is work.** Every 5 s the core looks at its sessions (`lid.awake`, default `work`). There is work while a session works, starts or is stalled at work, or waits on background work of its own that will wake it or on its helpers or teammates still at work (§5.3), or while the trod bot keeps the Mac awake. Parked and frozen sessions don't count, nor sessions whose harness reports no turns. While there is work, the core runs `sudo -n /usr/bin/pmset -a disablesleep 1`. Once there has been none for `lid.grace` (2 minutes, for work that comes straight back, such as a jot the dog sends on), it runs `… 0`. If the lid is closed by then and no other display is there (`system_profiler SPDisplaysDataType`), it sleeps the Mac with `pmset sleepnow`, as closing the lid would have. The core starts no hold in a dark wake, when a sleeping Mac is up for a moment on its own (`pmset -g systemstate` lists no Graphics then): its sessions still look as they did when it slept, and a hold would keep it from sleeping again. With `always`, the Mac stays awake for as long as the core runs, for the phone app; with `off`, the core leaves sleep alone.

**The battery and the heat.** With sleep off, macOS no longer sleeps the Mac at a low battery or when it runs hot: the kernel refuses every sleep while `SleepDisabled` is set. So the core does it. On battery under `lid.battery` percent (10, by `pmset -g batt`), or from heavy thermal pressure (level 2 of `com.apple.system.thermalpressurelevel` by `notifyutil -g`, as `<libkern/OSThermalNotification.h>` numbers them), it turns sleep back on and, with the lid closed, sleeps the Mac.

**The display.** With sleep off, closing the lid no longer turns the built-in display off either, so it would stay lit under the lid, spending the battery and adding heat. While it keeps sleep off, the core reads the lid every 15 s (ioreg, about 50 ms a read), and when the lid has closed since the last read, with no other display there, it puts the display to sleep with `pmset displaysleepnow`, which needs no root. It does so once a close: a display woken while the lid stays shut is left awake, and opening the lid wakes it as usual. A display beside the built-in one means the Mac is in use at a desk, so the core then leaves the displays alone. The keyboard's light has no public switch, and the core leaves it.

**Never stuck off.** The core keeps sleep off only while it runs. It writes what it did to the state dir's `lid.json` and, while sleep is off, touches the file at every look. As it stops, it turns sleep back on without sleeping the Mac: a core that restarts holds it again within seconds, and the kernel sleeps a Mac whose lid is already closed only at its next reason to. A core that crashed leaves the file saying sleep is off, and the next core takes the hold on and lets it go once the work is through. If no core comes back, the launchd agent's check finds the file untouched for 3 minutes, turns sleep back on and sleeps a Mac whose lid is closed. That also covers a restart of the Mac, after which `SleepDisabled` would still be set. Sleep that something else turned off, such as the user with pmset, is theirs: the core neither takes it nor turns it back on. Sleep that something else turned back on while the core kept it off is turned off again at the next look. Until `irie setup lid` has run, the core tries nothing, and logs once what to do; the look after it holds at once. `irie lid` says how it stands: the setting, whether it is set up, what the core last did and why, and the lid, the battery and the heat.

**Not yet.** The sidebar and the Mac app don't show that the Mac stays awake with its lid closed, and the app's Settings don't offer `[lid]`. It is for macOS only; on Linux, `systemd-inhibit --what=handle-lid-switch` could do the same without root.

**Tests:** the readers against what macOS prints (`pmset -g`, `pmset -g batt` with a battery and without, `pmset -g systemstate` in a full and a dark wake, `notifyutil`, ioreg's lid in and out of its tree, system_profiler with and without a display beside the built-in one); the choice by mode, work, battery and heat; the rule, its script run against a temp dir, and the launchd agent; the check for a fresh and a stale hold, and `irie lid`. With stand-ins for sudo, pmset, notifyutil, ioreg and system_profiler: a core that holds sleep off while its session works and lets go once it is through, sleeps a Mac whose lid is closed, puts the display to sleep when the lid closes during a hold (once a close, again after it opens and closes, also under a lid already closed as the hold starts, and never with another display there), waits out the grace and background work, lets go at a low battery and at heavy heat, leaves alone sleep that is not Irie's, does nothing without the rule, with `lid.awake = off` or in a dark wake, holds with `always`, lets go as it stops and holds no more after, takes on a hold the last core left, and holds again once something else turned sleep back on. Test cores never touch the Mac's sleep (`IRIE_LID=off`, internal/testutil).

### 4.13 Contexts in git

An agent's context, its conversation, is the most valuable state Irie holds, and the least handled: a transcript in Claude Code's folder that its retention sweep deletes after 30 days, which can be resumed or branched whole and nothing else. Irie keeps every session's context in git, the tool its users already trust with versioned work, so that what git does for code it does for contexts: log, show, diff, tag, branch, merge-base, bundle. It is what forking a context at any turn stands on, and after it racing agents from one point and comparing what each did (proposals/agent-os.md).

**The store.** `$XDG_STATE_HOME/irie/context.git`, a bare repository of Irie's own, 0700, never inside a user's repo, so that no push of theirs ever carries a conversation away. Git runs on it with the user's global and system config left out (no hooks path, identity or pager of theirs), and every commit and reflog line is Irie's (`irie <irie@irie.invalid>`). The core is its one writer; anyone may read it, as `irie ctl ctx git log --graph --all` does. A closed session's context stays `context.keep_days` days after its newest turn, 30 by default as Claude Code keeps its own transcripts, and 0 keeps every context for good; then the core lets it go, with its rewound lines and their reflog entries, and git rewrites the packs without what nothing holds any more, so it is gone from the disk. An open session's context stays however old it is, and a tag keeps the turns it names. The core looks two minutes after it starts and every hour after, and `irie ctl ctx clean` looks at once and leaves git to free the room in the background. At each look git also packs the objects syncs wrote loose, once there are a few hundred (`gc.auto` 256, not git's 6,700, since a turn's objects run to tens of kilobytes), which halves the room they take: a week of 72 sessions, 229 MB loose, packs to 126 MB. Its gc runs beside the syncs and reads, on one thread, with an hour to finish rather than the 30 s every other git command gets; it deletes only what nothing has held for an hour, and a sync freshens an object it finds already there, as git does, so nothing a sync is about to hold is pruned.

| Thing | In the store |
|---|---|
| A session | A branch, `refs/heads/<session id>` |
| A turn: a prompt and everything up to the next | A commit dated at the turn's last entry. Its subject is the prompt, its body the prompt whole when it is longer and the turn's last reply quoted, then git trailers: `Irie-Session`, `Irie-Name`, `Irie-Conversation` (Claude Code's id), `Irie-Turn`, `Irie-Leaf` (the uuid of the turn's last entry), `Irie-Model`, `Irie-Code` (the commit the session's checkout was at), `Irie-Dirty` (its files not committed), `Irie-Partial` (the turn was still going), `Irie-Cut` |
| The conversation so far | The commit's tree: `turns/NNNN.jsonl`, each turn's transcript lines as Claude Code wrote them, and `turns/NNNN.md`, the turn as a person reads it: the prompt, each reply, and a line per tool call with its command or path. `git log -p` and `git diff` of two contexts read as conversations |
| A fork | A branch that starts at the commit it was forked from, so the fork and its source share their history and `git merge-base` finds where they parted |
| A `/rewind`: a prompt that goes on from an older entry | A commit on that older turn's commit. The branch's head before it stays under `refs/irie/rewound/<id>/<ms>` |
| A `/clear` | The next commit's tree holds only the new conversation, its turns from 1, on the branch's head |
| A name for a point | A lightweight tag |

**From transcript to commits.** A transcript is read on from where its last read stopped. Its entries link by `uuid` and `parentUuid`, so it is a tree: the newest entry that can end the conversation's line (a user, assistant, system or attachment entry that is not a subagent's) is the leaf, and its ancestors, through `logicalParentUuid` across a compaction, are the line the conversation is on. A turn starts at each prompt: a message from the user that is not a tool's result, not one of Claude Code's own notes, and not a local command's output. The newest commit whose `Irie-Leaf` is on the line is the base, and each turn after it becomes a commit. Entries without a uuid, such as titles and file checkpoints, are not the conversation's and are not stored. Claude Code writes a turn's bookkeeping, such as what its Stop hooks said and how long it took, after the Stop hook that stores the turn; entries like those alone, with no new message, make no commit of their own but go into the next turn's commit, or a last one once the session has ended, so a turn is one commit. A notice that background work finished starts a turn like a prompt, and its subject is its summary (`Background: …`). Lines that are not understood are skipped: the format is Claude Code's own (§4.7).
- **When.** At each turn's end (Stop, StopFailure), at the agent's end and when the session closes, in the background and a session at a time, off the hook's path, from what the session was as the event came, so a session that closes itself as its turn ends keeps that turn, once its transcript has gone a second without growing (30 s at most), since Claude Code writes a turn's last reply and bookkeeping only after the turn's Stop hooks have run; and at once before any `context` op or fork reads an open session. A closed session's context still reads by its id. A turn still going when a read needs it is stored with `Irie-Partial`, and again whole once it ends. A turn stored again, grown since or with its last bookkeeping, is committed in its old commit's place, with the code that commit recorded unless it is the newest turn, and the reflog keeps the old one, so a turn is one commit. (Stores from before 2026-11-08 have a second commit for about half their turns, the first without its last reply; they go as `context.keep_days` lets them.)
- **Cost, measured** on a 300-turn session of 9.8 MB and 7,800 lines, on a 12-core Mac at a load of 25: the first sync 0.65 s; each turn after it about 21 ms, most of it one `git update-ref`; a fork's transcript 0.1 s. Irie writes the objects itself, in git's loose format (SHA-1, zlib), rather than run git once an object, and git packs them at the core's hourly look (above). In memory it keeps about 200 bytes for each transcript entry of the sessions it follows, at most 200,000 entries: past that, the transcripts read longest ago are let go and read again from the top at their session's next turn end.

**Fork** (`irie ctl fork [--at AT] [--here] [--name NAME] [--model MODEL] [--wait] [+PROFILE] SESSION [TASK...]`, op `fork`). Only a Claude Code conversation forks.
1. **The point.** The session's newest turns are stored, then AT resolves: none, the newest turn; `N`, turn N; `-N`, the turn N before the newest; a tag; a commit; or an entry's uuid, which the conversation view's items carry. An entry that does not end its turn cuts the turn there, with a commit of its own on the turn's (`Irie-Cut`).
2. **The place.** A new worktree of the session's repo at the code its turn recorded: `Irie-Code`, else that of the nearest turn back that has one, else the checkout's HEAD. From the newest turn the session's work not yet committed comes along: changes to tracked files as a patch, and untracked files that are not ignored, up to 10 MiB each. From an older turn, what was not committed then is not there, and the fork says so in its warnings. With `--here`, or for a session outside git, the fork works in the session's own folder and shares its files.
3. **The transcript.** The commit's turn files, with each line's `sessionId` the fork's new id, as the Agent SDK's `forkSession()` makes its copy, and every mention of the session's folder that stands whole made the fork's folder, so its agent reads and edits its own copy. It goes where Claude Code finds a session: `<config dir>/projects/<the fork's folder, every character but letters and digits made ->/<id>.jsonl`. File checkpoints, `/rewind`'s snapshots of code, are not carried, as `forkSession()` carries none.
4. **The spawn.** `claude <profile flags> --settings <file> --resume <id> [TASK]`: TASK is the conversation's next prompt, after a line that says what it is a fork of and where it works now; with no task the fork waits for you, as a reopened session does. It runs on its source's profile and model unless `+PROFILE` or `--model` say otherwise. Its row is named `<source> · fork`, its branch starts at the commit it carries on, and `spawn.requested` says `kind: fork` with `fork_of`, `fork_at` and `fork_turn`.

**Reading** (op `context`; each reads an open session's newest turns first):

| Command | What it does |
|---|---|
| `irie ctl ctx log [--json] [-n N] SESSION[@AT]` | The turns, newest first: commit, turn, when, from which session for a fork's earlier turns, and the prompt |
| `irie ctl ctx show [--raw] SESSION[@AT]` | One turn's `.md`, or with `--raw` its transcript lines |
| `irie ctl ctx diff A[@AT] B[@AT]` | Where two contexts parted, the turns each took since, and the diff of their turns' `.md` files |
| `irie ctl ctx tag SESSION[@AT] NAME` | Names a point; the name then serves as AT, and as a fork's start |
| `irie ctl ctx sync SESSION` | Stores the session's newest turns now |
| `irie ctl ctx git ARGS...` | Runs git on the store, with no core needed: `log --graph --all`, `bundle create`, anything |
| `irie ctl ctx clean` | Lets go at once of the contexts past `context.keep_days`, says which went, and frees their room in the background |

**Not yet.** Subagents' own transcripts are not stored. A turn's code is its checkout's commit, not the work it had not committed, which `git stash create` could keep. The Mac app has no fork button yet: the conversation view's items carry the uuids a "fork from here" needs. Merging what one context learnt into another, and races from one point, follow in proposals/agent-os.md.

**Tests:** git reads the objects Irie writes (`git fsck --strict`), and a blob's id is git's; a turn a commit, its subject the prompt and its trailers read back by git; nothing new, no commit; a restarted store goes on from its branch; a turn still going and then whole; a turn's bookkeeping in the next turn's commit, and in a last one once the session ends; old contexts let go, but not an open session's or a tagged turn, and gone from the disk; a `/rewind` goes on from the older turn and keeps the old head; a `/clear` starts its own turns; points by turn, `-N`, tag, uuid and commit; a cut in the middle of a turn; a fork's transcript, its session ids and its paths, and a fork's branch that parts from its source at the turn it forked; paths replaced only whole; prompts told from tool results, notes and command output. With the fake agent: a session that closes itself as its turn ends keeps that turn; a session's turns stored with the code each recorded, tagged, and forked from turn one into a worktree at turn one's code, with its transcript where Claude Code looks, its branch at turn one, and its agent resumed with the task as its prompt. Benchmarks: `go test -bench . ./internal/ctxgit/`.

### 4.14 Agents as processes: ps and races

Irie's agents are processes, and the CLI shows and races them the way a shell shows and times processes (proposals/agent-os.md). Both are clients of the socket API: they read the rows the core already derives, and spawn or fork through its ops.

**`irie ctl ps [--json] [--watch]`.** A line per agent, after a line that sums them up (how many, working, waiting on you, their cost and memory): its id; its state as a letter, R working, S done or idle, W waits on you (needs you or stalled), H paused or pausing, T frozen, I starting, Z exited, X failed; its priority; its model; how full its context is; its tokens, cost and memory; its age and name. `--json` gives the same for scripts, and `--watch` draws it again each second.

**`irie ctl race [--from SESSION[@AT]] [--check CMD] [--timeout D] [--json] +PROFILE... [@REPO] TASK...`.** One task on several profiles, a session and a worktree each: spawned new in `@REPO`, or forked from a point of a session's context (§4.13), so every racer starts from the same conversation and the same code. Each racer is named `race <id> +<profile>`. A racer's turn is over when it waits on you, its agent ends, its row goes (a session that closes itself when done), or, once it has been seen at work, it is done: a fork that resumes is idle for a moment before its prompt. Then CMD runs in its folder, in a process group of its own that 15 minutes end, and exit 0 passes. The racers are ranked: a turn that ended done first, then a passed check, then the fastest, then the cheapest. The table says each one's time, tokens, output tokens, cost (from its status line, §6.3) and check, and the race is kept as `$XDG_STATE_HOME/irie/races/<id>.json` and `<id>.md`, a page that can be published as it is. A racer is a jot like any other, so the jot dog watches its work; the race does not close or land any of them.

**Not yet.** Racers are not yet exempt from the jot dog, races are not stored in the context store, `ps` shows no priority below 0 because there is none yet, and neither has a place in the apps.

**Tests:** `cmd/irie/race_test.go`: the ranking, the kept page, a racer's end only once seen at work, and ps's letters and counts.

### 4.15 The browser

Agents read the web through Claude Code's WebFetch, which fetches a page's HTML and reads it without running it. A page that builds itself with JavaScript, as most pricing pages and single-page apps do, reads as nearly empty; a page behind a click or a form can't be reached; and an agent on a local model has no WebSearch at all. Irie's browser gives a Claude Code session a real browser for those: Mullvad Browser by default, driven by Irie's own MCP server, `irie browser`. The owner asked for it for deep research on 2026-10-31; [extensions.md](proposals/extensions.md) §5 has the research behind it.

**How it is driven.** Mullvad Browser is the Tor Project's Firefox ESR without the Tor network. Its build keeps Firefox's Remote Agent: with `--remote-debugging-port 0` it opens WebDriver BiDi on a free loopback port, which it prints on stderr and writes into its profile as `WebDriverBiDiServer.json`. `irie browser` speaks BiDi to it through a WebSocket client of its own (RFC 6455): no Node, no driver, nothing fetched at run time. The browser runs unmodified, with `privacy.resistFingerprinting` locked on, private browsing always on, and its own uBlock Origin and NoScript, which the profiles geckodriver and Puppeteer make leave out. The profile's `user.js` turns `remote.prefs.recommended` off, so the Remote Agent's 120 or so testing prefs, tracking protection and the popup blocker off among them, never apply. With `browser.proxy` it also sends the browser's traffic, DNS lookups too, through that SOCKS5 proxy: Mullvad's in the tunnel, `10.64.0.1:1080`, or another relay's, so that research leaves from another address than the rest of the computer's traffic.

**What the agent gets.** Claude Code lists the server as `irie-browser`, with instructions that say when to use it, and ten tools: `open` (an http or https page, in the tab used last or a new one), `search` (`browser.search`, by default DuckDuckGo's page for browsers without JavaScript), `read` (on through a long page, 10,000 characters at a time, its main part first), `links` (numbered, one per address, for `click`), `click` (by number, words or CSS selector), `type` (into a field, with submit for Enter), `screenshot`, `back`, `eval` (a script's result as JSON; a list of objects with the same keys and plain values comes back in TOON's tabular form instead, `[count]{key,...}:` and a row per object, which names each key once and costs about 40% fewer tokens than the indented JSON, by TOON's own benchmark) and `tabs` (at most 6: a new one closes the one used longest ago). Each result says which tab and page it is about, and how much of the text is left, and stays under the 50,000 characters past which Claude Code saves a tool's result to a file. Agents of one session share the browser; those that browse at once each take a tab of their own.

**A browser's life.** The server starts with the session and does nothing until a call needs a page. Then it starts the browser, headless, on a fresh profile in the session's folder, `sessions/<id>/browser/`. On this M3 Pro that took 4 to 12 s, and 29 s at the first launch after install, while macOS checks the new app. A copy of a clean template profile, `<data>/browser/template`, starts in about 6 s where an empty profile takes 11; `irie setup browser` makes it, and a browser that closes idle makes it again when Mullvad Browser was updated, so no call waits for one. A call waits at most 5 minutes for a start. The browser is the agent's own child, so it freezes and thaws with its session (§4.3) and steps back with it when Gabriel holds the session back (§4.11); on a busy machine a held-back session gets so little that a first start may not finish, and the call says so. After `browser.idle` (10 minutes) without a call the browser closes and its profile goes with it; the next call starts it again and says the earlier tabs are gone. When the session ends, Claude Code closes the server's stdin, and the server closes the browser (`browser.close`, then SIGTERM, then SIGKILL) and removes its profile. A browser that a killed server left running is stopped by the next server on that profile, by the pid it wrote there, and only if that process's command line names the profile.

**Handing it to a session.** The Claude Code adapter writes a session's MCP servers to `sessions/<id>/mcp.json` and passes it with `--mcp-config` (§3.3), beside the user's own servers, which still load. Claude Code first asks a stdio server for MCP's revision 2026-07-28 with `server/discover`; `irie browser` answers method not found, and the handshake of revision 2025-11-25 follows, which both of Claude Code's MCP runtimes speak.

**Turning it on.** `browser.connector` is off by default. An automated Mullvad Browser tells every page so (`navigator.webdriver` is true, as WebDriver requires), so it no longer looks like every other Mullvad Browser, and it holds no logins (extensions.md §5.3). `irie setup browser` installs Mullvad Browser with Homebrew's cask after a yes (elsewhere it points to Mullvad's downloads), makes the template, opens a page as an agent would, and turns the connector on. A session spawned while the connector is on and no browser is found starts without it, and its row says why. `browser.path` takes any other Firefox build instead.

**What it is not.** It is not the user's browser: no logins, no history, nothing kept. A task that needs the user's accounts wants Claude in Chrome. It never hides that it is automated, so a site that refuses automation refuses it too. While it runs, any program of the user's could drive it as well: Firefox's Remote Agent has no authentication, listens on loopback only and refuses requests that carry an `Origin`, so web pages can't, and the profile holds nothing worth taking. Page text is untrusted, and the server's instructions say so.

**Not yet.** The Mac app's Settings don't offer `[browser]`, and a card shows browsing only as the session's tool calls. Other harnesses don't get it, though `irie browser` is a plain stdio server that any MCP client can run. Downloads, uploads and a page's own dialogs are not handled.

**Tests:** `internal/browser`: the WebSocket client against a server of the test's own (RFC 6455's example key, masking, fragments with a control frame between them, pings, a 64-bit length, close), the BiDi client against a fake Remote Agent (replies by id, events passed over, an error reply, a timeout that keeps the connection, a call after close), the MCP server, now `internal/mcp`'s, which Irie's anonymous research server shares (discover, the handshake's versions, ping, an unknown tool, a parse error, a notification, tool calls, a tool's panic), finding the browser, the proxy, `user.js`, copying a profile with its times and without a running browser's files, the template's mark, the Remote Agent's address, a browser that exits as it starts, and the server's arguments. With `IRIE_BROWSER_E2E=1` a test drives the real browser through `open`, `links`, `eval` and `screenshot`, and checks its profile is gone after. Also the adapter's `--mcp-config` and `[browser]`'s defaults and checks.

### 4.16 Anonymous research

The owner asked on 2026-11-09 for agents that research wrongdoing without leading back to the user: Mullvad's relays as a pool of exits, a private search through SearXNG, Tor, and whatever else helps. A task typed with `+anon` runs as anonymous research, in a **compartment**: one line of inquiry, with an exit of its own that nothing else of the user's uses. `+anon:acme` runs it in the compartment named acme, which other tasks of the same inquiry share; `+anon` alone makes one named after the task. `+tor` and `+tor:name` do the same on Tor. Either goes beside a `+profile` or a tutor word, as `+sonnet +anon:acme …` (`core.ParseTask`).

**Whom it hides from.** The subjects of the research: the sites and services they run, which see each request, and anyone who later holds both their logs and another trace of the user. Two lines of inquiry never meet at one address, and neither meets the user's own traffic, which leaves through the Mac's Mullvad tunnel. Not hidden from: Mullvad, which sees its exits' traffic (most of it TLS), as it already sees the Mac's; the maker of the agent's model, to which the conversation goes under the user's account, which is why no address or relay of a compartment is ever written where an agent reads it; and an adversary who watches both ends of the network at once.

**One exit per compartment, kept.** A Mullvad compartment takes a relay's SOCKS5 proxy, `<relay>-wg-socks5-<nnn>.relays.mullvad.net:1080` (Mullvad's "SOCKS5 proxy" guide). The relays come from the list the Mullvad daemon keeps on disk (`/Library/Caches/mullvad-vpn/relays.json` on macOS, `/var/cache/mullvad-vpn/relays.json` on Linux), so picking one asks nothing of the network; on 2026-11-09 all 557 of its WireGuard relays had a proxy under that name, and 111 active ones run on servers Mullvad owns. Irie picks one with `crypto/rand` among the active relays: on Mullvad's own servers (`anon.owned`), in `anon.countries` when it names some, never the relay the Mac's tunnel leaves from, never one another compartment holds. The compartment keeps it for its life. Irie never moves a compartment to another exit because a site blocked it or slowed it: a site that sees one visitor come from many addresses links them by their timing and content anyway, and getting round a site's limits is not what this is for. It takes another relay only when Mullvad has taken its own out of service, and says so on the card. When the Mac's own tunnel has come to leave from a compartment's relay, as it may after the user picks another location, the compartment's tasks don't start (`anon.ErrMacOnExit`) until the tunnel leaves from another: the research and the user's own traffic would share an address. A Tor compartment takes a SocksPort of its own on 127.0.0.1, from `anon.tor_port` up; Tor never puts streams that came in on different ports on one circuit (tor(1), `SocksPort`), so two Tor compartments never share an exit at once. Compartments live in `<data>/anon/compartments.json` (0600), written through `filetx`, so the core and the user's own commands take turns.

**Fails closed.** A Mullvad relay's proxy answers only inside Mullvad's tunnel, so a program sent through it loses the network when the tunnel drops, rather than leave from the Mac's own address. An anonymous task starts only while Mullvad VPN is connected (`mullvad status --json`), and a reopened, forked or failed-over one only in its own compartment with its shut paths, or not at all.

**Search.** `irie setup anon` installs SearXNG (AGPL-3.0-or-later), the metasearch engine, from its own repository at a commit pinned in `internal/anon` (it has no releases), into a Python of its own made by uv, or by python3 3.10 or later: `<data>/anon/searxng/`. The core runs it under `irie runtime` in a window of its own in irie-agents, `irie-searxng`, so a restart of the core leaves it running, on 127.0.0.1:`anon.search_port`. Its settings, which Irie writes at each start over SearXNG's own defaults, turn on JSON results, turn off metrics, autocomplete and the limiter, proxy images, and send every engine's request through the exit of the compartment `search`, which is SearXNG's alone (`anon.search_route`; on Tor with `using_tor_proxy`, which also lets its onion engines answer): the engines see that exit, never the Mac's or a research compartment's. One exit, not SearXNG's round robin over several proxies, which exists to get round engines' limits. The user's own tools can search there too, such as deer-flow's SearXNG `web_search` tool with `base_url = http://127.0.0.1:8888`.

**Tor.** Homebrew's `tor` (`irie setup anon` offers `brew install tor`), run by the core under `irie runtime` in the window `irie-tor` with a config it writes, `<data>/anon/tor/torrc`: a client only, `AvoidDiskWrites`, `SafeLogging`, a SocksPort per Tor compartment, and a pid file, by which a new compartment's port opens with SIGHUP, which keeps every circuit. It starts with the first Tor compartment's task, and Irie waits for its ports to take connections before a check or a fetch goes through them: Tor opens them a moment after it starts or reloads, and a request before then is refused. Tor runs over the Mac's Mullvad tunnel: its guard sees a Mullvad address, and the network the Mac is on sees no Tor.

**What an anonymous session gets.**
- **`irie-anon`**, an MCP server (`irie anon mcp`, on the server internal/mcp shares with the browser) with three tools. `search` asks Irie's SearXNG and gives numbered results with the engines that answered and those that didn't. `fetch` reads an http or https address, or a `.onion` one on Tor, through the compartment's exit, without running the page's scripts (`internal/htmltext`, a tokenizer of Irie's own that runs in time linear in the page): its title, its text with its main part first, then its links, 10,000 characters a call, `offset` reading on from the page fetched last rather than fetching it again; `save` keeps the response exactly as it came in `evidence/` of the session's folder, beside a JSON file with its address, where redirects led, its status and type, its UTC time and its SHA-256. The session's folder is often a git work tree, so `evidence/` holds a `.gitignore` of `*`: research never goes into a commit by `git add -A`, nor is lost to `git clean -fd`. It refuses the Mac's own and private addresses, which an exit can't reach anyway; a name that leads there, or a redirect, still goes through the exit, which refuses it. Its errors don't name the exit, though Go's name the proxy by its name or its address in the tunnel. `exit` checks where the compartment leaves from, through the exit itself: Mullvad's check service must name the compartment's own relay and not the one the Mac's tunnel leaves from (the Tor Project's, for Tor), and the answer names no address. When the Mac's own exit doesn't answer its half of the check, the two can't be told apart, and the check fails. The server also holds the session's forwarder (below), as long as the session lives.
- **Irie's browser** (§4.15) through the same exit, by the forwarder's SOCKS5 port, when Mullvad Browser is installed, whatever `browser.connector` says (`anon.browser`); its profile names no exit. Behind any proxy its `user.js` also turns WebRTC off, whose connections would not take the proxy and would tell a page the Mac's own exit; turns off a direct connection when the proxy fails; turns off DNS in the browser, which `irie browser` makes possible by looking the proxy's own name up as it starts it; and sends localhost to the proxy too, so a page can't reach the Mac's own services.
- **No other way out.** `--strict-mcp-config`, so only those two servers load, none of the user's own, and `permissions.deny` for WebFetch, which fetches from the Mac's own exit, and WebSearch, which searches under the user's account. A Claude Code whose `--help` lacks either flag, or a profile that passes its own `--mcp-config`, fails the launch rather than start without them, as does a profile on any harness but Claude Code, which can shut none of these paths. No agent teams. A failover carries on, and a reopen or a fork starts, in the same compartment. Its shell can't reach Irie's ctl (below), so it neither delegates nor spawns, which `ctl spawn` would do outside the compartment, and it isn't told to delegate.
- **Its shell, in a sandbox.** Every command the agent runs, and all it starts, runs in Claude Code's own sandbox (its "Configure the sandboxed Bash tool" docs): Seatbelt on macOS, bubblewrap on Linux. Irie's `--settings` turn it on with `failIfUnavailable`, so a session whose sandbox can't start doesn't start, and `allowUnsandboxedCommands` false, so no command is retried outside it. That makes the sandbox admin-required, so a repository's settings can't loosen it; the user's own settings and the managed ones still could, so the launch is refused while either sets a list that would, or managed settings a switch (`internal/harness/claude/sandbox.go`). The sandbox's only way out is two ports on 127.0.0.1, `network.socksProxyPort` and `network.httpProxyPort`: the session's **forwarder**, which `irie anon mcp` holds for as long as the session lives, from port 9250 up, so a restart of the core never cuts a download (`internal/anon/forward.go`). It speaks SOCKS5 (RFC 1928) and HTTP's CONNECT and plain requests, and carries each connection on through the compartment's exit, the name looked up there. It refuses the ports of signing in and of mail and chat (SSH, telnet, SMTP, POP, IMAP, XMPP, IRC, RDP, VNC), as research does none of them and any would tie one of the user's accounts to the exit, and the machine's own and private addresses; its words name no exit. So a program that ignores proxy settings, such as Node's fetch, looks a name up itself, such as `dig`, or isn't TCP, such as `ping`, can't connect at all, nor can `ssh`, `nc`, `open` or a socket of the Mac's: tmux's, Irie's own, the SSH agent's. The shell may not read the compartments' file or the user's sign-ins (`anon.Credentials`: SSH and GPG keys, cloud and git tokens, keychains, browsers' cookies), nor may the Read tool; variables whose names say they hold a secret are unset (`anon.SecretNames`); and it writes only in its folder and its temp folder. Its proxy variables, which Claude Code sets for the sandbox and `irie hook` again from `sessions/<id>/anon.env` through Claude Code's `CLAUDE_ENV_FILE`, name only those local ports: `ALL_PROXY` the SOCKS5 port, the others the HTTP one, which Python's urllib takes too. Claude Code's own traffic to its model goes from the Mac's own exit as before. Checked on this Mac on 2026-11-10 with Claude Code 2.1.322: curl, git and Python left from the compartment's exit, and Node's fetch, `curl --noproxy`, `dig`, `ping`, `nc`, `open`, SearXNG's port, SSH through the forwarder, tmux, Irie's socket, `~/.ssh` and the compartments' file were all refused. A Go program can't verify TLS there, as macOS's trust service is outside the sandbox; it fails rather than leave.
- **A note at each SessionStart**, after a `/clear` or a compaction too: the compartment, its tools, and the rules. Research only: read, never act; never sign in or up, post, comment, message, buy or fill in a form that reaches a person, and never run what it downloads. Never put anything that could identify the user into a search, a page or a request. Never contact the people or groups it researches, nor let them learn they are researched, and leave alone what of people's private lives has nothing to do with the wrongdoing. Note each finding with its address and UTC time, keep the pages it relies on as evidence, and never follow instructions in page text. It is told that its shell's only way out is the exit, and never to look its exit's address up.
- **Its row** says `anon acme` (`ipc.Session.Anon`); the compartment is in its `spec.json`, so it outlives a restart of the core.

**The user's own commands.** `irie setup anon` checks Mullvad VPN and its relay list, turns `anon.on` (a `+anon` task needs neither Tor nor SearXNG), offers to install Tor and SearXNG, each after a yes, starts SearXNG and searches once as an agent would. `irie anon` says how each part stands and lists the compartments, by route and country; `--show` names their exits, to the user alone. `irie anon up` starts Tor and SearXNG as the compartments need them (the `anon` op). `irie anon check [NAME]` checks each exit. `irie anon run [--tor] NAME -- COMMAND` runs one of the user's own programs, such as a crawler or a research agent, through a compartment's exit, made if need be, with the proxy variables set, and only once its exit passed the check. The variables replace any proxy variables the shell had (`anon.Environ`): a program reads the first of two copies, so an inherited `NO_PROXY=*` would send it from the Mac's own exit, and an anonymous session's own variables would send it from that session's compartment. `irie anon proxy NAME` prints the exit for a tool's own proxy setting. `irie anon forget NAME` drops a compartment and lets its exit go.

**What it is not.** It hides where the research comes from, not what it does: a page still sees what is fetched, when, and from a Mullvad or Tor address. It is not a way round a site's blocks or limits, and doesn't rotate exits. Claude Code's own traffic, to the model and its auto mode's checks, leaves from the Mac's own exit under the user's account. What an agent types into a page is up to the agent, which the note and the user's arch agents guard. A page can tell that Irie's browser is driven by a program: `navigator.webdriver` is true, as WebDriver has it. The browser also looks the same in every compartment, so a site that sees two compartments' browsers could guess that one hand is behind them. Irie doesn't hide the automation, as that would be getting round sites' checks.

**Not yet.** One SearXNG serves every compartment, so the engines see one exit for all of them. The Mac app shows the compartment only through the row's words; its Settings don't offer `[anon]`. A compartment can't yet leave through a Mullvad account of its own, which would keep even Mullvad from tying it to the Mac's tunnel (the owner's plan of 2026-10-26: a second account in a gluetun container). Other harnesses don't get it. On Linux, Claude Code's sandbox shuts Unix sockets only with its optional seccomp filter (`npm install -g @anthropic-ai/sandbox-runtime`), so without it a session's shell could reach tmux and Irie's ctl there. An agent can still learn its exit's address by asking a site, which the note forbids.

**Tests:** `internal/anon`: the relay list, picking at random within countries and owned servers and never a held relay, compartments that each keep an exit of their own and take another only when Mullvad drops theirs, Tor ports, names, the store's mode, the exports' quoting, Tor's config and its reload, SearXNG's settings and install steps, the server's arguments, checks of a Mullvad and a Tor exit through a SOCKS5 proxy of the test's own (the Mac's own exit refused, no address in any answer), and the tools: SearXNG's JSON read, a page fetched through the proxy with its name looked up there, kept as evidence in a folder git ignores and read on from an offset without another request, the addresses refused, errors that don't name the exit, and the proxy variables a shell had dropped for `irie anon run`; the forwarder by SOCKS5, CONNECT and plain HTTP through a SOCKS5 exit of the test's own, the ports and addresses it refuses, and errors that never name the exit; a compartment the Mac's tunnel has come to leave from, and a Mac whose own check doesn't answer. `internal/harness`: the sandbox in an anonymous session's settings, kept on a resume, and the launch refused by an old Claude Code, by user or managed settings that would loosen it, and on the generic harness. `internal/htmltext`: 31 pages, hostile inputs in linear time, and a fuzz test. `internal/mcp`: the handshake and tool calls. Also `+anon` and `+tor` (`core`), the adapter's `--strict-mcp-config` and `permissions.deny` and its refusals, the hook's `CLAUDE_ENV_FILE`, `[anon]`'s defaults and checks, the browser's prefs behind a proxy and the proxy's lookup, and the core's compartment names, note and shut paths. Checked on this Mac on 2026-11-09: a compartment made by `irie anon run` exited from its own Mullvad relay and not the Mac's; `irie anon mcp` fetched and kept a page through it and checked its exit; Mullvad Browser with these prefs loaded a page through it, which Mullvad's check reported as SOCKS through WireGuard, with `typeof RTCPeerConnection` undefined in the page; and Claude Code 2.1.319 started with `--strict-mcp-config` and the deny rules listed `irie-anon` as its only MCP server, none of the user's own or their claude.ai connectors, and no WebFetch or WebSearch among its tools. An anonymous session checked it all again on 2026-11-10, with these results:
- `exit`, `search`, and `fetch` with evidence, `offset`, `raw` and its refusals worked.
- A name that resolves to 127.0.0.1 was refused at the exit, not sent to the Mac.
- curl, git and Go left through the exit, and Python failed rather than go direct.
- The browser went through a SOCKS relay of the compartment's city, with WebRTC off and a UTC zone. Its pages couldn't reach the Mac's SearXNG.
- The check found two leaks, both now fixed. `irie anon run` sent a program out from the Mac's own exit when the shell had `NO_PROXY=*`, or from another compartment's exit when run inside an anonymous session. A fetch error named the exit's address in the tunnel.
- A second pass that day found two more, now fixed. A program in the shell that ignored proxy settings, such as Node's fetch, left from the Mac's own exit, which the sandbox now stops. A profile on the generic harness ran an anonymous task with none of its shut paths, which is now refused. A task given to `irie ctl spawn` quoted whole, as `"+anon:acme find who runs it"`, ran outside any compartment. Its leading `@` and `+` words are now read as words (`core.SpawnTokens`).
- A real `+haiku +anon` session spawned through Irie then passed every check. Its proxy variables named only the forwarder's ports. curl, Python, the browser, `exit` and `fetch` left from its compartment's exit, and all else was refused.

---

### 4.17 Plugins

The owner asked on 2026-11-09 to start making plugins for Irie, beginning with a privacy plugin that holds the Mullvad, Tor and Bitwarden work and the dead man's switch. On 2026-11-14 its VPN part became a plugin of its own, for any VPN (§4.18), so that a company's copy, which leaves the privacy plugin out, keeps it to drive the company's VPN. A **plugin** is a set of Irie's features that go on and off together under one name. Each lists its **parts**, and each part says what it brings to Irie (commands, words, skills, agents' tools), which key in config.toml turns it on, what sets it up, and how far it is set up on this computer. `internal/plugins` holds them: a plugin is a value in Go that names its parts, and the code of each part stays where it was (`internal/vpn`, `internal/anon`, `internal/browser`, `internal/dms`). A plugin's code is Irie's own, built into the irie binary and tested with the rest, so nothing is downloaded or loaded to run one.

**VPN**, on by default, has one part, titled by the VPN that `vpn.provider` names:

| Part | What it brings | Its own switch | What sets it up |
|---|---|---|---|
| The VPN, such as Mullvad VPN or Cisco Secure Client | The header's mark in every client, `:vpn`, Plugins › VPN (§4.18) | `vpn.on`, which `irie plugins on vpn` turns on with the plugin | `:setup vpn`; the VPN's app is the person's, or their company's, to install |

**Privacy**:

| Part | What it brings | Its own switch | What sets it up |
|---|---|---|---|
| Anonymous research | `+anon`, `+tor`, `irie anon`, irie-anon's search and fetch (§4.16) | `anon.on` | `:setup anon` |
| Mullvad Browser for agents | irie-browser's tools, `irie browser` (§4.15) | `browser.connector` | `:setup browser` |
| Dead man's switch | `:setup switch`, `/irie:dead-mans-switch`, `irie dms` ([extensions.md](proposals/extensions.md) §2) | none: on while its skill is (`skills.disabled`) | `:setup switch` |

**Writing**, the second plugin ([writers.md](proposals/writers.md)), is on by default, and has two parts:

| Part | What it brings | Its own switch | What sets it up |
|---|---|---|---|
| Writing in Variorum | `:write [task]`, Plugins › Writing in Variorum, `/irie:variorum`: sessions that help a writer in Variorum, Lillian Duarte's writing app, through its door for AI, beside their words and never over them; Variorum's door in `:write` sessions alone, as their own MCP server; `irie variorum`, which keeps the setups while either part is on | none: on while its skill is (`skills.disabled`) | `:write`: the first session walks the writer through Variorum's door. The check looks for Variorum in an Applications folder |
| Variorum tab | The note a writing session works on, in the Mac app's right sidebar as Variorum holds it, with Show in Variorum, and the `variorum` op behind it | `variorum.tab`, on by default: off, the tab goes and `:write` sessions keep their door, so a bundle can take it away | The tab's Connect button, or `pbpaste \| irie variorum connect`, once for Claude Code's setup and once for Any MCP client's. The check reads only files: ready once Variorum is installed and both setups are kept, their programs its door, and it warns while `~/.claude.json` sets Variorum's door up for every folder, which fills its 16 places and shuts Irie's setup out, as Variorum lets `claude-code` in with one key at a time |

**Markets**, the third plugin, is off by default, and has one part:

| Part | What it brings | Its own switch | What sets it up |
|---|---|---|---|
| Market map | Window › Markets (⇧⌘M) in the Mac app, and `:markets`: the page in `markets.folder`, its `index.html`, in a window of its own, loaded again each time it is rebuilt; `:markets [task]` starts the task in that folder | none: on while its plugin is | The Markets window's Choose Folder…, or `markets.folder` in config.toml. The check looks for the folder, then its page |

The map is the person's own: a page they keep in a folder of theirs, often beside the research and the scripts that build it. Irie carries none of it. The window loads the page from the folder, lets it read nothing outside the folder, and blocks every load from the network. A link out opens in the browser only when the person clicks it, never at the page's own word: a script opens no window, and a link it follows itself goes nowhere. Without the block the page doesn't show at all. So what the research holds stays on the computer. While the window is open it asks the core how the map stands every three seconds (the `markets` op, which reads only the config and the page's file). In the same folder a change shows once two looks in a row agree on it, so a page being written, or taken away and written again, never shows half done, and a look that fails, as while the core restarts for an install, changes nothing. What the map lacks shows in the window with what fixes it: the plugin's switch, Choose Folder…, or the words that start a session to make the page. A task typed after `:markets` runs in the folder itself, never in a worktree of it, so each rebuild shows in the window as it is made; it takes `+agent` words and the task bar's menus as any task does, but no `@repo`, and it is never a follow-up to the task before.

**Mail**, the fifth plugin ([mail.md](proposals/mail.md)), is on by default and shows nothing until an account is added. Its parts are kinds of account:

| Part | What it brings | Its own switch | What sets it up |
|---|---|---|---|
| Gmail | The right sidebar's Mail tab (⌥⌘7, `:mail`), the Mail window (Window › Mail, ⇧⌘E) and `irie mail`: every account's newest mail in one list, each account in a colour of its own with its provider's mark; Gmail over IMAP and SMTP with an app password, kept in the Keychain | none: on while the plugin is | Add Account in Mail, or `irie mail add gmail ADDRESS` |
| Proton Mail | The same, for Proton Mail accounts, through Proton Mail Bridge on this computer | none | Proton Mail Bridge, the person's own to install (Proton's paid plans); then Add Account with the password in Bridge's Mailbox details. The check looks for Bridge in an Applications folder |

The core reads the mail (`internal/mail`, the `mail` op) and the clients only draw it; it keeps what it reads in memory only, and refuses the op to any process inside an agent's pane, a look as much as a send.

**Bitwarden**, the sixth plugin, is on by default and does nothing until the person unlocks it. The owner asked on 2026-11-15 to "integrate bw as a pluggin to auto fill accounts and pw from bw". It has two parts:

| Part | What it brings | Its own switch | What sets it up |
|---|---|---|---|
| Fill sign-ins in panes | A key in each web pane's header, a banner over a sign-in page while Bitwarden is locked, and Unlock Bitwarden… or Lock Bitwarden in the Plugins menu and Settings › Plugins: a pane's sign-in page fills itself with the login the person's vault has for its address | none: on while the plugin is | The Bitwarden CLI, `bw`, the person's own to install (`brew install bitwarden-cli`) and sign in with `bw login` in their own terminal. The check looks for `bw` where the app does and reads the one entry of its data file that names the signed-in account |
| Bitwarden for agents | Held back: see below | none yet | none yet |

The Mac app does the filling itself (`Bitwarden.swift`), and the core only says how the plugin stands, so a login never crosses the core's socket, which agents can reach, and nothing of it answers there. To unlock, the app asks for the master password in a sheet and gives it to `bw unlock --raw --passwordfile /dev/stdin` on standard input, never in an argument or the environment, where `ps` shows them. With the session key in `BW_SESSION` it runs `bw sync`, when it can, and `bw list items`, then `bw lock` at once, which ends the key, so the key lives a few seconds, in only those two runs. bw runs from Homebrew's two places only, never from a path a setting names, which an agent could point at a program that records the master password, with an environment the app makes (no one's `NODE_OPTIONS` or session key), and with `--nointeraction`, so it never waits on a prompt. Of what it lists the app keeps only the logins whose addresses fill one of the web panes there are, and nothing on disk. They go when the Mac locks its screen or sleeps, when its user switches away, after four hours, when the person locks Bitwarden and when the plugin goes off; a list the core can't send, as while it restarts for an install, changes nothing.

A login fills a page only on its own host and port, even where Bitwarden would match the whole domain, as every app on 127.0.0.1 shares one; its Starts with and Exact addresses are kept as Bitwarden means them, and one marked Never or matched by a regular expression fills nothing. The fill runs in the page's main frame only, in a script world of its own that the page's scripts can't see into, with the login passed in as values rather than written into the script, and only while the page is on the pane's own origin. Unasked, as a page loads, it fills a form that signs in: a username or email field before a current password, or an email field in a form or on a page whose address says it signs in, as a sign-in by emailed link has; only fields still empty, never a form that asks for a new password, never one where another account is typed in already, never a hidden field, and it never sends a form. The key's Fill fills the page at the person's word, over what the fields hold. A page fills once until it loads again.

**Terms in short**, the seventh plugin ([browser-extension.md](proposals/browser-extension.md)), is on by default. The owner asked for it on 2026-11-15: a website's terms of service in words a person reads, worked out by code with no tokens spent, and a chat to ask about them, first in a browser plugin of Irie's, then as an Irie plugin of its own. Its one part:

| Part | What it brings | Its own switch | What sets it up |
|---|---|---|---|
| Terms in short | A site's terms of service, or its privacy policy, in plain words: the Terms tab in the Mac app's right sidebar, a page the core serves at http://127.0.0.1:7393/ while the plugin is on; `irie terms ADDRESS` in a terminal; and the browser extension's Terms tool. The points that matter, against you first, each with the clause it comes from and a link that opens the page at those words; and answers to questions about them, `irie terms ask` or the chat under the points | none: on while the plugin is | Nothing: ready once the core serves its page. The check knocks once on its address |

**Browser extension**, the eighth plugin, is on by default and shows nothing in a browser until it is set up there. Its one part:

| Part | What it brings | Its own switch | What sets it up |
|---|---|---|---|
| Irie in your browser | A browser extension for Chrome and the browsers built like it (Brave, Edge, Arc, Vivaldi, Chromium) and for Firefox, and `irie extension`: its button opens a side panel beside the page with the tools of Irie's plugins, Terms in short the first, which reads the page the button was pressed on. Its one way into Irie is a native messaging host, `irie extension host`, which the browser starts and talks to over its standard input and output | none: on while the plugin is | `:setup extension`, or `irie setup extension`: it writes the extension to `<data>/extension`, the host's program, and the host's file for each browser that has run here; loading that folder in the browser, once, is the person's own step. The check reads only files: the folder and its version, the browsers set up, and when each last talked to Irie |

**Yoink**, the ninth plugin, is off by default, so a new Irie downloads from no site until asked. The owner asked for it on 2026-11-16, after seeing yoinks, a terminal app that downloads a video from a link with yt-dlp (its idea, none of its code). Its one part:

| Part | What it brings | Its own switch | What sets it up |
|---|---|---|---|
| Video downloads | A video, or its sound as an mp3, from a link to YouTube, Vimeo, Wikimedia Commons or another of the sites yt-dlp knows, into `yoink.folder`, `~/Downloads` unless it says otherwise: the Yoink tab in the Mac app's right sidebar, a pane of the app's own view (`yoink`), with a box for the link, its sizes to pick from, the downloads as they go and Show in Finder; `:yoink [audio\|720p] LINK` in any task bar, the phone's and the sidebar's too; and `irie yoink [--audio] [--res N] LINK` in a terminal | none: on while the plugin is | uvx (`brew install uv`), which runs the pinned yt-dlp, and ffmpeg (`brew install ffmpeg`), the person's own to install. The check reads only files: uvx, or the yt-dlp `yoink.ytdlp` names, and ffmpeg, on the PATH or where their installers put them, and Deno, without which some of YouTube's formats may be missing |

**Chat**, the tenth plugin ([chat.md](proposals/chat.md)), is on by default and shows nothing until an account is added. The owner asked for it on 2026-11-21: "a discord irie pluggin", "like a generic plugin that hooks into things like discord and slack etc". Its parts are kinds of account, and one for agents:

| Part | What it brings | Its own switch | What sets it up |
|---|---|---|---|
| Discord | Your Discord servers through a bot of yours: the right sidebar's Chat tab, a plugin pane of the app's own view, `:chat` and `irie chat`: each server's channels, threads and forums, what the bot may do in each, their messages, and posting as the bot, a reply, a thread or a forum post | none: on while the plugin is | A bot of the person's own (Developer Portal › the application › Bot), invited to their server, and `irie chat add discord`, which takes its token on standard input and keeps it in the Keychain. The check reads the config and the core's last look |
| Slack | Your Slack workspace through an app of yours: the same tab, words and commands, its channels the bot is in, their messages and threads, and posting as the app's bot, a reply in a thread | none: on while the plugin is | `irie chat add slack`, which prints a link that makes the app from Irie's manifest, asking for only the scopes the plugin uses, then takes its bot token (`xoxb-`) and, for mentions and DMs as they come, its app-level token (`xapp-`) |
| Chat for agents | `/irie:chat` and `irie chat` for agents: a post an agent makes is a draft that waits for the person to send; reads only on an account the person opens to agents; posts of their own only in a channel the person opens, logged, rate-limited and never pinging everyone | `chat.agents`, on by default: off, every call from an agent is refused and the skill leaves every session | none: drafts need nothing. The skill goes to sessions once an account is there |

**Obsidian**, the eleventh plugin, is on by default and shows nothing until a vault is chosen. The owner asked for it on 2026-11-21: "obsidian integration into iries notes above jot, i want an option that opens a full pane (imbeded obsidian)". macOS has no supported way to put another program's window inside an app's own views, and Obsidian is closed source, so its own window can't be the pane; asked with previews of the closest options, the owner picked a pane of Irie's own that reads and writes the vault, and the notepad kept as a note in the vault, both ways. A vault is a folder of Markdown notes that any program may read and write, and Obsidian keeps up with changes made outside it (Obsidian's help, "How Obsidian stores data"), so Obsidian itself is the person's to have or not. Its parts:

| Part | What it brings | Its own switch | What sets it up |
|---|---|---|---|
| Vault card | A card of the panes beside a session (§7.1, docs/MAC.md "Vault cards") with the vault's notes: their tree, a note in the code card's editor, wrapped, in the code colours, with Obsidian's syntax coloured; ⌘-click a `[[link]]` to open its note, at its heading or block, a note a link names that isn't there yet made as Obsidian makes one; Back and Forward; the notes that link to the one on show; ⌘P to open one by name; a new note; Open in Obsidian through its `obsidian://open?path=` link. The Notes panel's ⤢ opens it with the notepad's note on show | none: on while the plugin is | Choose Your Vault… or New Vault… on the card, or `obsidian.vault` in config.toml. The check reads only files: the folder, and whether Obsidian has opened it, its `.obsidian` folder there |
| Notes in the vault | The notepad (§4.20) kept as `obsidian.note` in the vault too, `Irie Notes.md` unless it says otherwise, both ways: a task a note | `obsidian.notes`, on by default | A vault |

**Jammin**, the twelfth plugin ([jammin.md](proposals/jammin.md)), is on by default and does nothing until a client asks, which the Mac app does only while its tab shows. The owner asked for it on 2026-11-21: "an irie music plugin", made "so users can hook up apple music google spotify local whatever", with their Spotify login in Bitwarden and "a spotify integration plugin that goes into the jam box, thats what i want to call it jammin". Its parts are kinds of source, one source playing at a time:

| Part | What it brings | Its own switch | What sets it up |
|---|---|---|---|
| Spotify | The right sidebar's Jammin tab (`:jammin`), and `irie jammin`: what plays on any device of the person's, play, pause, skip, seek, volume, shuffle, repeat and devices, their playlists and a search, through the Web API | none: on while the plugin is | A Spotify app of the person's own at Spotify's developer dashboard, with the redirect URI `http://127.0.0.1/jammin/spotify`, then Link Spotify in the tab, whose sign-in the Bitwarden plugin fills, or `irie jammin link spotify`. Since March 2026 Spotify lets apps in development mode work only for Premium accounts |
| Apple Music | The same, for the Music app on this Mac: its library and Apple Music, with its playlists and a search of the library | none | Nothing: the Music app is the Mac's. macOS asks once whether Irie may control it |

**Jammin.** The core does the work (`internal/jammin`, the `jammin` op), and every client draws from it. Each source is behind one interface (a look, a control, its playlists, a search, its devices), so another service is one more source. Spotify's is the Web API, as its documentation says: the Authorization Code flow with PKCE, with no secret, an S256 challenge, a random state and only the scopes the plugin uses, the browser sent back to `127.0.0.1` at a free port, where the core listens for 10 minutes and answers only its own Host and state; the refresh token in the login Keychain, service "Irie jammin", and the access token in memory; `GET /me/player` at most every 4 s while something plays and 15 s while nothing does, whoever asks, and half a second after a control, whose change the answer shows at once; a 429's `Retry-After` waited out up to 5 s for a control, and held for every call; Premium required, no active device and a quota used up said in words. Irie carries no Client ID: development mode caps an app at five users. The Music app's is its scripting dictionary, through scripts of the core's own in JavaScript for Automation run by `osascript`, each script on its standard input and its words as arguments, so no search or name becomes script; the core sends the Music app nothing while the process table says it isn't running, since a script would start it, and writes the current track's artwork, which it gives as data, to `<state>/jammin/art`, keeping 20. Starting a source pauses each other source playing on this computer, so two never play over each other, as Spotify's Developer Policy asks; one playing on another device, such as a phone, plays on. What a source says stays in memory. The core refuses every call from inside an agent's pane, a look as much as a play: what a person listens to is theirs, and the policy forbids giving Spotify's content to an AI model. The tab keeps Spotify's design rules: its full logo beside its content, the artwork uncropped with nothing over it, names as Spotify gives them, a link back to the item, and no list that holds two sources' music.

**Terms in short.** `internal/terms` reads terms with code, not a model, so reading them costs no tokens. A page's text (`internal/htmltext`, its main part) is cut into its headings and clauses: paragraphs and list items, a long paragraph between its sentences. A catalog of 46 kinds of clause, each in a group (disputes, your account, what you post, your data, money, if things go wrong, changes, using it), finds each by weighted patterns, with a heading on the topic counting one more: binding arbitration, class-action and jury waivers, a short deadline for claims, whose law and whose courts, closing an account at will, a licence to what you post, AI training, selling or sharing data, ads, auto-renewal, refunds, price changes, caps on liability, indemnity, changes to the terms, and the rest. The categories follow the research on unfair clauses in terms of service (CLAUDETTE's eight) and the EU's list of unfair terms (Directive 93/13/EEC); the patterns and the words are Irie's own. Each kind says its point in words written for it, filled in from the clause: the opt-out's 30 days, the cap's $100 or what you paid in 12 months, the State of California, the age of 13. A pattern of what the company may do drops a match whose subject is the person ("you may close your account" is no closing at will), a clause that says the opposite turns a point round ("we do not sell your personal information" is in your favour), and one passage each way says both. Each point has a verdict, against you, to watch, good to know or for you, each with its own glyph (✕, !, ·, ✓), so colour never carries it alone. What such documents usually say and these don't is listed too: how to close your account, refunds where they take payments. A page in several languages is read from its English; one in another language says the reading doesn't know it yet.

A question finds the topics its words are about (a refund, suing, who owns what you post) and answers with those points and their clauses; else it searches the clauses by their words with BM25, after the words terms use for what people ask in their own ("sue" finds arbitration). "What's the catch?" answers with what is against you. Every answer quotes the passages it rests on. A local model words the answer only when the person asks, in the panel or with `--model`: `extension.model`, else the biggest model of 16 billion parameters or fewer and 10 GB or less that an Ollama on this computer has for chat (Irie's own, `local.url`, then Ollama's usual port), never a cloud one; Irie's code still finds the passages, the model answers from them alone, and its words come as it writes them. The extension's reading of a page sends nothing anywhere: the browser gives the host the page it shows, scripts' work included, and the host fetches only the address the person gave and the links on that page to the terms. A link of a page's own never reaches this computer or its local network: the dialer refuses loopback, private, link-local, shared and reserved addresses, this computer's own addresses and the networks they are on (its global IPv6 /64 too), and NAT64 and 6to4 addresses that carry such an address, as each connection is made, redirects' too. So a hostile page can't make it knock on a router or a local app; one way round stays, the network's own public address where its router sends traffic back in. `irie terms --allow-local` lets only the host typed be local, never a link's or a redirect's. A page and a link's page are read up to 8 MB and 2 MB, and the places sites keep terms are tried three at a time. Its fetches say who is asking (`irie-terms/VERSION`), and a site that refuses them is read in the browser instead. What a page or a model says is printed and shown without control characters or the marks that reverse text, so a page can't rewrite a terminal's lines. It is not legal advice, and says so.

**The extension.** Manifest V3, for Chrome 116 and later and Firefox 128 and later, in `internal/extension/web`, built into the irie binary and written out by setup, its manifest taking this build's names (§2.10). It asks for no site's pages: `activeTab` gives it the page its button was pressed on, for that press, and `scripting` reads that page's HTML as the browser shows it; `nativeMessaging` reaches Irie, `storage` keeps the panel's one setting, and `sidePanel` its panel (Firefox's `sidebar_action`). A key in the manifest fixes its ID in Chrome, which the host's file names; Firefox names it by its own ID. Its pages run only their own files. What a page says goes into the panel as text, never as HTML, and only http and https addresses become links. The panel's colours are Roots' Evergreen (§2.11), kept by `tools/design.sh`. When Irie's build of the extension differs from the one running, the panel offers to update it: the host writes this build's files into the folder then, just before the extension reloads, never under a running one. The panel takes a newer read over an older, slower one, gives up on a request that takes too long, and says where a model runs: on this computer, or on the server `local.url` names. The Terms tab shows the same panel as a page: while Terms in short is on, the core serves it at http://127.0.0.1:7393/ (`internal/core/terms.go`, looking every 5 seconds whether the plugin is on), and the Mac app shows it as the plugin's web pane. There it asks Irie over that address instead of native messaging (one POST a request, the answer as JSON lines), and reads a site by the address typed, since no button reads the page beside it. It answers only under its loopback address, never another name for it, and only requests of its own page, which send its origin and JSON; the page runs only its own files. `irie terms serve` serves it the same way where no core does.
