**Yoink.** The core does the work (`internal/yoink`, the `yoink` op), so every client draws the same downloads. yt-dlp downloads, never vendored and never fetched unpinned: `uvx --from 'yt-dlp[default]==2026.10.26' yt-dlp`, the release `yoink.version` names (a release, never `latest`), with the extras yt-dlp's own instructions give, which carry what YouTube's player needs; or the yt-dlp program `yoink.ytdlp` names. ffmpeg, with the ffprobe that comes with it, merges a video with its sound and makes mp3s, and yt-dlp is told their folder. A look at a link runs `yt-dlp --dump-single-json` and gives its title, site, uploader and length, and its choices: the best, each height, tallest first, named by the smaller side as yt-dlp sorts them (an upright phone video is 1080p), and the sound alone as an mp3, each with about how big it comes to. A playlist is refused: Yoink takes one video at a time. A download takes the tallest video no taller than the choice, with H.264 and AAC where the site has them at that height, so a Mac plays it, merged into an mp4 where they fit one and an mkv otherwise; or the best sound, made an mp3. Its file takes yt-dlp's own name, `%(title).150B [%(id)s].%(ext)s` with `--restrict-filenames`, in the folder (`-P home:`), and its parts wait in a folder of its own under Irie's state (`-P temp:`) until they are whole, so a canceled or failed download leaves nothing in Downloads. yt-dlp says how far it is in `--newline` and `--progress-template` lines, and which formats it takes and the file it made with `--print`, each line starting with a tag of Yoink's. A download runs in a session of its own and writes those lines to files in its folder rather than to pipes to the core, so it runs on while the core restarts, as at each install: the core after reads the folders, watches each that still runs by its pid and start time, and finishes each that ended while no core watched from what it wrote. Three run at once, and a fourth is refused until one ends; the list keeps the 30 newest that finished, and Clear forgets them, their files staying. Cancel ends the download's whole process group and removes its parts. With the plugin off no download starts, and one already running finishes.

A link is data. Yoink takes one http or https link that names a site, with no name or password before it, which can hide where it goes, up to 4,096 characters, and hands it to yt-dlp as one argument after `--`, never through a shell. yt-dlp runs in its download's own folder with `--ignore-config`, so no config file, its user's or one in the folder it runs in, adds a command to run after a download, and since 2024.07.01 it writes no file whose extension isn't on its list of media and data types (CVE-2024-38519). What a site says, a title or an error, shows on one line without control characters or the marks that reverse text. **Agents can't use it:** the core refuses the op to any process inside an agent's pane, a look as much as a start, typed `:yoink` too, as it does Mail's. A link is the person's own word. A page an agent reads could otherwise send Irie to fetch from a site of its choosing, fill the person's Downloads with files they open later, or read what they download; and an agent that needs a video runs yt-dlp itself, in its own folder, where its person sees what it did. Sites that want you signed in, such as Instagram, need cookies, which Yoink doesn't take yet: they come later, and such a site's refusal says so.

**Chat.** The core does the work (`internal/chat`, the `chat` op), and every client draws from it. Each service is a connector behind one interface (who the token is, its servers and channels, a channel's messages, a post, an edit, a delete), so another service is one more connector, with no change to the tab, the words or the agents' rules. Discord's is the REST API v10, as its documentation says: `Authorization: Bot`, its `DiscordBot` User-Agent, its rate-limit buckets from the `X-RateLimit` headers and a 429's wait honoured up to a minute, after which a call fails and says how long; what the bot may do in each channel worked out from its roles and the channel's overwrites in Discord's order; channels it may not view, which Discord leaves out since 2026-11-16, skipped. A bot's token, never a person's: Discord forbids automating a person's account, and a token whose user isn't a bot is refused. Slack's is its Web API, as its documentation says: a read as a GET with its query and no body, a change as a POST of JSON, the token in the Authorization header, a 429's Retry-After honoured as Discord's is, and each refusal's code in words with what fixes it, a scope the app lacks by name; a user token (`xoxp-`), which would speak as the person, is refused. A Slack thread is its first message's ts, read with `conversations.replies`. Slack has no `allowed_mentions`, so the text decides who is pinged: the person's own post keeps the marks it spells out and has the rest of `<`, `>` and `&` escaped, a draft the person sends keeps its people and loses everyone, here and groups, and an agent's own post names its people as plain words, which ping no one since Irie never sends `link_names`. Without the Message Content intent Discord leaves people's message text out, except in DMs to the bot, messages that mention it and its own; the core reads the application's flags at sign-in, and a read says so once, above the messages, rather than showing them empty. The core keeps each account's channels for 5 minutes and a channel's newest page for 30 s, in memory; on disk, in `<state>/chat`, only the agents' drafts (`drafts.json`), which must outlive the core's restarts until the person sends them, and the log of every post, edit and delete agents made (`log.jsonl`, the newest 2,000, with their text), so the person sees all that agents said in their name; nothing anyone else wrote. Tokens live in the login Keychain, service "Irie chat", written as Mail's passwords are, never in config.toml or an answer of the core's.

**What agents may do with chat.** The core knows a call from inside an agent's pane by its process, as Mail's refusals do, and which session it is (`agentOf`). An agent's post is a draft, kept with its session, that waits for the person to send, changed or not, or discard; the agent sees its own drafts and what came of them, and may withdraw one while it waits. A draft the person sends pings the people it names and no more (`allowed_mentions` of users). An agent posts on its own only in a channel the person sets to `post` (the tab, `irie chat agents CHANNEL post`, or the channel's table in config.toml), never for a whole account and never in a DM; there it posts at most once a minute and `chat.agent_posts` an hour (10 unless set, at most 60), counted from the log, pinging no one (`allowed_mentions` with nothing to parse), and each post is logged before it answers; it edits or deletes only its own posts there. Every post or draft of an agent's that names everyone, here or a role (Discord's `@everyone`, `@here`, `<@&ROLE>`; Slack's `<!everyone>`, `<!channel>`, `<!here>`, `<!subteam^…>`) is refused. An agent reads messages only on an account with `agents_read`, each read headed as what people wrote, never instructions; otherwise it sees channels by name, without their topics. Adding, testing or removing an account, sending a draft and saying what agents may do are the person's alone. An agent that sets out to could still edit config.toml or read a token with `security`, as with Mail's passwords: the rules bind an agent that goes through Irie, and the skill tells agents never to try the rest.

**Obsidian.** The vault is read and written by Irie's own code: the Mac app's card for the notes a person reads and edits (`VaultPane.swift`, `Vault.swift`, on the code card's documents, which save as they are typed and take in what changes on disk), and the core for the notepad's note (`internal/obsidian`, `internal/core/obsidian.go`). The `obsidian` op only reads: where the vault is (`obsidian.vault`, `~` expanded), its name, the folder's, as Obsidian names a vault, whether Obsidian has opened it, and how the notepad's note stands; the vault is chosen with the `config` op. A note's links are Obsidian's ("Internal links"): `[[Note]]`, `[[Folder/Note]]`, `[[Note#Heading]]`, `[[Note#^block]]` and `[[Note|shown words]]`, `![[embeds]]`, and Markdown links, `[words](Note%20name.md)`. A name finds the note of that name beside the one linking first, then the one nearest the vault's top; a link to a file that is no note opens it in the app the Mac opens it with; one to a page opens it on a browser card. The code card's Markdown colours take Obsidian's additions ("Obsidian Flavored Markdown", "Tags"): links and embeds, `#tags`, `==highlights==`, `%%comments%%` over lines, a `^block` identifier at a line's end and a callout's `> [!kind]`.

The notepad's note is a task list. Each task is a note: `- [ ] words`, ticked `- [x] words`, its lines after the first indented under it, and at its end a block identifier ("Internal links": a caret, then letters, digits and dashes), Irie's own, `^i` and six letters and digits of the note's id's SHA-256, the same wherever it is made, or one the person gave the task, which stays. A new note starts with a line Obsidian hides, a `%%` comment that says what the note is. The core looks at the note each second and as the notepad changes. What the note changed since the core last saw it goes to the notepad as the notepad's own ops, each written as a transaction (§4.20): a task's new words are an edit, a tick a check, a task new to the note a note after the note before it, a task gone a delete to Recently Deleted, and a new order moves. A task finds its note by its block identifier, else by the words it had, else, while it is new and has none yet, by its place. Then the notepad goes to the note: every task that is a note takes its note's words and tick, in the notepad's order, so the lines that are no task, a heading or words of the person's, stay where they were written; a note new in the notepad goes after the note before it; a note deleted there goes. The notepad stays the copy kept whole:
- Nothing the note does loses a note: a task deleted there waits in Recently Deleted for 30 days, as any deleted note does; a note gone from the vault deletes nothing, and is written again; and notes gone at once, more than three with none of the note's tasks left, or more than ten that are more than half, as from a file a slip or a sync emptied, are held, written back, and the notepad's `vault.problem` says so.
- Nothing the core writes loses the person's words in the note: it writes only once the note has been still for 5 seconds after another program changed it, as Obsidian saves as it is typed, at most once a second, and only once it has read the note again and found it as it was; whole or not at all, beside it then renamed over it, keeping its mode (a new one 0600). A change the notepad turns down, as a note past its size, leaves its task as written. A task the note edits after the notepad deleted it brings the note back, as an edit of a deleted note does.
- `obsidian-notes.json` keeps what the next merge weighs the note against, each task's note, block identifier, words and tick when the two last agreed, so a core started after the note changed tells a task deleted there from a note new in the notepad. Without it, as the first time, every task with a note keeps the notepad's words and ticks, a task with no note is a new one, and nothing is deleted.
- A notepad a newer core wrote, shown only (§4.20), goes to no note.

The notepad's frame names its note while the notepad is kept in the vault (`vault`: its path, when the two last agreed, and what keeps them apart, when something does), so the Notes panel marks it, says a problem under its title, and its ⤢ opens the vault's card on the note.

**One switch.** `[plugins] privacy`, on by default: each part keeps its own switch, and those are off by default, so a new Irie shows nothing of it until asked. `[plugins] vpn` is the VPN's in the same way; since the VPN plugin has one part, turning the plugin on turns the part on too. With the plugin off, none of its parts runs, whatever its own switch says. `config.Config`'s `VPNOn`, `AnonOn` and `BrowserOn` are where the rest of Irie asks (`VPNOn` with `plugins.vpn`, the others with `plugins.privacy`); a word or command of a part that is off says which switch turns it on (`plugins.Why`); the guide's skill leaves every session, with the reason in `irie skills` (`skills.Settings.Left`); and the VPN's mark leaves every client. Each part's own switch keeps its value meanwhile, so the plugin on again brings back what was on.

**A build without a plugin.** A plugin's code can also be left out of the binary, for a copy that must not carry it, such as a company's (§2.10): `go build -tags noprivacy` builds Irie without the privacy plugin, `-tags nowriting` without the writing plugin, `-tags nomarkets` without the markets plugin, `-tags novpn` without the VPN plugin, `-tags nomail` without the mail plugin and its IMAP and MIME libraries, `-tags nobitwarden` without the Bitwarden plugin, so the Mac app shows no key in a pane, `-tags noterms` without Terms in short, `-tags noextension` without the browser extension plugin, `-tags noyoink` without Yoink, `-tags nochat` without Chat, `-tags noobsidian` without Obsidian, `-tags nojammin` without Jammin, and `-tags novpn,noprivacy,nowriting,nomarkets,nomail,nobitwarden,noterms,noextension,noyoink,nochat,noobsidian,nojammin` without any of them. A company's copy usually keeps the VPN plugin, to drive the company's VPN (§4.18), and leaves Mail, Terms in short, the browser extension, Yoink and Jammin out with the other personal plugins; it keeps Obsidian, whose vault is a folder of notes on the computer. Each plugin's files carry its tag: its entry in `internal/plugins`, which adds itself to the plugins this build has; its uses in `internal/core` (`anon.go`, `browser.go`, for which `privacy_off.go` stands in, `vpn.go`, for which `vpn_off.go` does, `markets.go`, for which `markets_off.go` does, `mail.go`, for which `mail_off.go` does, `yoink.go`, for which `yoink_off.go` does, `chat.go`, for which `chat_off.go` does, `obsidian.go`, for which `obsidian_off.go` does, `jammin.go`, for which `jammin_off.go` does, and `variorum.go`, for which `writing_off.go` does); its commands in `cmd/irie` (`anon.go`, `browser.go`, `dms.go` and `privacy.go`, which registers them and their lines in the usage, `vpn.go` for `irie setup vpn`, `mail.go` for `irie mail`, `writing.go` for `irie variorum`, `terms.go` for `irie terms`, for which `terms_off.go` stands in, `extension.go` for `irie setup extension` and `irie extension`, for which `extension_off.go` does, `yoink.go` for `irie yoink`, for which `yoink_off.go` does, `chat.go` for `irie chat`, for which `chat_off.go` does, and `jammin.go` for `irie jammin`, for which `jammin_off.go` does); Terms in short's page in `internal/core/terms.go`, for which `terms_off.go` stands in; the mail plugin's own package, `internal/mail`, the writing plugin's, `internal/variorum`, Yoink's, `internal/yoink`, Chat's, `internal/chat`, Obsidian's, `internal/obsidian`, Jammin's, `internal/jammin`, and the packages Terms in short and the browser extension share, `internal/terms` and `internal/extension`, which a build leaves out only without both; and its skills (`internal/skills/bundled_privacy.go`, `bundled_writing.go`, `bundled_chat.go`). Anonymous research uses `internal/vpn` for Mullvad too, so only a build without both the VPN and the privacy plugin leaves that package out. The config keeps the plugins' defaults itself (`internal/config/privacy.go`, and the VPNs' names in `config.VPNKinds`), so it needs none of their packages; it still reads a config.toml that sets them, their switches (`VPNOn`, `AnonOn`, `BrowserOn`) read as off, and `irie config print` leaves the keys out. Such a build has no `irie anon`, `irie browser`, `irie dms` or `irie setup anon|browser|privacy|vpn`; `plugins` lists only the plugins it has; `hello` leaves out the `vpn` and `anon` ops, so the Mac app's Plugins menu, which holds each plugin's items, lists only the plugins the build has and shows the VPN's items only with the `vpn` op, the `markets` op without the markets plugin, the `mail` op without the mail plugin, so the Mac app shows no Mail tab, window or menu items, the `variorum` op without the writing plugin, so it shows no Variorum tab, the `yoink` op without Yoink, so it shows no Yoink tab, the `chat` op without Chat, so it shows no Chat tab, the `obsidian` op without Obsidian, so it shows no Vault card, and the notepad stays in Irie alone, and the `jammin` op without Jammin, so it shows no Jammin tab; and `+anon`, `:vpn`, `:setup anon`, `:setup switch`, `:write`, `:markets`, `:yoink`, `:chat` and `:jammin` say the plugin isn't in this build (`plugins.Why` refuses a part no plugin of the build has). `internal/plugins`' test builds every package with the twelve tags and checks that `irie` links none of `internal/vpn`, `anon`, `browser`, `dms`, `htmltext`, `mcp`, `mail`, `variorum`, `extension`, `terms`, `yoink`, `chat`, `obsidian` and `jammin`, nor the mail plugin's IMAP and MIME libraries, then builds `irie` with a company's eight, `noprivacy,nowriting,nomarkets,nomail,noterms,noextension,noyoink,nojammin`, and checks that it links `internal/vpn`, `internal/chat` and `internal/obsidian`, which a company's copy keeps, and none of the others; `tools/check.sh` vets every package with both sets.

**How far each part is set up.** Each part's check reads only files and the config: whether `mullvad`, `tor`, SearXNG, Mullvad Browser and its clean profile are where Irie looks, whether the switch's checks were made, and whether `bw` is installed and names a signed-in account. It never reads the network, an account or a vault. A part is *ready* (on, with what it needs), *off* (its own switch), *needs* (on, and it lacks something: the sentence says what and what fixes it), *off with its plugin*, or *held* (not in this build). The core adds what only it knows, the VPN's state now.

**Where it shows.**
- `irie plugins` prints each plugin and a line for each part, with ● ready, ! needs, · off and ○ held, so colour never carries the state alone. `irie plugins on|off PLUGIN [PART]` turns a plugin or a part on or off, through the core when it runs, which keeps the file's comments, else in the file.
- `:plugins` in any task bar answers with the same lines.
- `:markets` opens the Mac app's Markets window, as Window › Markets (⇧⌘M) does while the markets plugin is on; a client without the window says where the page is.
- `:chat` says how the chat accounts stand and which drafts wait; the Mac app shows its Chat tab instead.
- `:yoink [audio|720p] LINK` starts a download, and `:yoink` alone says how the downloads stand; the Mac app shows its Yoink tab for either, and a client without it says the text.
- `:jammin` says what plays on each source of the jam box, and `:jammin play`, `pause`, `next` and `previous` act on the one playing, else the one on show, or on the source named first (`:jammin music next`); the Mac app shows its Jammin tab for either.
- `:setup anon`, `:setup browser`, `:setup privacy` and `:setup extension` start a tool session that runs `irie setup NAME`, as `:setup local` does: the person answers each yes or no in its terminal. `irie setup privacy` turns the plugin on if it is off, then offers each part that isn't ready, one at a time, and starts the dead man's switch guide when asked.
- The Mac app's menu bar has a Plugins menu: each plugin's switch, each part by name and state with a submenu of its sentence, its switch and Set Up…, and Plugin Settings…, which opens the tab below.
- Settings › Plugins in the Mac app: each plugin with its switch, and each part with its state, its sentence, its own switch (greyed while the plugin is off) and a button for its words, as the Plugins menu has them: Set Up… for `:setup` words, Show Mail for `:mail`, and Start <part>… for words that start a session, such as `:write` or a plugin's word. The core's `plugins` op answers it, and with `plugin`, `part` and `on` turns one on or off, so the app never needs to know which key or value a switch takes. The VPN's switch moved here from Settings › General.
- The VPN's part in the Plugins menu also has Connect and Disconnect, and for a VPN that has them, as Mullvad does, Turn Lockdown Mode On or Off and Account Time Left: the header says what the VPN can do (`can`).
- A part's pane is a tab in the Mac app's right sidebar, after Irie's own tabs, while the part is on; View › Right Sidebar lists the panes, and a pane's part in the Plugins menu has Show in Right Sidebar. Your bundles head Settings › Plugins and the Plugins menu, each with its one switch.

**Bitwarden for agents, held back.** It was built on 2026-11-01 on the branch `irie/irie-extensions` ([extensions.md](proposals/extensions.md) §3) and never installed; it sat in the privacy plugin until the Bitwarden plugin came, on 2026-11-15. Before it came into a plugin, a review on 2026-11-09 found that an agent, with nothing but its own shell, could get past the person's Touch ID: the core counted a process whose parent is pid 1 as the person's, the keys that may allow a request were read from a file any of the user's processes can write, the master password and bw's session key were in bw's environment, where `ps -E` shows them on macOS 14, and an Allow named the request but not the command or environment it would run with. So the part shows, held, and nothing of it runs: [extensions.md](proposals/extensions.md) §3.8 lists what must change first, and the work stays on its branch until then.

**Panes, plugins of your own and bundles** ([plugin-panes.md](proposals/plugin-panes.md)). The owner asked on 2026-11-14 for plugins "as like pane views", with panes for local web apps and a bundle of them.
- **A pane** is a tab a part brings to the Mac app's right sidebar (`ipc.PluginPane`: an `id`, the plugin's name, a dot and the part's; a `title`, an SF Symbols `icon` and a `kind`). The app draws a tab for each pane whose part is on, ready or needing something, after Irie's own tabs; a part that is off, or whose plugin is, has none. A `web` pane shows a page: its `address`, the one `origin` it may load from, the `start` words that start the app behind it and the `store` of website data it keeps, its plugin's. Any other kind names a view of the app's own, as the Writing plugin's Variorum tab can be.
- **A plugin of your own** is a manifest in the plugins folder of Irie's config folder, `plugins/NAME.toml`: the plugin NAME, made of parts that need no code of Irie's (`internal/plugins/manifest.go`). It has a `title`, a `summary`, web panes, each a `[panes.<id>]` table with a `title`, an `address`, an `icon`, `start` words and `remote`, and helpers, skills and tools (below). A manifest is data: Irie reads it and runs nothing from it but a helper's program once you turn its plugin on, and `start` only shows. `[schedules.<id>]` tables are kept for a later build, each a part that is held. Its switch is `plugins.manifests.NAME` in config.toml, on until set off, as the Dock runs the controls in your own `dock.json` without asking, except a plugin with a pane off the computer, which is off until set on, so a manifest dropped in the folder can't bring a remote page by itself. A manifest is read only as a plain file of at most 64 KB. A manifest that doesn't read shows as its plugin with no switch and the reason (`problem`); a name that isn't a manifest's, or one of Irie's own plugins' names, is refused the same way.
- **A helper of yours** is a `[helpers.<id>]` table: a program of yours that Irie keeps running while its plugin is on, such as a listener that turns what you text yourself into sessions (`internal/plugins/ownhelpers.go`, `internal/core/ownhelpers.go`). `run` is the program, a name on the PATH or a full path, and its arguments, `dir` the folder it runs in (the home folder when left out), `status` a JSON file it keeps, `open` a file or folder its Open button opens, `title` and `icon` its name and SF Symbols name; `~/` stands for the home folder. The core runs it under `irie runtime` in a window of its own in irie-agents, `irie-helper`, with `IRIE_BIN` and `IRIE_HELPER` (its id, `PLUGIN.ID`) set, so the helper's whole process group ends when its pane goes. One that exits starts again at most every 30 s; one whose program, arguments or folder change starts again; one whose plugin goes off, or whose manifest goes, stops; and an install or a restart of the core leaves it running, since Irie's version isn't in its spec. It is a child of Irie's tmux server, so on macOS it has what Irie.app was granted, such as Full Disk Access. A manifest whose helper runs a program is off until you turn its plugin on, as one with a page off the computer is, so a manifest dropped in the folder, by an agent say, can't run anything by itself. The `helpers` op lists them, each with whether it runs, since when, what is wrong, and what its status file says: `state` (`ok`, `working`, `needs_you`, `error`), a `line`, and up to 20 `items`, each a `title`, a `detail`, a `state` (`working`, `waiting`, `needs_you`, `done`, `error`), a `session` of Irie's a click shows and an `open` file a click opens. The file is read as data, at most 64 KB, with what can't be shown left out. With `id` and `restart` the op starts one again. The Mac app's right sidebar shows them in its Helpers tab (MAC.md).
- **A skill of yours** is a `[skills.<id>]` table: `path`, a folder of yours that holds a SKILL.md, and `title`, its name in Settings (`internal/plugins/ownskills.go`, `internal/skills/read.go`). While its plugin is on, every Claude Code session that takes skills gets it with Irie's own, as `/irie:<id>`, in the sealed folder it starts with, so a change to the folder reaches the sessions that start after; SOURCES.md there, and `irie skills`, say which plugin it comes from. It comes whole, its references and scripts too, at most 64 files and 1 MB, leaving out hidden files and refusing a link inside the folder, so it can bring in no other file of the computer's. Its SKILL.md needs frontmatter with a `description`, and a `name`, if it gives one, the table's id. As Irie's own skills are, it is words: its frontmatter may set no field that changes what an agent may do (`allowed-tools`, `hooks`, `model` and the rest), and no Markdown of it may run a command as it loads (an exclamation mark before a backtick), so turning a plugin on gives an agent no more than the skill's words and the tools it names. `skills.disabled` leaves it out by name as it does Irie's own. The name of one of Irie's own skills stays Irie's, and of two plugins of yours with a skill of one name the first, by its file's name, keeps it; the other's part says whose it is.
- **Tools of yours** are a `[tools.<id>]` table: `run`, an MCP server of yours, a program that speaks MCP over its standard input and output, as a helper's run is written, and `title` (`internal/plugins/owntools.go`, `internal/core/owntools.go`). While its plugin is on, every session whose agent takes `--mcp-config` gets it among its servers as `<id>`, so Claude Code names its tools `mcp__<id>__<tool>`, and the agent starts it as the session starts; an anonymous research session never does, as it takes no server but Irie's (§4.16). A server's name may not be `irie` or start with `irie-`; Irie's own servers come first in a session and keep their names, and the first of your plugins, by its file's name, keeps one of yours. A part needs its program on this computer to be ready.
- **Words of yours** are a `[words.<id>]` table: `:<id>` in a task bar starts a session for the plugin's app (`internal/plugins/ownwords.go`, `core/read.go`). `skill`, one of the manifest's own `[skills.<id>]`, is what the session starts on, as a profile's skill starts `+chaos` (§4.19): `:<id> task` hands the skill the task, whose words name the session, and `:<id>` alone starts the skill by itself, named by the word's `title`, which the Plugins menu and Settings offer as Start <title>…. `dir`, a folder of yours, is where its sessions run, in place, as `:markets` runs in the map's folder, and then the task takes no `@repo`; without one, a task's `@repo` places it, else it runs in a scratch folder, and the task bar's own folder never does, since the session is for the plugin's app. A task takes `+agent` words and the menus' agent, model and tutor mode as any task does, and is never a follow-up to the task before. A word may not take the name of one of Irie's own commands, and of two plugins with one word the first, by its file's name, keeps it. While its plugin is off, or its table doesn't read, typing it says why, and an unknown command's answer lists the words that work. A word runs nothing until you type it, so it keeps no plugin off by itself.
- A skill's words and a server's tools go to every agent, so a manifest with either is off until you turn its plugin on, as one that runs a helper is: the plugin's summary says what waits for the switch, in one sentence.
- **A web pane loads only its origin:** 127.0.0.1, localhost or ::1, or with `remote = true` an https address off the computer. Its web view keeps its plugin's own website data (`WKWebsiteDataStore(forIdentifier:)`, from a name-based UUID of the plugin's name), so logins last and no other plugin's pane, Dock control or window sees them; a content rule list blocks every load from elsewhere, the navigation delegate refuses every page from elsewhere, a link out opens in the browser only from a link the person clicked, as in the Markets window, and the page has no bridge to Irie. Every frame starts without WebRTC, which a content rule list doesn't govern, and the camera and microphone are denied. A page that opens more than three dialogs in ten seconds gets no more until it loads again, and one whose web process stops twice in a minute waits for Reload. It keeps its page while other tabs show. When nothing answers it says the app isn't running and shows the `start` words, and tries again every 3 s while it shows. The part's check knocks once on an address on this computer, a TCP connect and nothing sent, so Settings and the menu say so too, and never knocks on a remote one. What the page can't show, such as an export, goes to Downloads; its alerts, confirms and file fields work as sheets.
- **A bundle** is a manifest with a `plugins` list: plugins, or parts as `PLUGIN.PART`, one a line, that go on and off together. Its switch sets each one's own switch and holds none (the `plugins` op with `bundle` and `on`; `irie plugins on|off BUNDLE`): a plugin by its switch, a part by its own, taking its plugin on with it; a part with no switch of its own goes on with its plugin and stays while the plugin does. It reads on while all of them are, off while none is, and some between, and lists each with how it stands, missing when it isn't here.
- **On the phone** ([PHONE.md](PHONE.md) §2.2): `remote.panes` in config.toml names web panes on this computer that phones reach, each over HTTPS at a port of its own, as `"invoices.invoices" = 7390`. The phone app's gateway serves that port with the app's own certificates and passes each request on to the pane's app while the core's `plugins` op says the pane is on, as the app's own pages would send it, so an app that listens only on 127.0.0.1 goes on a phone's Home Screen unchanged. The setting is config.toml's, never a manifest's, so no manifest brings a pane to the phone by itself; `irie remote panes` prints each pane's addresses.

**Later.** Plugins from outside Irie, each in a repository of its own, pinned to a commit and checked by hash as M12 does for skills, holding what needs no code of Irie's: skills, MCP servers and set-up steps. A plugin's parts with code of their own come in as Irie's do, through a pull request. Until then the plugins folder holds your own manifests, written by hand, and a manifest's skills and tools are the folder and program it names on this computer, as they are when its sessions start.

**Tests:** `internal/plugins`: the defaults (the plugin on, its parts off, the guide ready, Bitwarden held), parts on with nothing installed saying what they lack and what fixes it, then ready once their programs are there, the VPN's state from the core, the switch's date, the plugin off turning every part off while their own switches keep their values, the reasons a word gives, the guide's skill left out with its reason, each switch's key and value, the text, and the markets plugin off by default, then the map lacking a folder, the folder and its page in turn, ready with the page's change, and `~` in its folder; a manifest's plugin with its web pane, ready once something answers and saying what starts it until then, addresses on and off the computer and never a knock on a remote one, its switch, manifests that don't read and why, schedules held, a manifest's skill and tools off until its switch is on and ready after, sessions' skills with yours after Irie's own, a profile with no skills and `skills.disabled` leaving yours out, tables and folders that can't be a skill or a server and why, the first plugin keeping a name, a word with its skill and folder, ready, off with its plugin and saying why, refused for one of Irie's commands, another plugin's skill or a relative folder, the one sentence of what waits for the switch, and a bundle turning its plugins on and off, reading on, off and some, naming what isn't here, and taking a part's plugin on with it. The Bitwarden plugin: on by default, its fill needing `bw`, then its sign-in, and ready once both are there, a data file of a shape it doesn't know read as signed in, and Bitwarden for agents held whatever the switch says. `internal/config`: `plugins.manifests`. `internal/skills`: a skill of yours read whole, without hidden files, and refused for a link inside, a missing SKILL.md, frontmatter or description, another name, too many files or too much. `internal/core`: your skill in a session's sealed folder while its plugin is on, a change to it in a new folder only, your tools among a session's servers after Irie's own, a session started with both, and a typed word starting a session on your skill, with a task and alone, the `plugins` op, `:plugins`, a part turned on through the op written to config.toml, the plugin off through it, the VPN's op and mark with the plugin off, `+anon` refused with the plugin off, the `markets` op's answers and refusals, a `:markets` task run in the map's folder itself, as a step of the history, and a manifest's plugin and a bundle through the op. `cmd/irie`: `irie plugins` with a bundle. `core`: the new words, and a plugin's word: its task on the skill, with the menus but never their folder, in its folder in place, alone, off and saying why, refused with no task when it has no skill, and named in the list of commands. The Variorum tab's: `internal/variorum` parses both setups and refuses other shapes without ever quoting a key, checks the door's program, keeps the setups 0600, and calls a stand-in for the door, the test binary in a fake Variorum.app: two pages of a read, the door's refusals and `isError` word for word, a door that never answers and one that ends mid-answer, and `irie variorum door` passing on only the key, a home and a plain PATH; `internal/plugins` the part's check, its switch and a bundle taking it off; `internal/core` the `variorum` op, off with the tab, the note from a session's hooks, and Variorum's door in a `:write` session and in no other, with the tab off too; `cmd/irie` the setups kept with the tab off. The phone's panes': `internal/remote`: a pane's requests reaching its app as its own pages' would (the Host, an Origin or a Referer of the phone's address and none other, a redirect to the app's own address, the gateway's X-Forwarded headers and never the phone's, events as they come), nothing passed on while the pane is off, gone, elsewhere or not a page, or to a request from beyond the local network, and the core asked about the panes now and then; `internal/config`: `remote.panes` and the ports it may not take; `cmd/irie`: `irie remote panes`. The browser extension's: `internal/terms` reads a page of terms whole (its kind, date, company, length and reader, each point's verdict, words, clause, heading and link, against you first), each kind of clause by itself and the clauses that only look like one (the person's own right to close an account, a prohibition on selling users' data, a warranty that is no warrant, the Data Privacy Framework's arbitration, a privacy policy's minors), headings in each shape, sentences past abbreviations, a page in several languages and one in another, a page built by scripts, text fragments, sites and places, the grade, questions answered from the points and from the clauses' words, the passages a model answers from, a fake Ollama's models and its streamed answer, and the fetcher's links: a page's terms found through its links, the passages it offers when it finds none, a PDF refused, Latin-1 read, every local, private and reserved address refused, this computer's own networks and NAT64's and 6to4's too, unless the person asked, and then only for the host they typed, a privacy policy asked of terms that link to none, a page's hidden characters, and a model's reply cut short; `internal/extension`: its ID from its key, the files it writes and that none loads from elsewhere, the folder kept current, setup's host files for Chrome and Firefox with the program quoted, `Look` and `Remove`, framing both ways, and the host answering hello, a page, questions with and without a model, an update only when asked, the links that may be terms as the panel reads them, and its refusals, a request with a field of the wrong type naming itself, over pipes as a browser would; where a model runs; `internal/plugins` the parts' states step by step and the Terms tab's pane, ready once its page answers; `internal/core` serving the page while the plugin is on, gone when it is off and back when it is on again; `cmd/irie`: `irie terms` on a file, as JSON, a question and standard input, and off with its plugin. Yoink's: `internal/yoink`, with a stand-in for yt-dlp in testdata that answers by the link's words with no network: links taken and refused, the choices and their sizes (an upright video's too), a playlist refused, yt-dlp's errors in sentences, a site's sign-in among them, the progress lines yt-dlp 2026.10.26 printed for a video and its sound, a download into the folder with its parts and logs gone after, the sound alone, a failure with its reason, a cancel that ends the process group and removes the parts, a link holding a shell's words reaching yt-dlp whole and running nothing, three at once and a fourth refused, downloads that outlive their core and one that ended while no core watched, and the engine found or lacking; `internal/plugins` off by default, then needing uvx and ffmpeg in turn, ready, Deno, and `yoink.ytdlp`; `internal/core` the op's looks, starts, cancels and clears, `:yoink` typed, and every call from an agent refused, typed words too; `cmd/irie`: `irie yoink` against a stand-in core, its steps on standard error and the file's path on standard output, a failure, the formats and the list; `core`: `:yoink`'s words. Chat's: `internal/chat`, against stand-ins for Discord's and Slack's APIs that answer as their documentation says, never the services themselves: signing in, a stale token and a person's refused in words, the intent's flags; a server's channels in Discord's order with what the bot may do in each from its roles and overwrites, a hidden one skipped; messages with people, roles, channels and emoji by name, hidden text said once; posts, replies, threads, announcement threads and forum posts, edits and deletes, each with the pings it may send; 429s waited out, one too long refused and its route held; the agents' rules: a post a draft that outlives a restart and only the person sends, another agent seeing none of it, withdrawing one's own, everyone, here and roles refused, posts of their own only where opened, once a minute and the hour's count from the log, edits of one's own posts only, reads only where let, topics kept from agents, DMs always drafts, `chat.agents` off and an account closed; adding an account only once its token signs in, the same bot again, removing it with its tokens and drafts; the token stores, the Keychain in a temporary keychain of its own; Slack's tokens each of its kind, a person's refused, both pages of its channels, the bot's first and the DMs last, messages with people, channels and links in words, a thread's, the pings each kind of post keeps, a GET never with a body, a missing scope by name, a 429 waited out, the manifest and its link, and the agents' rules on Slack. `internal/core`: the op, `:chat`, an agent's draft sent only by the person, the plugin's parts, an agent refused with `chat.agents` off and the plugin off. `internal/plugins`: the parts' states, and the skill left out without an account or with `chat.agents` off. `internal/config`: the settings checked. `cmd/irie`: `irie chat` against a stand-in core. Obsidian's: `internal/obsidian`: a note's tasks found at the top level, outside its properties, fences of code and comments, with their indented lines, ticks of any mark and block identifiers, Irie's own or the person's; line breaks, a byte order mark and a last line without a break kept; the notes and the note kept in step both ways, the person's heading and lines where they wrote them; a line deleted going to Recently Deleted, a note emptied, as by a sync, held and written back, three gone and nine of twelve deleted; the first merge taking the person's tasks in their place; the person's own block identifier kept and found; a line being typed one note; a deleted note brought back by an edit in the note; a copied line a note of its own; 400 random notepads rendered and read back as they were; and 300 rounds of random edits on both sides losing none of the person's lines, making a note of every task and settling in one sync; `internal/plugins`: on by default and waiting for a vault, the notes' switch, a vault that isn't a folder, ready, and opened by Obsidian; `internal/config`: `obsidian.vault`, `obsidian.notes` and the paths `obsidian.note` may take; `internal/core`: the notepad kept in a vault, Obsidian's tick, a new line and the person's heading taken, the note not written while just changed, the notepad's changes going to it, a line deleted, the note gone and written again, a restart that still knows a delete, and the `obsidian` op; five notes gone at once kept and written back; and the notepad kept in Irie alone with the plugin, the part or the vault off, a vault that isn't there said, and a newer core's notepad going to no note. Jammin's: `internal/jammin`, against stand-ins for Spotify's Web API and its accounts service that answer as its documentation says, never Spotify itself: the sign-in's PKCE words, its loopback redirect with a port, a wrong state and another Host changing nothing, a refusal on Spotify's page said, the code traded with the verifier and the refresh token kept, refreshed and replaced; what plays, nothing, an episode and an ad, each control's request, the cadence and a control's change shown at once, Premium required, no active device, the quota and 429s in words; playlists, a search and devices in Spotify's 2026 shapes, and this computer's device; the Music app against a stand-in for `osascript`: no script while it isn't running, the look in milliseconds, artwork written and the newest 20 kept, permission refused in words, each control with its words as arguments, and each name the scripts use in the Music app's dictionary; one source at a time, another paused that plays here and one on a phone left playing; the token stores, the Keychain in a temporary keychain of its own. `internal/core`: the op, `:jammin`, the plugin's parts from the core's last look, every call from an agent refused, typed words too, and the plugin off. `internal/plugins`: Jammin on by default with its tab, Spotify needing an app, then ready, and Apple Music where the Music app is. `internal/config`: the settings checked. `core`: `:jammin`'s words. `cmd/irie`: `irie jammin` against a stand-in core, its controls, a move by a device's name, the lists, and a sign-in that asks for the Client ID, opens the page and waits.

### 4.18 VPNs

The owner asked on 2026-11-14 for VPNs other than Mullvad, chosen in the config, and for VPN control to stay in a company's copy (§2.10), whose people have to use the VPN their company approves. The VPN plugin follows one VPN, the one `vpn.provider` names, through that VPN's own command line, and shows it as it showed Mullvad VPN: the header's mark in every client, `:vpn`, the Plugins menu's VPN items, and the phone apps' Connect and Disconnect.

**Kinds.** `internal/vpn` holds a kind for each VPN it knows. A kind gives its name and title, where its app comes from, what `vpn.server` names for it, the systems it runs on, how to find its command, and how to make a `vpn.VPN`, which reads the state and connects and disconnects. A VPN may do more, and the clients offer only what it does. A `Placer` takes places, as Mullvad's relays, Tailscale's exit nodes and NordVPN's countries are. A `Lockdowner` has a mode that blocks the network while it is off, and a `Timer` an account whose time runs out; Mullvad's has both. A `Listener` says each change itself, as Mullvad's `status listen` does. The core asks every other VPN its state every 10 seconds, and again right after each change; each read is numbered as it starts, so a read begun before a change can't undo the change's own. The header carries the VPN's kind, its title and what it can do (`can`).

| `vpn.provider` | The VPN | Its command | The state from | `vpn.server` | Runs on | Beyond on and off |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `mullvad` | Mullvad VPN | `mullvad` (`vpn.mullvad`) | `status --json listen`, which says each change | none | macOS, Linux | place (a relay), lockdown, time |
| `tailscale` | Tailscale | `tailscale`, or the Mac app's own binary | `status --json`'s `BackendState` | none | macOS, Linux | place (an exit node; `none` goes out directly) |
| `netbird` | NetBird | `netbird` | `status --json`'s `daemonStatus` | none | macOS, Linux | none |
| `warp` | Cloudflare WARP (Cloudflare One) | `warp-cli` | `warp-cli status` | none | macOS, Linux | none |
| `nordvpn` | NordVPN | `nordvpn` | `nordvpn status` | none | Linux | place (a country, a city or a server) |
| `cisco` | Cisco Secure Client, once AnyConnect | `/opt/cisco/secureclient/bin/vpn`, or AnyConnect 4's | `vpn state` | its host, or an entry of its profile | macOS, Linux | none |
| `globalprotect` | GlobalProtect | `globalprotect` | `globalprotect show --status` | its portal | Linux | none |
| `forticlient` | FortiClient | `forticlient vpn`, or 7.2's `fortivpn` | `status` | the connection's name | Linux | none |
| `system` | the system's own VPNs | `scutil --nc` (macOS), `nmcli` (Linux) | the VPN's status, or NetworkManager's active connections | the VPN's name | macOS, Linux | none |
| `command` | any other | the person's own | `vpn.status`'s output, against `vpn.connected` and `vpn.connecting` | shown while it is up | any | none |

The state is one of connected, connecting, disconnected, disconnecting and error, with where the tunnel goes while it is up, as the VPN names it: a relay, a gateway, a portal, an exit node or a network. Any other state is an error that says what the VPN said. A VPN that is off for a reason, such as a sign-in it waits for, says the reason after "VPN off".

**A company's VPN.** The brand file names the VPNs a copy offers (`brand.VPNs`, §2.10), the first `vpn.provider`'s default, and what they connect to (`brand.VPNServer`). The config refuses any other provider, and so do `:vpn use` and `irie setup vpn`; the plugin's summary and the setup name only those. A copy that names its VPNs shows the VPN from the start: `vpn.on` defaults to on. A company keeps the VPN plugin when it leaves the others out (`Tags = "noprivacy,nowriting,nomarkets,nomail,noterms,noextension,noyoink,nojammin"`), and `novpn` leaves the VPN out too. A VPN that Irie doesn't know by name works through the command kind, with the commands the company names: one prints the state, which a pattern recognises, and one each connects and disconnects. Each is a program and its arguments, run without a shell.

**Signing in.** Irie never takes a password, a token or an account's number, and gives a command nothing to answer a prompt with. `:vpn on` runs the VPN's own connect, and a VPN that needs a sign-in asks for it in its own window or browser page, or its command fails and says why. Tailscale signed out or waiting for its admin's approval, and NetBird signed out, are told so, rather than left waiting on a page no one sees. Cisco's command line can't connect while Cisco's own app holds the connection, which on a Mac it usually does, nor with single sign-on, which Cisco supports only in its app; then Irie says to connect in the app, and never opens it itself (§4.11, *Out of sight*). Cloudflare WARP may first want its terms accepted: Irie never accepts them for anyone (warp-cli's `--accept-tos`), and says how. A company may lock WARP's switch, or GlobalProtect may want a reason before it disconnects, and the VPN's own words say so.

**Where each runs.** NordVPN, GlobalProtect and FortiClient have command lines only on Linux. GlobalProtect's Mac app keeps a VPN in System Settings that reads disconnected even while it is up, so the system kind can't follow it either; on a Mac it takes commands of the company's own that say its state, if it has any. A kind that isn't for the computer's system says so, and `:vpn use` and the setup refuse it.

**Privacy.** As with Mullvad (extensions.md §4.3), Irie keeps only the state and where the tunnel goes, never the machine's own address or place, and never logs a VPN's output. For each VPN it reads only the parts that carry those: Tailscale's backend state and exit node, and not its addresses or the tailnet's name, which may be a person's email or user name; NetBird's daemon state, and not its address, name or endpoints; NordVPN's state, server, country and city, and not its `IP:` line; FortiClient's state and connection, and not its user or address; scutil's first line, and not the extended status after it, which holds the tunnel's addresses; nmcli's names, types and states, which is all Irie asks it for. It never runs the commands that print addresses: Cisco's `vpn stats`, GlobalProtect's `show --details` and `show --host-state`. Tailscale's and NetBird's JSON is read from stdout alone, past any warning before it. An error carries the last three lines a command printed, never a line of JSON, which can hold every address the VPN knows, nor a sign-in prompt, which can hold the user it remembers ("it asked for a sign-in" stands in for one).

**Who changes it.** The person, from any client. The `vpn` op refuses a change, `:vpn use` included, from inside an agent's process tree (extensions.md §4.4). The `config` op doesn't yet check who calls it (company-copies.md, R14), and the command kind's commands are programs the core runs, which makes that check more pressing.

**Choosing.** `irie setup vpn [VPN]`, or `:setup vpn` in a task bar, which runs it in a tool session. It lists the VPNs this copy offers, with ● where the command is installed, and offers the one named, else the configured one if it is installed, else the first installed one. It asks for `vpn.server` where the VPN takes one, listing the system's own VPNs for the system kind (`scutil --nc list`, or nmcli's VPN and WireGuard connections), then turns the plugin and `vpn.on` on. `:vpn use NAME` does the same in a word: it writes `vpn.provider`, turns VPN control on, and the core follows the new VPN at once. Another VPN than before leaves the last one's `vpn.cli` and `vpn.server` behind, back at their defaults. A change to any VPN setting follows the VPN afresh. A config.toml from before the VPN plugin that turned the privacy plugin off, and says nothing of `plugins.vpn`, keeps the VPN off, as it was. The custom VPN needs its `vpn.connected` pattern to match only the connected state's own words: `"connected"` alone also matches Disconnected.

**Tests:** `internal/vpn`: the kinds are the config's, in order, and a copy names only kinds; the words for any VPN; opening one, with what each lacks; a missing command that names where its app comes from; the command kind against scripts, its patterns, an exit code that doesn't decide the state, and an error with only its last lines; scutil's states and list, with its addresses left out; nmcli's escapes, states and list; Tailscale's JSON, a warning before it, the exit node, the tailnet's name left out, every backend state, and its commands, including a signed-out connect; NetBird's states, as JSON and as text, an older NetBird's link, its addresses left out of the state and of an error, and a signed-out connect; an error's lines without JSON, prompts or long lines; WARP's states, old and new, a locked switch, terms it wants accepted and never `--accept-tos`; NordVPN 6's and 5's status, a pause, its address left out, and its places; Cisco's state lines, Unknown before the real state, the person sent to its app when its command line can't connect, and never `stats`; GlobalProtect's states, `disabled` and its portal; FortiClient's states, its user and address left out, and both its commands. `internal/config`: `vpn.provider` and its keys, the VPNs a copy offers, and the VPN kept off for a file that turned the privacy plugin off. `internal/plugins`: the VPN plugin on with its part off, its title from the provider, what each VPN lacks, the plugin's switch apart from privacy's, turning it on whole, and its settings with `~`. `internal/core`: Mullvad as before, a VPN asked every few seconds, its words, the words it lacks refused, `:vpn use` written to config.toml, the last VPN's server left behind, followed at once, and refused from an agent, and an older read never replacing a newer. The VPN's tests pass in a copy whose brand names its VPNs, and under the race detector. `core`: `:vpn use` and `:setup vpn`.

### 4.19 The chaos monkey

The owner asked on 2026-11-14 for a chaos monkey agent, to stress test products and features, whatever they are pointed at. It is one of Irie's skills, `chaos`, run as `/irie:chaos` (internal/skills/bundled/chaos), and `+chaos`, a built-in profile whose sessions start on it, in any repository: `+chaos @shop break the refund flow`.

- **What it does.** The skill makes the agent a chaos monkey for the task: it learns what the target must keep doing, its steady state, measured from outside, then breaks its inputs, timing, processes, disk, network and load on purpose, one fault at a time, the likeliest and most harmful first, and reports each failure with its severity, the shortest steps that replay it and their seed, and the likely cause. It fixes nothing unless the task asks. Its catalogue of faults (`reference/faults.md`) says how to cause each on macOS and Linux, such as a full disk from a small disk image that needs no administrator, or a process stopped at moments the seed picks.
- **Safely.** It attacks only a copy it owns for the run, such as a dev server it starts or a scratch folder, and never production, the person's live data or the copy they use, anything shared, other people's systems or any third party's service; a site on a shared host is tested as a local build. No real money, messages or personal data: test modes, fakes and example.com. It asks first, with the exact commands, when a test can only reach something live, shared, paid or beyond the machine, and skips it when nobody answers. Every fault has an end and an undo, every run is put back, no window it opens lands where the person works (§4.11, *Out of sight*), and Mikael's door, auto mode and Gabriel's word hold as for any session. Against Irie itself it never touches the running Irie, and writes its experiments as Go tests on a test Irie, as `TestIrieKeepsItsOwnProcessesRunning` does (§2.9, `reference/irie.md`).
- **On call.** Every Claude Code session has the skill, as it has Irie's others (§3.3), so any agent can run it on what it just built, itself or in a subagent, when asked to stress test, chaos test, fuzz, load test or try to break something.
- **A profile that starts on a skill.** `+chaos` is Opus 5.5 at high effort in auto mode with `skill = "chaos"`. Any Claude Code profile may name a skill of Irie's (`profiles.<name>.skill`): its sessions start with `/irie:<skill>` and then the task, which Claude Code hands the skill, and the task's own words name the session. A spawn on such a profile checks first that the skill is there and on and that the profile's Claude Code loads Irie's plugin, and says why not (`skills.enabled`, `skills.disabled`, a profile with `skills = false`, an old Claude Code), as a guide does; the + list says what the profile runs. A task that already runs the skill, as one carried on from such a session, is kept as it is.

**Why a skill and not an agent of its own.** Claude Code's `--agent` replaces its own system prompt with the agent's, and a chaos monkey runs commands, servers and browsers all day, for which that prompt is written; a skill keeps it, comes to every session, and any agent can use it.

*Not yet:* tools of Irie's own for faults that the agent now makes itself, such as a proxy that delays, drops or cuts connections from a seed; a night shift of chaos runs on what landed that day.

**Tests:** the skill is bundled, on call rather than a guide, and points to its references; `+chaos` is a built-in profile; `profiles.<name>.skill` must name a skill, on a Claude Code profile that takes Irie's skills; the task goes to the skill once, however often it is carried on; a spawn says why the skill can't run before anything is made; with the fake agent, `+chaos` typed as a task starts a session on `/irie:chaos`, named by the task, with the skill and its references in Irie's plugin folder; the + list says what it runs.

### 4.20 The notepad

Notes a person keeps for later and doesn't want to lose, though they don't mean to do them now: a checklist, as in Notes, that a sidebar shows between the selected session's details and the task bar. Each note is a line to tick off. When the time comes, one goes to the task bar as if it were typed there and sent.

**What a person does.**
- Whatever is typed in the notepad's last line becomes a note. Return makes it and leaves the last line ready for the next; Return in a note makes an empty one under it; ⇧Return breaks a line inside one; Backspace in an empty note takes it away. A paste of several lines makes a note of each line pasted, after a note of what was typed there before. Esc or a click elsewhere leaves what is typed in the last line there, kept, never a half-written note.
- The circle before a note ticks it off, or on again (⇧⌘U, Notes' own key). Ticked notes stay where they are: only the person moves a note, by its menu or ⌃⌘↑ and ⌃⌘↓, as only the person moves a card (§4.1).
- The arrow beside a note, ⌘Return or Send as a Jot in its menu sends it to the task bar: the client calls `input` with the note's text exactly as the task bar would, the menus' repo and the selected session included, so its `@`, `+` and `:` words work, and a note that reads as more for the jot just sent goes to that jot's agent (§4.2). `input` names the note (`note`), and once the input went through, whatever it did but ask, the core ticks the note off and keeps when it went and the session it started or went to. A note the core never had, as one sent before its add came, is taken in from the text that went, and the add, when it comes, leaves it be. A task placed on a paired machine takes no note there: the notes are this machine's. The note stays, with a paper plane that says when it went and shows that session.
- A note deleted waits in Recently Deleted for 30 days, the newest 500; a notice offers Undo, and the notepad's menu brings any of them back, each to where it was. Delete Done Notes clears the ticked ones the same way. A note emptied and then deleted keeps the words it had there; only a line never written in leaves nothing.
- The title folds the notepad to one line: the count of notes to do and the next one.

**Keeping them.** The notes are the person's own words, so nothing is to lose them:
- The core owns them (§2.1), in `notepad.json` in Irie's data folder (§5.4), and writes every change before the op answers, as a transaction: the file holds the notes before the change or after it, never part of either, whatever stops the core, and an op whose write fails changes nothing and says so.
- The first change of each day first copies the file as it stood to `backups/notepad/<day>.json` beside it; the newest 30 copies stay.
- A file that does not read is never written over: the core moves it aside to `notepad.json.bad-<time>`, takes the notes from the newest copy that reads, writes them back, and the notepad's `problem` says so. A file the core can't read at all, as without permission, is shown as a problem and left alone.
- A file of a newer version, as after `tools/live.sh --rollback`, is shown and never written (`read_only`), so the newer core's notes stay whole. A client keeps its changes meanwhile and sends them once a core that can take them is back.
- Each change is the same however often it is sent (§2.4, `notepad`): an add names its note by the client's own id, an edit sets the text, a check sets the tick, a delete or a restore of one done already does nothing. So a client keeps each change it has not had an answer to, the Mac app in its defaults the moment it is made, and sends it again once the core is back: a note typed while an install restarts the core, or just before the app is stopped, goes in when the core answers. An edit of a note deleted meanwhile brings it back with the text, and one the core never had adds it. The app also keeps a copy of the notepad, so the notes show while the core is away, and what is typed in the last line before it is a note stays in its defaults as it is typed, as the task bar's draft does.
- `rev` counts the file's changes. A reply and a frame come on connections of their own, so a client keeps the notepad with the highest `rev` it has heard; only a new subscription's first frame may go back, as after a core restored a copy.

**In the vault.** While the obsidian plugin's Notes in the vault is on and a vault is chosen (§4.17), the notepad is kept as a note in the vault too, `Irie Notes.md` unless `obsidian.note` says otherwise, a task a note, both ways: a tick, a new line or new words there, in Obsidian, on a phone through the vault's own sync or on the Vault card, come to the notepad as its own changes, and the notepad's go there. The notepad stays the copy kept whole, so nothing above weakens: what the note asks of the notepad is the notepad's own ops, written as transactions and kept by the day's copy and Recently Deleted; a note gone from the vault deletes nothing; notes gone from it at once are held and written back; and a notepad shown only goes to no note. The notepad's frame names the note (`vault`), so the Notes panel marks it kept there, says what keeps them apart under its title, and its ⤢ opens the note on a Vault card, a full pane beside the terminal.

**Not yet.** The terminal sidebar and the phone apps don't show the notepad, though the op and the frame are theirs to use; a phone shows it through the vault's own sync and Obsidian. Notes have no due dates or reminders, and none of an agent's own.

**Tests:** `internal/core/notepad_test.go`: notes kept across a restart, in their order, with the file 0600 under the data folder; changes sent twice changing once and writing nothing the second time; deleted notes, alone or together, going back where they were; an edit keeping what was typed against a delete and a lost add; a file cut short coming back from the day's copy and kept aside; a newer version's file shown and never written; 30 copies and 30 days of Recently Deleted; frames after the snapshot and each change; and a note sent through `input` starting its task, ticked off with that session, kept through a core restart. The vault's: `internal/core/obsidian_test.go` and `internal/obsidian` (§4.17).

### 4.21 Agent locks

Irie can say when an agent it runs is set up in a way known to be vulnerable, and what to update ([agent-locks](proposals/agent-locks.md)). The idea comes from a talk-to-camera video by lizthedeveloper (2026-09-19): CVEs cover packages, but nothing records an agent's whole setup to check, so Irie records each one as a lock file records exact versions, and checks it.

**A lock per session.** As each session's agent starts, at a spawn, a resume, a fork or a reopen, the core writes its lock to `sessions/<id>/lock.json` (`internal/lock`), and a reopened session gets a fresh one. It is written beside the launch, never holding it up, from what the launch knew and what the agent reads as it starts:
- `irie`: Irie's version and the SHA-256 of the irie binary, read once for each size and time of change it has.
- `harness`: the adapter's id, its version as the probe read it (`claude --version`, cached per binary, §3.3), its binary with any link followed (a native install's versioned file), and for Claude Code the npm package `@anthropic-ai/claude-code` at that version, whose versions the native builds share.
- `profile`, `model`, and `permission_mode` as the command line gave it (`bypassPermissions` for `--dangerously-skip-permissions`; empty for the CLI's own default).
- `mcp`: Irie's own servers for the session (§4.15, §4.16), which carry Irie's version, then the user's own from Claude Code's config, each name once as Claude Code ranks the scopes: the folder's own (`local`, in `~/.claude.json` under the folder's path), the project's `.mcp.json`, then those for every folder (`user`, in `~/.claude.json`; with `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, `.claude.json` in that folder). An anonymous session loads Irie's alone. Each server's package comes from its command: `npx -y @scope/pkg@1.2.3` and `npm exec` are npm's, `uvx pkg==1.2`, `uvx pkg@1.2` and `uvx --from pkg==1.2 cmd` PyPI's; a package with no exact version, as `npx pkg` or `pkg@latest`, runs whatever is newest and has none. A server's environment and headers are never read. Of another's command than Irie's own only the program and the package are kept, with the SHA-256 of the whole command, and of an address only its scheme and host: the rest may hold a secret.
- `plugins`: Irie's plugins that are on (§4.17), which are code in the irie binary and carry its version and hash, and your own, with the hash of their manifest.
- `skills`: Irie's skills the session gets, from its plugin folder, each as `/irie:<name>` with the hash of its files.
- `hooks`: Irie's own, the events of its settings file with the hash of its table, then each of the user's own (`~/.claude/settings.json`) and the project's (`.claude/settings.json`): its event and matcher, its program, and the hash of its whole command.
- `notes`: what it could not read, and why.

**`irie lock [SESSION] [--json]`** (op `lock`) prints the locks of the open sessions, or of one by its id or name, or a closed one by its id, a line a part, each package with what the last check found for it; a peer's session has its lock on its own machine, so only the packages its row names. A session started before Irie wrote locks has none until it is reopened, resumed or forked.

**`irie lock --check`** asks OSV.dev's batch API (`POST /v1/querybatch`) about every harness and package version the open sessions' locks name, and the peers' rows, then reads each advisory it lists once (`GET /v1/vulns/{id}`), and prints each that affects one: its id, its summary and the first version that fixes it, with the sessions that run it. A query sends a package's ecosystem, name and version, and the reads an advisory's id: nothing of the sessions, the machine or the user. An advisory known from the last check whose modified time OSV gives again is not read again. It runs only when asked: `lock.check = "daily"` lets the core run it once a day, and the default is off, since OSV.dev is a Google service; `lock.osv` names the API, such as a mirror of your own. What a check found is kept in `lock-check.json` in the state folder, each version for 30 days after a check last asked about it, so the marks outlast a restart.

**The mark.** A row carries its lock's packages (`lock`), each with what the last check found: whether it was checked, how many advisories affect it, how many no version fixes yet, and the version that fixes the rest. While any does, its `words` say so in the meter's calm style, the part and its version first: "Claude Code 2.1.250: 1 advisory, fixed in 2.1.251", "MCP server github 0.6.2: 2 advisories, no fix yet". The Mac app's card and the phone apps show them under the detail, in muted ink with no colour of their own; `irie ctl list` after the detail, and `irie ctl ps` counts them in its ADV column. A peer's rows come with their own core's marks (§2.8), and this core marks the versions its own check knows too, so an unattended machine's sessions are checked from here.

**Not yet.** The skills in the user's own skills folders and the project's, the local settings file's hooks, the user's installed Claude Code plugins and what they bring, managed settings, and a profile's own `--mcp-config` files are not in the lock. A shared registry of vulnerable setups, the other half of the idea, needs many people's locks, which Irie could feed one day only by an explicit export; a model's own known weaknesses have no public feed to check.

**Tests:** `internal/lock`: a lock for a fake harness and fake MCP servers in each scope, with none of their secrets in the file; the packages of npx's and uvx's command lines; versions ordered as npm and PyPI order them; a check against an `httptest` stand-in for OSV that has an advisory for one version and none for the next, sending names and versions alone, reading no advisory twice, and saying so when OSV doesn't answer; the card's words; results kept a month. `internal/core`: a spawn of the fake agent writing its lock, with a fake MCP server of its config, and a check marking the row of the version with the advisory and not the next; marks after a restart and on a peer's rows; the daily check once a day and never while off. No test reaches the network.

---

## 5. Data and config

### 5.1 Event envelope

One JSON object per journal line and per `event` frame:

| Field | Type | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `v` | int | Envelope version, `1` |
| `seq` | uint64 | Assigned by the core; strictly increasing within a journal |
| `ts` | string | RFC 3339 UTC with milliseconds |
| `session` | string | Irie session id: `s` plus 12 base32 characters, sortable by time |
| `agent` | string | `main` or `sub:<vendor agent id>`; empty for session-wide events |
| `source` | enum | `hook`, `host`, `tmux`, `transcript`, `irie`, `user` |
| `harness` | string | `claude-code`, `generic`, … |
| `kind` | enum | See §5.2 |
| `vendor` | object | The harness's own ids, verbatim: `session_id`, `prompt_id`, `tool_use_id`, `agent_id`, `agent_type`, `hook_event` |
| `data` | object | Kind-specific; every string capped at 2 KiB. A hook event the shim kept while no core listened has `late` too, and `ts` is when it came (§3.3) |

```json
{"v":1,"seq":4182,"ts":"2026-10-26T14:02:11.408Z","session":"s0k3j9x2m4a7q","agent":"sub:a1b2c3","source":"hook","harness":"claude-code","kind":"tool.started","vendor":{"session_id":"6f1c…","tool_use_id":"toolu_01…","agent_id":"a1b2c3","agent_type":"Explore","hook_event":"PreToolUse"},"data":{"tool":"Bash","summary":"go test ./..."}}
```

### 5.2 Event kinds

| Kind | Data | From | Nearest standard term (ACP / OSC 7501) |
|---|---|---|---|
| `spawn.requested` | `task_bytes`, `cwd`, `repo`, `worktree`, `branch`, `profile`; for a move (§4.6) `resume` (the vendor id) and `kind`, and no `task_bytes`; from M10 `class`, `task_hash`, and `routed`, `route`, `parent`, `continues` when they apply (§6.4) | irie | none |
| `spawn.failed` | `reason`, `exit`, `tail` | irie, host | error |
| `session.started` | `transcript`, `model`, `permission_mode` | SessionStart | `session/new` |
| `prompt.submitted` | `chars` | UserPromptSubmit | user message / working |
| `tool.started` | `tool`, `summary`, `phrase` (the call in plain words: "Running the tests") | PreToolUse | `tool_call` pending / working |
| `tool.finished`, `tool.failed` | `tool`, `ms`; for a shell command that runs tests or checks, `tests` (`passed`, `failed` or `ran`) and `test_line`, the line that shows a failure (§4.8) | PostToolUse, PostToolUseFailure | `tool_call_update` completed / failed |
| `input.needed` | `reason`: `permission`, `question`, `plan`, `elicitation`, `stalled`, `turn`; `phrase` for a permission, a question or a plan ("Asks: may it run rm -rf build?"), and for `turn` what the user is to write ("Your turn: …"). `turn` follows the `turn.ended` of a teach-mode turn whose last message holds a `TODO(human)`, from irie; the agent waits at its prompt, so `send` still takes a message | PermissionRequest, Notification, PreToolUse(AskUserQuestion, ExitPlanMode), Elicitation, readiness timer; a teach-mode Stop | `request_permission` / blocked |
| `turn.ended` | `message`: the turn's last message, cut; whole in `result.md` (M10). `stop_hook_active` when a stop hook sent the turn on before; `waiting`, how many background tasks and wakeups of its own will wake it, and `waiting_on`, the first task's description (§4.8); `subagents`, how many of those tasks are subagents, when Claude Code listed them: a Stop with none ends the helpers still listed (§5.3); `unproven` (`tests`) for a jot whose last message says the tests pass while its latest test run failed (§4.8) | Stop | stop reason `end_turn` / done |
| `turn.failed` | `error`; `details` and `message`, what the API said and the error as the conversation shows it, when Claude Code gives them | StopFailure | error |
| `subagent.started`, `subagent.finished` | `agent_type`, `transcript` | SubagentStart, SubagentStop | none (an ACP RFD covers subagents) |
| `session.ended` | `reason` | SessionEnd | `session/close` |
| `pane.exited` | `code`, `signal` | tmux, host | none |
| `gate.set`, `gate.held`, `gate.released` | `scope`, `tool`, `held_ms` | irie, shim | blocked |
| `freeze.on`, `freeze.off` | `pids`, `frozen_ms`; `by` (`trod`) and `reason` when the trod bot froze or thawed it at the week's cap (§4.10) | user, irie | none |
| `priority.set` | `level`, `reason` | irie | none |
| `note.queued`, `note.delivered` | `note`, `kind` | irie, shim | none |
| `ui.renamed` | `before`, `after`, `before_named`: whether the name before was the user's (§4.2) | irie | none |
| `ui.parked`, `ui.unparked` | none | user | none |
| `ui.pinned`, `ui.unpinned` | none | user | none |
| `ui.sub_stopped` | `agent` (a helper) or `teammate` | user | none |
| `ui.spaced` | `space`, the session's space (`""` for the first) | user; irie for a new session that starts in its opener's space | none |
| `ui.grouped` | `group`, the group of yours the session is in (`""` for none) | user; irie for a new session that starts in its opener's group, or comes back in its group as it reopens | none |
| `ui.undo`, `ui.redo` | `action` (its kind), `label`, `before` and `after` (the session `open`, `closed` or `gone`), `choice` when it answered a question (§4.5) | user | none |
| `ui.closed`, `ui.reopened` | `name`; `ui.closed` also `named` (§4.2), `created`, `vendor_id` and `resume`, which say how reopen brings it back (§4.5), and for a session lost with the tmux server `lost`, with `parked`, `pinned` and `frozen` when it was; and what its next turn will cost in its prompt cache, when Irie knows: its `context` in tokens, `context_usd`, `cache_last` (its last request), `cache_ttl` and `model` (§6.3) | user; irie for a close by `spawn.close_when_done`, or of a lost session | none |
| `outcome.rated` | `verdict`: `good` or `bad` | user (M10, §6.4) | none |
| `outcome.escalated` | `profile`: the more accurate one the task ran on next | irie (M10, §6.4) | none |
| `route.failover` | `to` and `profile` of the session that carries the task on, or `reason` it waits | irie (M10, §6.4) | none |
| `delegate.timed_out` | `wait_ms`, the wait its caller gave up at, and `to_profile`, where the subtask went on | irie (§6.4) | none |
| `route.fallback` | `from` and `to`, the models of a switch at a model's own usage limit, `limit`, what the failed turn said, and `reason` when the session waits for the reset instead | irie (§6.4) | none |
| `model.switched` | `model`, the one it runs from then on; `from`, `requested` and `source` (`command`, `picker`, `sdk`, `auto`, `resume`) | PostModelSwitch | none |
| `dog.nudged` | `dirty`, `ahead`, `land`, `unproven`, `message`: what the jot left, what its record contradicts, and what its agent had said. It stands in for that stop's `turn.ended`, since the turn goes on | irie (§4.8) | none |
| `trod.sent` | `reason`: `question`, `error` (with `error`), `limit` or `loose`; `how`: `stop`, standing in for that stop's `turn.ended` (with `message`), `ask`, standing in for the question's `tool.started` and `input.needed` (with `question`), or `typed`, followed by the message's `prompt.submitted` | irie (§4.10) | none |
| `boost.on`, `boost.off` | `boost.on`: `error`, the API error of the second failed turn in a row, `turns` and `until`; `boost.off`: `why`, `turn` (a turn ended without an API error) or `time` (15 minutes passed) | irie (§4.3) | none |
| `cache.rebuilt` | `at`, the request's time; `message_id`; `name`, the session's then; `tokens` written into the cache again; `usd`, what that cost at list price, for a model with known prices; `idle_ms` since the request before; `ttl`, `1h` or `5m`, the life of the cache it found; `model`; and when Irie knows, `cause`: `you`, `trod`, `dog`, `thaw` (with `by`, `trod` for the trod bot's), `reopen`, `background`, `limit`, `boost`, `tool`, or `changed` for one that was not cold. Agent `sub:<id>` for a helper's | transcript (§6.3) | none |
| `worktree.dropped`, `worktree.restored` | `path`, `repo`, `branch` and `commit`, the branch's tip; `worktree.dropped` also `land`, the branch it was in, and `bytes`, the room it took, written before anything goes, so that a dropped worktree can be put back by hand; `worktree.restored` also `kept`, git's word, when git refused the drop at the last and the worktree stayed | irie (§4.5) | none |

### 5.3 Derived state

Each row shows the first state in this table that applies; the table is in precedence order. Pausing, paused and frozen also show as flags next to the main glyph.

| State | Glyph | Colour | From |
|---|---|---|---|
| failed | `✕` | red | `spawn.failed` |
| exited | `·`, or `✕` if the code is not 0 | dim (red if the code is not 0) | `pane.exited`, `session.ended` |
| frozen | `◆` | cyan | `freeze.on` without a later `freeze.off` |
| paused | `‖` | yellow | `gate.held` |
| needs you | `●` | **red** | `input.needed`, `turn.failed`. Cleared by the next prompt or tool event |
| stalled | `!` | **red** | Readiness timer |
| pausing | `‖` | dim yellow | `gate.set` with no hold yet |
