| working | `◐` | default | `prompt.submitted`, `tool.*`. Once the turn is over, a helper's `tool.*` leaves it over: the session waits on its helpers |
| starting | `◌` | dim | `spawn.requested` |
| waiting | `◉` | the agent's colour | done or idle while its helpers (`subagent.started` with no `subagent.finished` yet) or its teammates (a spinner in their terminal's title) still work: its own turn is over, and it waits on them. The row's state stays `done` or `idle`, with `subs` (`helpers`, `teammates`), and its detail says what it waits on in place of to verify: the work in the background its Stop named, as "Waiting on its work in the background" (§4.8), or else on whom, "Waiting on 3 teammates", after what a jot left. The sidebars and the trod bot count it as working, not to verify, and the status line says `◐ waiting on 3 teammates` (§6.3); `irie ctl delegate` goes on waiting for its result, close-when-done leaves it open, the Mac stays awake for it (§4.12), and undoing its start asks first, since closing it ends them too. A Stop whose `background_tasks` lists no subagent ends every helper still listed, whose `subagent.finished` never came (the core was away, or the turn was cut short) |
| done | `✓` | green | `turn.ended`, not yet viewed; the sidebars and the status line call it *to verify*, since a finished turn is not checked work. Turns into idle when you show the session. `ui.red_on_done = true` shows it red instead. Parked, it is out of the way: the sidebars call it *Parked*, draw its name dim and leave it out of the to verify count |
| idle | `○` | dim | done and viewed |

The sidebar draws these colours from its palette (§5.5, `ui.theme`): red is the palette's needs colour, green its done, yellow its paused and cyan its frozen; default is ink and dim is muted, so a dim red or dim yellow row has a red or yellow glyph and a muted name. In every palette, light and dark, each text colour reads at 4.5:1 and the accent at 3:1 on every surface (WCAG 2.2, 1.4.3 and 1.4.11). The four state colours (red, green, yellow and cyan) also stay apart for colour-blind viewers, as Brettel, Viénot and Mollon's simulation shows them, for protans, deutans and tritans alike; none of them looks the same as ink or muted to anyone, and green never takes the accent's colour. They differ in lightness as well as hue: in light mode needs you is deeper than paused, since a red and an amber of one lightness look the same to a deutan. The sidebar paints every cell's background itself, so this holds whatever the terminal's theme is. It asks the terminal for its background (OSC 11, which tmux answers with the attached client's) and draws the dark set until an answer comes. Each state has its own glyph, so colour is never the only cue (1.4.1): only failed and an exit with an error code share `✕`, and only paused and pausing share `‖`. The core derives frozen (M6), and will derive paused and pausing (M5); the sidebar already draws them. The Mac app draws the same glyphs in the same roles ([MAC.md](MAC.md) §5).

Before the palettes, neither sidebar followed this table exactly. The terminal sidebar:
- drew its title and the command bar's prompt in Irie's sun yellow, which reads at 1.8:1 on a light terminal;
- took its state colours from the terminal's own theme, so their contrast was whatever that theme gave;
- left failed rows out of the needs-you count, though they are red;
- had no look for frozen, paused or pausing.

Two older faults were fixed just before: working shared needs you's `●`, and an exit with an error code shared a clean exit's `·`. The Mac app's faults are listed in [MAC.md](MAC.md) §5.

### 5.4 Files

Irie uses the XDG defaults on macOS too (`~/.config`, `~/.local/state`, `~/.local/share`, `~/.cache`), so paths match Linux. Directories are 0700 and files 0600.

| Path | Contents | Written by |
|---|---|---|
| `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/irie/config.toml` | User config (§5.5) | user |
| `$XDG_STATE_HOME/irie/tmux.conf` | Generated tmux config | core |
| `$XDG_STATE_HOME/irie/tmux-dock.conf`, `dock-trust.json` | The dock server's generated config (§2.3); the hashes of the repo Dock files the user trusted, the newest 200 | core |
| `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/irie/dock.json` | The user's own Dock controls, for every session, in cmux's Dock shape (§2.4) | user |
| `$XDG_STATE_HOME/irie/state.json` | Snapshot (atomic rename): `version` and the undo and redo stacks (§4.5); from later milestones per session the id, name, cwd, worktree, branch, profile, vendor ids, parked, priority and order. Keys a version does not know are kept; a file without the stacks is an empty history, and one that is not JSON is moved to `state.json.bad` | core |
| `$XDG_STATE_HOME/irie/journal/YYYY-MM-DD.ndjson` | Event envelopes, kept `journal.keep_days` days | core |
| `$XDG_STATE_HOME/irie/sessions/<id>/` | `spec.json`, `task.md`, `claude-settings.json`, `gate.json`, `exit.json`, `host.json` (the host's pid, its agent's, and that it knows freeze), `freeze.json` (while the session is frozen), `note-<n>.md`, `status.json` (the last status line update, §6.3), `result.md` (the last turn's last message, whole, §6.4) | core, host |
| `$XDG_STATE_HOME/irie/sessions/<id>/mcp.json`, `sessions/<id>/browser/` | The session's own MCP servers (§3.3); its browser's profile while the browser runs, with `irie-browser.pid`, removed when it closes (§4.15) | core; `irie browser` |
| `$XDG_STATE_HOME/irie/sessions/<id>/lock.json`, `lock-check.json` | The session's agent lock, written as its agent starts; what the checks found for each harness and package version, each kept 30 days after a check last asked (§4.21) | core |
| `$XDG_STATE_HOME/irie/local.json` | The model `irie setup local` chose for `+local`, and what models took in memory once loaded (§6.3) | core |
| `$XDG_STATE_HOME/irie/cards.json` | Materialized cards (docs/proposals/materialized-cards.md): each card's spec, compacted, the hash of the recipe and the inputs it was made from, when and by which local model, the last failure, and the remake under way, which a core that starts again drops. Mode 0600, written as a transaction on each change; a file that does not read is a cache lost, written over | core |
| `$XDG_DATA_HOME/irie/notepad.json`, `backups/notepad/<day>.json`, `notepad.json.bad-<time>` | The notepad (§4.20): `version`, `rev`, the notes in their order and Recently Deleted; a copy as it stood before each day's first change, the newest 30; a file that did not read, moved aside and never written over. In the data folder, since they are the person's own words | core |
| `$XDG_DATA_HOME/irie/obsidian-notes.json` | What the core knew of the notepad's note in the Obsidian vault when the two last agreed: the note's path, and each task's note, block identifier, words and tick (§4.17) | core |
| `<obsidian.vault>/<obsidian.note>` | The notepad kept in the vault too, a task a note (§4.17); the person's, and Obsidian's, as much as the core's | core, the person, Obsidian |
| `$XDG_STATE_HOME/irie/context.git/` | The context store, a bare git repository: a branch per session, a commit per turn (§4.13) | core |
| `$XDG_STATE_HOME/irie/{core,ui}.log` | Logs | core, ui |
| `$XDG_STATE_HOME/irie/ghostty.json` | Id of the Irie window's Ghostty terminal | `irie` (M9) |
| `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/irie/`, or `$TMPDIR/irie-<uid>/` if unset (macOS) | `core.sock`, `core.lock`, `app.lock`. macOS limits socket paths to about 104 bytes, so this path must stay short | core |
| `$XDG_DATA_HOME/irie/worktrees/<repo>/<slug>/` | Worktrees | core |
| `$XDG_DATA_HOME/irie/scratch/<slug>/` | Working dir for tasks that name no repo | core |
| `$XDG_DATA_HOME/irie/skills/plugins/<digest>/` | The plugin folder of Irie's own skills, named by a digest of what it holds, its files read-only; checked before each launch that uses it and made again if anything changed; removed at the next core start when no session uses it | core |
| `$XDG_DATA_HOME/irie/anon/` | Anonymous research (§4.16): `compartments.json` (0600), `tor/` (its `torrc`, data and pid file) and `searxng/` (its checkout, Python, `settings.yml` and secret) | core; `irie anon`, `irie setup anon` |
| `$XDG_STATE_HOME/irie/sessions/<id>/anon.env`, `<cwd>/evidence/` | An anonymous session's proxy variables, which `irie hook` adds to `CLAUDE_ENV_FILE` at SessionStart; the responses its fetch kept, each beside a JSON file of where and when (§4.16) | core; `irie anon mcp` |
| `$XDG_DATA_HOME/irie/browser/template/` | The clean profile each start of Irie's browser copies, with `irie-template.json` naming the browser build that made it (§4.15) | `irie browser`, `irie setup browser` |
| `$XDG_STATE_HOME/irie/dms/` | The dead man's switch helpers' `healthchecks.key` and `switch.json` (the checks' ids and ping URLs), both 0600 and never printed | `irie dms` |
| `$XDG_CACHE_HOME/irie/env.json` | Captured login-shell environment | `irie app-launch` |
| `~/Applications/Irie.app` | The launcher | `irie install-app` |

**Writes are transactions** (`internal/filetx`). A writer holds the file's write lock, an flock on `.<name>.lock` beside it, from before it reads the file until the new contents are in place. So writers take turns, whether goroutines of one process or processes of their own, and none loses a change another made meanwhile: the `config` op reads, changes and writes `config.toml` all under it (§5.5). The new contents go to a temporary file in the same directory, `.<name>.<random>.tmp`, which is synced with fsync(2) and then renamed over the file. So a reader, which takes no lock, and a restart after a crash find the old file or the new one, never part of either. The sync is fsync, as SQLite's is by default on macOS, and not `F_FULLFSYNC`, which also empties the drive's cache: that took about 8 ms a write against under 1 ms, and some files, such as `status.json`, are rewritten many times a minute. The kernel lets a lock go when its holder exits, so a crash never leaves one held, and a writer that waits 5 s for its turn gives up with `held by another process`. Lock files stay once made: removing one while a writer waits on it would let two writers in. A file linked from elsewhere is written where it lives, so the link stays, and a file replaced keeps its mode. `core.lock` and `up.lock` are flocks of the same kind. Only two kinds of file are written otherwise: the journal and the logs, which are only appended to, and the message file that `send` hands to tmux, which the core removes at once.

### 5.5 Config and defaults

`$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/irie/config.toml`. Every key is optional, and an unknown key is an error that names it. The values shown are the defaults.

The Mac app's Settings window changes the file through the `config` op (§2.4), which edits it in place: a key the file sets keeps its line, its comment and its place, a new key goes after the last key of its table, and a new table goes at the end. The edit is refused unless the file then decodes to its old values with exactly the change made, and loads. It is one transaction under the file's write lock (§5.4), so another writer waits its turn and neither change is lost. A file linked from elsewhere is written where it lives, and the file keeps its mode. The core runs on the result at once, with any hand edit made since it started: the next task, the agents' menus and the sidebars' header (`red_on_done`, `close_when_done`) follow, and turning `close_when_done` off stops the closes still waiting. What is read only as Irie starts waits for its next start: `tmux.*`, `journal.keep_days`, the tmux keys, and the terminal sidebar's `ui.theme` and `ui.red_on_done`. Irie's own Ollama server keeps its settings until it starts again.

```toml
[ui]
sidebar_width = 34            # columns
focus_key = "C-]"             # tmux key that toggles sidebar <-> slot
flip_key = "C-\\"            # tmux key that flips the slot: conversation <-> raw terminal ("" for none)
view = "conversation"         # what the slot shows first: conversation | terminal (§4.7)
max_sub_rows = 6
red_on_done = false
helper_style = "browser"      # browser | terminal: how a view draws a helper beside its session (§4.4)
new_tabs = "last"             # last | first | next: where a new session goes in the list; next is just after the session that started it (§4.1)
bar = "cheer"                 # cheer | tips: what the strip under the terminal shows, one at a time; the default is the brand's (§2.10)
terminal_links = "rolodex"    # rolodex | browser: where a link ⌘-clicked in the Mac app's terminals opens: the session's panes (a page on a Browser card, a file of text on the Code card at its line) or the Mac's own apps; ⇧⌘-click opens it in the other (op link, docs/MAC.md)
ghostty = "auto"              # auto | off  (M9: open/focus the Irie window in Ghostty)
theme = "rosewater"           # rosewater | terracotta | lilac | evergreen: the Mac app's palettes, for the sidebar and the slot; light or dark follows the terminal (§5.3)
freeze_dog = "10s"            # how long Irie's window or a sidebar may stop answering before Raphael force quits it and starts it again (§2.4, §4.11); "0s" = never, else at least 3s

[spawn]
default_profile = "auto"      # auto = the lowest cost per success among profiles accurate enough (§6.4)
default_cwd = ""              # "" = new scratch dir per task
ready_timeout = "30s"
hide_tmux = true              # remove TMUX/TMUX_PANE from agent env
close_when_done = false       # close a session once its turn ends and it stays quiet for close_delay (reopen undoes it; status, report and question sessions are left open)
close_delay = "30s"           # how long a finished session waits; a new prompt, or anything it needs from you, cancels the close
teammates = "off"             # panes | off: Claude Code sessions may start agent teams, each teammate in a terminal beside its session (§2.3)
follow_up_window = "2m"       # a jot that reads as more for the jot sent within this long before goes to its agent instead of a new one (§4.2); "0s" = never
follow_up_delay = "8s"        # how long such a jot waits before it is typed to the agent, so that New task can take it back
preferences = ["Keep any text or notification you send the user short enough to take in at a glance on a phone: …"]   # how you like agents to work, a line each, told to every session as it starts (§4.2); your list replaces the built-in one

[profiles.claude]             # the strongest: hard work and orchestration
harness = "claude-code"
command = "claude"
model = "claude-opus-5-5"
effort = "max"
permission_mode = "auto"
args = []
env = {}
cost = ""                     # what the + list says it costs; "" = Irie's own reading (§6.3)
sdk = false                   # true: the session runs on the Agent SDK, in Irie's own pane (§3.5), for a key whose credits cover the SDK but not Claude Code
fallback_model = ""           # a model outside the profile's own family, such as "claude-opus-5-5" for a Fable profile: at the model's own usage limit its sessions switch to it in place (§6.4); "" = they wait for the reset

[profiles.sonnet]             # built in: fewer tokens for most coding tasks
harness = "claude-code"
command = "claude"
model = "claude-sonnet-5-5"
effort = "medium"
permission_mode = "auto"

[profiles.haiku]              # built in: a fortieth of Opus's price, for small work
harness = "claude-code"
command = "claude"
model = "claude-haiku-5-5"
permission_mode = "auto"

[profiles.local]              # built in: Claude Code on a local model (§6.1), no tokens
harness = "claude-code"
command = "claude"
model = ""                    # "" = the one `irie setup local` chose; +ollama:<model> names any other
local = { runtime = "ollama", min_context = 65536, enforce = true }

[profiles.big]                # built in: Claude Code on Irie's own llama-server (§6.5), for big models in little memory
harness = "claude-code"
command = "claude"
model = ""                    # "" = the best model it can run here; a GGUF file's name, or an Ollama model's
local = { runtime = "llama-server", min_context = 65536, enforce = true }
# local's peer = "Asahi" runs the model on that peer's llama-server, through an ssh tunnel (§6.5)

[profiles.mikael]             # built in: a session that runs as Mikael (§4.11), to talk with him
harness = "claude-code"
command = "claude"
model = "claude-opus-5-5"
effort = "high"
permission_mode = "auto"
arch = "mikael"               # the arch agent the session runs as; any profile may name one

[profiles.chaos]              # built in: the chaos monkey (§4.19), which tries to break what the task names and reports what broke
harness = "claude-code"
command = "claude"
model = "claude-opus-5-5"
effort = "high"
permission_mode = "auto"
skill = "chaos"               # the skill of Irie's its sessions start on, the task after /irie:chaos; any Claude Code profile may name one

# [profiles.helper]           # example: an agent of your own (§4.2)
# folder = "~/Helper"         # its sessions run here, in place, where its CLAUDE.md, skills and records are; a task's own @place wins
# model = "claude-sonnet-5-5"
# `irie config set profiles.helper.folder ~/Helper` makes one; set and unset change any key through the core

# [profiles.aider]            # example: generic harness
# harness = "generic"
# command = "aider"
# args = ["--model", "ollama_chat/gpt-oss:20b"]
# task = "paste"              # arg | paste
# cost = "free, 50 requests a day"

[local]                       # the Ollama server local profiles run on (§6.3)
ollama = ""                   # "" = PATH, then the usual install places
url = ""                      # a server you run, e.g. "http://127.0.0.1:11434"; "" = Irie runs its own
port = 11435                  # Irie's own server, on 127.0.0.1
context = 65536               # its OLLAMA_CONTEXT_LENGTH
kv_cache = "q8_0"             # f16 | q8_0 | q4_0; q8_0 takes about half the memory of f16
memory_gib = 0                # the most local models may take; 0 = two thirds of the machine's
env = {}                      # more settings for its server, such as OLLAMA_MODELS
llama_server = ""             # Irie's own llama-server (§6.5): any llama.cpp build, such as PrismML's; "" = PATH, then Homebrew's
llama_port = 11436            # it listens on 127.0.0.1; it takes context and kv_cache above too
llama_args = []               # more arguments for it, such as a build's own
llama_slots = 1               # how many sessions it serves at once, each with a context of its own
models = ""                   # GGUF files it may run besides Ollama's models; "" = $XDG_DATA_HOME/irie/models

[route]                       # how auto picks a profile (§6.4)
profiles = ["local", "haiku", "sonnet", "claude"]
min_accuracy = 0.85           # a profile below this for a task's class is never chosen
local_min_accuracy = 0.6      # the same for a local model, which costs no tokens
prior_weight = 10             # how many outcomes the starting guesses below count as
pressure = 80                 # from this much of the plan's five hours, in percent, its tokens cost more
failover = true               # carry a task on, on a cheaper profile, when a usage limit stops it
delegate = true               # tell Claude Code sessions they can hand subtasks to cheaper profiles
delegate_mode = "acceptEdits" # the permission mode of a delegated session

[route.accuracy]              # starting guesses: the share done right the first time, by class
local = { small = 0.85, medium = 0.40, hard = 0.05 }
haiku = { small = 0.92, medium = 0.70, hard = 0.30 }
sonnet = { small = 0.96, medium = 0.88, hard = 0.65 }
claude = { small = 0.98, medium = 0.94, hard = 0.90 }

[route.cost]                  # starting guesses: a task's cost relative to one on +claude
local = 0.0
haiku = 0.02
sonnet = 0.25
claude = 1.0

[route.words]                 # what makes a task small, medium or hard; each list replaces the built-in one
# small = ["typo", "rename", "docstring", …]
# medium = ["implement", "fix", "test", …]
# hard = ["design", "refactor", "debug", "review", "orchestrat", …]

[route.embed]                 # a task takes the class of the nearest task given one (irie ctl route --label)
model = "embeddinggemma-2:270m" # an Ollama embedding model; "" = route.words alone
url = "http://127.0.0.1:11434"  # the Ollama that runs it: your own, as Irie's holds one model
near = 0.75                   # below this cosine the nearest is unlike the task, and route.words decide

[bench]                       # several runs of one task, then a judge (§6.6)
runs = 3                      # how many sessions do the task
judge = "claude"              # the profile that scores them, makes the result and rewrites the playbook
pass = 70                     # a run scored this or more, of 100, is rated good
timeout = "1h"                # a run still at work after this is stopped and judged as it stands
rules = 30                    # the most rules a playbook keeps
close = true                  # once judged, its runs and judge close, to the history

[repos]
roots = []                    # e.g. ["~/code"]; scanned two levels deep for git repos
aliases = {}                  # e.g. { web = "~/code/website" }

[worktree]
root = ""                     # "" = $XDG_DATA_HOME/irie/worktrees
base = "HEAD"
branch_prefix = "irie/"
copy = [".claude/settings.local.json", "CLAUDE.local.md"]   # plus the repo's .worktreeinclude
submodules = true
drop = true                   # remove a closed session's worktree once its branch is in its repo's land branch (dog.land) and nothing else is in it or runs in it; reopen adds it back (§4.5)

[hold]
max = "24h"

[freeze]
warn_after = "5m"

[subagents]
keep_finished = "10m"

[notes]
held = "[Irie] The user paused you at a tool boundary for {{.Duration}} ({{.From}}–{{.To}} UTC). Re-check anything time-sensitive before you continue."
frozen = "[Irie] The user froze this session for {{.Duration}} ({{.From}}–{{.To}} UTC). Network calls or child processes may have failed meanwhile; re-check before you continue."
priority = "[Irie] The user raised this task's priority at {{.At}} UTC.{{if .Reason}} Reason: {{.Reason}}.{{end}} Focus on finishing it, defer optional work and report blockers early."
priority_cleared = "[Irie] This task's priority is back to normal ({{.At}} UTC)."
note = "[Irie] Note from the user: {{.Text}}"

[tmux]
path = ""                     # "" = PATH, then /opt/homebrew/bin, /usr/local/bin, /usr/bin
socket = "irie"

[journal]
keep_days = 30
tool_input = "summary"        # summary | none

[context]                     # each session's conversation in git, a commit per turn (§4.13)
keep_days = 30                # days a closed session's context stays after its newest turn; 0 = for good

[usage]
status_line = true            # be Claude Code's status line, to read cost and plan use; yours still runs (§6.3)
rebuilds = true               # the prompt-cache meter: count each session's cache rebuilds, what woke it and what they cost (§6.3)
rebuild_card = 1.0            # dollars: a card says how often its session woke cold today once that cost this much; 0 = from the first
wake_most = 4.0               # dollars: what a nudge is worth; the trod bot and the jot dog leave a session whose cache ran out be past it; 0 = no limit

[lock]                        # agent locks: each session's setup as its agent started (§4.21)
check = "off"                 # off | daily: daily lets the core ask OSV.dev once a day about the locks' harness and package versions; irie lock --check asks at once
osv = "https://api.osv.dev"   # the OSV API a check asks, such as a mirror of your own

[skills]                      # the skills that come with Irie, as a plugin for each Claude Code session only (§3.3)
enabled = true                # false = hand agents no skills
disabled = []                 # skills to leave out, such as ["present"]; a profile with skills = false gets none

[dog]                         # the jot dog: sees each jot through until its work lands (§4.8)
watch = true                  # read a jot's own worktree when its turn ends, and say on its row what is left
nudges = 2                    # how often it sends one jot back to finish between two of your prompts; 0 = only watch
land = {}                     # by repo (alias, folder name or path), the branch its work must reach, e.g. { irie = "live" }

[trod]                        # the trod bot: keeps sessions going while you are away at night (§4.10)
on = false                    # work every night, from start to end
start = "23:00"               # local time
end = "07:00"                 # the same as start = around the clock
away = "15m"                  # it steps in once the Mac has had no keyboard or mouse input this long; "0s" = at once
questions = true              # tell an agent that asks you something to decide itself
nudges = 8                    # how often it may send one session on in a night
retry = "15m"                 # how long a stopped session waits before it is sent on again
within = "24h"                # it sends on only sessions that stopped this recently; "0s" = however long ago
max_working = 4               # it sends a session on only while fewer than this many work; 0 = no limit
max_plan_use = 90             # percent: past this in either window of the plan it sends nothing on, and past it in the week's it freezes what would still spend
pace = false                  # keep the week to a day line: each day of the plan's week may take max_plan_use/7 more
keep_awake = true             # keep the Mac from sleeping while sessions have work left
rules = ["Don't push, publish or send anything off this machine: …", "Don't delete or overwrite what you didn't make, …", "Don't spend money, …"]
guidelines = ["When you have to choose, take the option you would recommend, …", …]   # each list replaces the built-in one

[lid]                         # a MacBook's lid: with it closed, the Mac stays awake while sessions work, once irie setup lid has run (§4.12)
awake = "work"                # work = while a session works or the trod bot keeps the Mac awake; always = while Irie runs; off = it sleeps as macOS decides
grace = "2m"                  # it stays awake this long after the last work stops
battery = 10                  # percent: on battery below this the Mac may sleep, at once with its lid closed; 0 = down to empty

[arch.gabriel]                # Gabriel: while you are at your computer and it is busy, what you work on gets the machine first (§4.11)
on = true                     # hold back what is out of your focus while the machine is busy and you are at it
outside = true                # slow your heavy apps outside Irie too, not only Irie's sessions
away = "5m"                   # with no keyboard or mouse this long you are away, and nothing is held back; "0s" = always at the computer
word = "10m"                  # a session you message or start stays in your focus this long
heavy = 50.0                  # heavy from this share of one CPU, in percent: an app outside Irie, which is slowed, or the tab you are on, for which the rest of your focus steps back
spare = ["zoom.us", "FaceTime", "Microsoft Teams", "Webex", "Slack", "Discord", "Music", "Spotify", "QuickTime Player", "OBS", "Logic Pro", "GarageBand", "Final Cut Pro"]   # never slowed
guard = true                  # turn away an agent's call that would put a window in front of you or on the desktop you are on
boost = "1h"                  # how long a boost lasts (irie ctl gabriel boost): your focus first, busy or not; at most 12h
always = false                # boost whenever you are at the computer (irie ctl gabriel boost always): the rest runs slower even with room to spare

[arch.raphael]                # Raphael (Raffi): restarts what stops answering, and looks over the computer (§4.11)
on = true                     # restart a part of Irie that stops answering, and look over the computer by himself
core = "2m"                   # how long the core's own loop may stand still before he restarts the core; "0s" = never, else at least 30s
every = "6h"                  # how often he looks over the computer; "0s" = only when asked, else at least 10m
known = []                    # startup items you know, by label, plist or program, which he no longer flags (It's mine adds one)

[arch.mikael]                 # Mikael, the first arch agent: a helper against evil and for the good you want to see (§4.11)
on = true                     # with every Claude Code session: his word as it and its subagents start, and the mikael agent on call
door = true                   # he weighs each call that could do harm that can't be taken back before it runs, and stops one that would do evil
good = []                     # the good you want to see in the world, in your own words; every session he is with hears it

[materialize]                 # cards a local model makes once and Irie keeps (proposals/materialized-cards.md)
model = ""                    # the local model that makes them, from Ollama here, such as "bonsai-2:9b"; "" = the best for chat that fits (16B, 10 GB at most)
sessions = false              # a session's conversation shows a card of where it stands, over its message box, made again when its state or newest message changes, at most every 2 minutes

[learn]                       # what helps you learn while your agents work (proposals/learning.md)
tutor = "off"                 # off | explain | teach: Claude Code's Explanatory or Learning output style; +explain and +teach set it for one task
visual = true                 # in a tutor mode, the agent draws a small diagram where a picture helps
agent_tips = true             # Irie's tips under Claude Code's spinner
links = {}                    # your own learning resources, by title, first in the Help menu's list, e.g. { "Team handbook" = "https://example.com/handbook" }

[env]
capture = "login-shell"       # login-shell | none: the environment of a server `irie up --login-env` starts, as Irie.app does (§2.7)

[remote]                      # the phone app (PHONE.md)
on = false                    # serve it on this machine's local network; the core keeps the gateway running
port = 7387                   # its HTTPS port; the setup page is on the port below, over plain HTTP
name = ""                     # what the phone calls this machine; "" = its computer name (macOS) or host name
listen = ""                   # the address to listen on; "" = every address of this machine
# [remote.panes]              # web panes of the plugins folder that phones reach, each over HTTPS at a port of its own (PHONE.md §2.2)
# "invoices.invoices" = 7390  # a pane's id, its plugin's name, a dot and the pane's: the port

[place]                       # where a task typed here starts when its words name no machine (§2.8)
mode = "local"                # local, or auto: to a peer with clearly more room while this machine is busy
headroom_cpus = 2             # this machine is busy once the work waiting to run leaves fewer CPUs free
headroom_gib = 2              # or less memory than this is free, or the kernel warns of memory pressure

# [peers.Asahi]               # one of the machines `irie peer add` paired with
# repos = ["irie"]            # the @names auto may send there; a task with no @ goes too
# ssh = "asahi"               # its ssh name, for fetching work back; "" = the one irie peer add --ssh used

[vpn]                         # VPN control (§4.18), for the VPN that provider names
on = false                    # show the VPN in every client, and take the :vpn words; on by default in a copy whose brand names its VPNs
provider = "mullvad"          # mullvad | tailscale | netbird | warp | nordvpn | cisco | globalprotect | forticlient | system | command; a copy offers those in brand.VPNs, the first by default
mullvad = ""                  # the mullvad CLI, which anonymous research uses too; "" = PATH, then /usr/local/bin and /usr/bin
cli = ""                      # another VPN's own command; "" = where its app puts it
server = ""                   # cisco: its host or profile entry; globalprotect: its portal; forticlient: its connection; system: the VPN's name; "" = the VPN's own choice, or brand.VPNServer
status = []                   # command: the program and arguments that print your VPN's state, such as ["/usr/local/bin/myvpn", "status"]
connect = []                  # command: the program and arguments that connect it
disconnect = []               # command: the program and arguments that disconnect it
connected = ""                # command: a regular expression its status's output matches while connected and never otherwise, such as "(?m)^State: Connected$"
connecting = ""               # command: one it matches while connecting; "" = none

[browser]                     # Irie's browser, for agents' research on the web (§4.15)
connector = "off"             # on | off: give Claude Code sessions Mullvad Browser through irie browser; irie setup browser turns it on
path = ""                     # the browser's executable; "" = Mullvad Browser where its installers put it, then on PATH
headless = true               # run it without a window
idle = "10m"                  # close it after this long without a call; "0s" keeps it open
proxy = ""                    # its traffic, DNS too, through this SOCKS5 proxy, such as Mullvad's "socks5://10.64.0.1:1080" inside the VPN
search = "https://html.duckduckgo.com/html/?q=%s"   # the search tool's page, %s for the query

[anon]                        # anonymous research: +anon and +tor tasks in compartments (§4.16)
on = false                    # take +anon and +tor tasks; irie setup anon turns it on
route = "mullvad"             # mullvad | tor: how a +anon task's new compartment leaves
countries = []                # Mullvad exits only in these countries, by code such as "se"; [] = any
owned = true                  # Mullvad exits only on servers Mullvad owns, not rents
search = true                 # run Irie's SearXNG for the search tool, once installed
search_port = 8888            # SearXNG's port on 127.0.0.1
search_route = "mullvad"      # mullvad | tor: where SearXNG asks the engines from
browser = true                # give anonymous tasks Irie's browser through their exit, when Mullvad Browser is installed
tor = ""                      # the tor executable; "" = PATH, then Homebrew's and Linux's places
tor_port = 9150               # the first of the Tor compartments' SocksPorts on 127.0.0.1

[markets]                     # the markets plugin's map (§4.17)
folder = ""                   # the folder that holds your map's index.html; "" = none chosen yet

[obsidian]                    # the obsidian plugin's vault (§4.17)
vault = ""                    # the vault's folder, a folder of Markdown notes; "" = none chosen yet
notes = true                  # keep the notepad (§4.20) in the vault too, as note, both ways
note = "Irie Notes.md"        # the notepad's note, a path in the vault outside the folders whose names start with a dot

[plugins]                     # Irie's plugins (§4.17): each turns all its parts off when it is off
vpn = true                    # the VPN in every client and :vpn (§4.18); vpn.on is its part's own switch
privacy = true                # anonymous research, Mullvad Browser for agents and the dead man's switch guide; each part keeps its own switch
writing = true                # writing in Variorum: :write and the skill its sessions run (docs/proposals/writers.md)
markets = false               # your market map in the Mac app's Markets window, and :markets
obsidian = true               # your Obsidian vault on a Vault card of the panes, and the notepad kept in it

[plugins.manifests]           # your own plugins, from the manifests in the plugins folder (§4.17), by name; one not named here is on
# invoices = false
```

---

## 6. Local models

### 6.1 What v1 needs

Claude Code reaches a local model through any server that speaks the Anthropic Messages API (`/v1/messages`): Ollama, LM Studio and llama-server all do. Pointed at one by hand, two things go wrong. The first is context size: Ollama defaults to a 4096-token context below 24 GiB of VRAM and silently cuts longer prompts, so an agent loses its instructions without any error. The second is model names: Claude Code's subagents and background tasks ask for its opus, sonnet and haiku aliases, which a local server does not have. A local profile (`local = { runtime = "ollama", … }`, such as the built-in `+local`) gets both right from Irie:

- **Environment.** As Ollama's own launcher (`ollama launch claude`) does, Irie sets `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` to the server, a placeholder `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN`, an empty `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` (one in the user's environment would win over the token), every alias (`ANTHROPIC_DEFAULT_{OPUS,SONNET,HAIKU,FABLE}_MODEL`) and `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBAGENT_MODEL` to the model, `CLAUDE_CODE_AUTO_MODE_SERVER=0` and `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_NONESSENTIAL_TRAFFIC=1`. It sets `CLAUDE_CODE_MAX_CONTEXT_TOKENS` to the context the preflight measured: for a model ID it does not recognise, Claude Code otherwise assumes 200K and compacts far too late. From 100k it also sets `CLAUDE_CODE_AUTO_COMPACT_WINDOW`, whose minimum that is. The profile's own `env` wins over all of these.
- **Context.** Irie runs its own Ollama server with the context it needs (§6.3), so nothing of the user's Ollama setup has to change. A server the user runs at `local.url` is checked as it is.
- **Permissions.** Claude Code asks auto mode's check of the session's own model before each command it runs, and a local model cannot answer it, so in auto mode every command was refused. A local model's session therefore runs in `acceptEdits` when its profile names no mode, or `auto`, and is allowed the build and test commands of `LocalAllowed` (internal/harness/claude): `go test`, `go vet`, `pytest`, `npm test`, `cargo test` and the like, with `go mod`, `go run`, `go version`, `go doc`, `go list`, `git add` and `git commit`, so a bench's run (§6.6) gets through on its own; a test runs whatever code it likes, so `go run` adds no new risk, and nothing in the list pushes. Claude Code runs its own read-only commands, such as `ls`, `cat` and `git diff`, without asking in every mode, but not `go version`, which the first bench's runs each asked for before anything else. Anything else waits for the user's OK, except in a session no one watches, such as a bench's, which refuses it (§6.6). Any other mode the profile names stays, without the list.

Before spawning on a local profile, v1 runs a preflight (M10):

| Runtime | Context probe | Fix shown on refusal |
|---|---|---|
| Ollama | Send a 1-token `POST /v1/messages` so the model loads exactly as the agent will load it, then read `context_length` from `GET /api/ps` | On Irie's own server: raise `local.context`. On the user's: set `OLLAMA_CONTEXT_LENGTH` (for the macOS app: its context length setting, or `launchctl setenv`, then restart Ollama), or leave `local.url` empty so Irie runs its own. The OpenAI and Anthropic shims cannot set the context per request |
| LM Studio (after M10) | `GET /api/v1/models`, field `loaded_instances[].config.context_length` | `lms load <model> --context-length <n>` |
| llama-server (Irie's own, §6.5) | `GET /props` (`default_generation_settings.n_ctx`), once `GET /health` answers 200 | On Irie's own server: raise `local.context`, which is its `-c` for its one slot |

**Preflight rules.**
- **Model.** The profile's own, else the one `irie setup local` chose, else the best of the models Irie suggests (§6.3) that the server has, an agent can run and the machine can hold, else the only such model there. With several others, the refusal lists them, cheapest in memory first; with none, it points to `irie setup local`. A name is the server's own, as in Ollama: `+ollama:qwen3.8` runs `qwen3.8:latest`, in any case.
- **Tools.** A model whose capabilities (`/api/show`) lack `tools` is refused.
- **Engine.** A model Ollama runs on its MLX engine (`details.runner` is `mlx`) is refused, and `+local` passes over it. Ollama pulls such builds for some tags on Apple silicon, such as the nvfp4 `qwen3.5:4b` of 2026-11. The runner caps of §6.3 don't reach that engine: on an 18 GiB M3 Pro a Qwen3.5 4B there grew to 16 GB in two Claude Code tasks, and swap reached 17 GB. A GGUF tag of the same model, such as `qwen3.5:4b-q4_K_M`, runs on llama-server.
- **Threshold.** Refuse below `min_context` (default 65536, Ollama's guidance for coding agents). A model trained for less is refused before it is loaded. A refusal names the runtime, the measured context and the fix.
- **Memory.** Refused when what the model takes (§6.3) is above the budget, or above what is free now less the reserve. The load happens only after this check. A refusal for want of free memory names the best model that fits now, such as `bonsai-2:9b fits now (+ollama:bonsai-2:9b)`.
- **Unreachable runtime.** Refused: "not reachable at <url>".
- **Bypass.** `enforce = false` in the profile, or `irie ctl spawn --force`, spawns anyway, with the problems as the row's warnings.
- **Expectations.** Local sessions carry a `local` tag, shown in the status strip. Anthropic does not support Claude Code on non-Claude models, so tool-use quality is the model's own. Ollama's shims ignore `tool_choice`.

### 6.2 After v1: routing and agents in tandem

| Step | What | Builds on |
|---|---|---|
| Tiers | v1 builds in `+sonnet` and `+local` beside `+claude` (§6.3). More profiles by tier (`+fast`, `+strong`) can follow. A task keeps its profile for its whole life | v1 profiles |
| Budget-aware choice | A per-task router skips a supplier whose subscription window is nearly used up and prefers local when it fits. Inputs: Claude Code's status-line `rate_limits`, which `irie statusline` captures from v1 (§6.3); CodexBar's `usage --format json` and `guard` for other suppliers | Envelope, profiles, §6.3 |
| RAM admission | v1 checks a local model's memory against the budget and the free memory before an Ollama spawn, and refuses (§6.3). Queueing the spawn until memory frees, and LM Studio's and llama-server's models, come later | M10 |
| Failover at a boundary | v1 carries a task on after a `StopFailure` with `rate_limit`, in the same directory, on a cheaper profile accurate enough for it (§6.4). Reopening the same conversation on another profile comes with reopen (M4) | Events, §6.4 |
| In tandem: delegation | v1: an orchestrator runs `irie ctl delegate "<subtask>"`, which picks a cheaper profile accurate enough, waits, and prints the result (§6.4). Later: an MCP tool that wraps it, siblings on other suppliers, and results as notes | Socket API, §6.4 |
| In tandem: review | When a turn ends, an optional reviewer profile from a different supplier reviews the worktree's diff. Its findings become a normalised record plus a note to the worker; at most two rounds | Worktrees, notes, adapters for other suppliers |
| Shared context | A per-repo roster of active agents, their tasks and branches, delivered as a note at spawn | Envelope |

### 6.3 Cost: tokens and memory

Every session costs something, and the spawn list says what before a task starts. A cloud model costs tokens. Every agent also costs the machine's memory, and a local model a lot of it.

| Cost | Cloud profile | Local profile |
|---|---|---|
| Tokens | Claude Code's own figure at list price (`cost.total_cost_usd`), and on Pro and Max the plan's five-hour and weekly use (`rate_limits`), from its status line | None |
| Memory | What the session's processes take, measured | The same, plus what its model takes: weights, the context cache at the server's context, and working room |

**Status line.** With `usage.status_line` (on by default), Irie's settings file makes `irie statusline` the session's status line.
- **Forwarding.** It forwards each update to the core (op `status`), which answers with the session's state and running subagents. The call takes at most 300 ms and never fails. An update that changed nothing, as an idle session's every few seconds, costs the core no diff and sends no frame; one that changed the session's figures diffs and sends only that session's row, and the header only when the plan's use moved.
- **The plan's use.** Every session sends what its own last request saw of the plan's two windows, so an idle session goes on sending old figures. The core keeps, for each window, the figure of the window that resets last, and within it the highest use, since a window's use only grows until it resets; a figure without a reset time is taken as it comes. So the header's plan figure never flips between sessions, and a restarted core folds every session's `status.json` the same way.
- **The user's own.** It then `exec`s the user's own status line on the same input. It finds it where Claude Code would: the project's local settings at the repo's main checkout, the shared project settings in the cwd, then the user's in `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` or `~/.claude`. Their `padding`, `refreshInterval` and `hideVimModeIndicator` are kept.
- **No status line of their own.** It prints a short line instead, because any status line hides Claude Code's footer hints: the session's state in the marks and colours of §5.3, then the cost, the context with a bar, and the plan's use (`✓ done · $1.23 · ctx ▰▰▰▱▱▱▱▱ 41% · 5h 62%`). A turn that ended while subagents or teammates still work is not done: the line says `◐ waiting on 2 helpers: Explore, dev` (or `◐ waiting on 3 teammates`), and while the turn runs `◐ working + 2 helpers`. Shares turn yellow from 70% and red from 90%; the rest is faint. While the session works, its state and the faint parts take its own colour instead: its card's mark in the Mac app's dark mode ([MAC.md](MAC.md) §5), at 4.5:1 or more on the app's terminal. The core works it out in the palette the app said it draws in (`theme`), or else `ui.theme`. Parts drop from the end to fit `COLUMNS`. It re-runs every 5 s (`refreshInterval`), since Claude Code's updates stop while the session is idle, as when it waits on background subagents. Every 2 s, each run an irie started in every session, cost some 15% of a core with 30 sessions.
- **Restarts.** The last update is kept in the session's `status.json`, so a restarted core still knows it.

**Prompt-cache rebuilds.** Claude Code sends a conversation's whole context with every request. Anthropic's prompt cache keeps what a request sent for five minutes or an hour, counted from the request that last read or wrote it, and each request's usage says which. A request within that time reads the context from the cache; one after it writes the whole context into the cache again. By Anthropic's pricing page (2026-11-14), Opus 5.5 reads its cache at $0.20 a million tokens and writes it at $5 for five minutes or $8 for an hour, so one rebuild into the hour's cache costs as much as 40 turns that read it, and into the five minutes' 25. A 300k context costs $2.40 to rebuild. Irie makes many itself: freezes longer than the cache lives, as the trod bot's at the week's cap, reopens, mass thaws, the trod bot waking stopped sessions at night. The meter (`usage.rebuilds`, on by default) counts them, so Irie knows the price before it wakes a session itself.
- **Finding them.** The token tally reads each request's usage from the transcript: `input_tokens`, `cache_read_input_tokens`, `cache_creation_input_tokens` with how much went into the hour's cache and the five minutes' (`cache_creation`), `output_tokens`, the model, `speed` and `inference_geo` (internal/rebuild). A request is a rebuild when it read less than half of what its conversation's request before sent, and wrote at least 20,000 tokens of that again, about what Claude Code's own system prompt and tools take. A request after a compaction starts a new context and is none. A helper's requests are a conversation of their own. A rebuild is cold when the conversation was idle as long as its cache lives, less two minutes or a tenth of an hour's cache, since a transcript dates a reply when it was written and a cache's life runs from its request's start; otherwise the start of its prompt changed, as for another model or other tools, which the meter counts as `changed`.
- **The price.** What it wrote again at the model's list price for the cache it went into: fast mode's prices for a model that has them, a tenth more with inference held in the US, and Haiku 5.5's prices for prompts over 100,000 tokens. A warm turn would have read the same tokens for a fortieth of that on Opus 5.5. A model whose prices Irie does not know is counted without a price; a local profile's session is not metered.
- **What woke it.** The meter looks at what the session's events say came between the request before and the rebuild: a reopen, else the thaw of a session frozen in the middle of its work (the trod bot's told apart from yours), else the latest of your message or a prompt you answered (`you`), a message the trod bot typed (`trod`), the jot dog's nudge (`dog`), the end of a turn that left work of its own in the background (`background`), a usage limit, at whose reset Claude Code carries on (`limit`), Irie's carry-on after API errors (`boost`, §4.3), and a tool call that ran about as long as the cache lives, by Claude Code's `duration_ms` (`tool`). So a permission prompt answered at once and then a long build is `tool`. A helper's own conversation counts only a reopen, a thaw and its own tool calls, since messages go to the session's agent. With nothing between them, a request that stopped to call a tool ran it all that time (`tool`). A thaw of a session that waited for you wakes nothing; your message after it does. The session's state keeps the last 256 of these; for a rebuild older than they reach, the cause is left out.
- **The journal.** Each rebuild is journalled once, as `cache.rebuilt` (§5.2), by its message id, so a transcript read again after a restart or a reopen records nothing twice; one over eight days old is not recorded. The core keeps the last eight days' rebuilds, read from the journal as it starts.
- **On the row.** `rebuilds`: today's count and cost since local midnight and the week's, the last seven days, with when the last was and what woke it. Once today's cost `usage.rebuild_card` ($1 by default), its `words` say it for the card, the price first so that a narrow card keeps it: "$4.10 today: woke cold 3 times", or "$4.10 today: rebuilt its cache 3 times" when some were not cold. The Mac app, the phone apps and `irie ctl list`, after the detail, show them. Once the cache of a session that does not work has run out, `cold` says what waking it costs: the context of the conversation its agent runs now (after `/clear` a new one, which costs nothing until its first request; after `/resume` the one resumed), its last request's prompt and reply, at the price of a write into its cache, with `since`, `tokens`, `usd` and `words` ("~$2.40 to wake"). `irie ctl ps` has today's count and cost in its REBUILT column, and `irie ctl list --json` both fields.
- **The report.** `irie ctl rebuilds [--json]` (op `rebuilds`) ranks the sessions, closed ones too, and the causes by what their rebuilds cost, today and this week.
- **Weighing the price.** Before the trod bot wakes a session whose cache has run out, by a message or by thawing what it froze, it weighs the price against what a nudge is worth, `usage.wake_most`: $4 by default, 500k tokens into Opus 5.5's hour cache, and 0 for no limit. Past it, it leaves the session be for the user and says why (§4.10). It batches where it can: warm sessions go first, since one that waited for its turn would go cold, while a cold one costs the same later. The jot dog's nudge comes at a turn's end, while the cache is warm, so it costs no rebuild; a turn that ends long after its last request, as one frozen at its end, is weighed the same way (§4.8).
- **Where you wake it.** Irie asks nothing before a thaw or a reopen, so the price goes where you wake a session: the help of the Mac app's Resume button says it, and so does the review dock's Reopen, from the closed list's `cold`; `undo`'s questions before a reopen add it; `irie ctl thaw` and `irie ctl reopen` print it once done. A closing session's `ui.closed` carries what reopening it will cost (§5.2); for a session lost with the tmux server (§4.5) that is read from its transcript's end. The closed list is sent again once one of their caches runs out.
- **Not done.** Sending a stopped session on before its cache runs out: with the five minutes' cache, a `trod.retry` of 15 minutes always finds it cold, but earlier tries would spend the night's nudges sooner. Weighing a freeze at the week's cap by what its thaw will cost. A reopen itself sends nothing, so it costs nothing until the next turn, and `irie ctl reopen --lost` brings every lost session back as before.

**Memory.**
- **Measuring.** Every five ticks the core measures each session's process tree (one run of `ps`), what the models of its own server take (`/api/ps`), and what the machine has free (macOS `kern.memorystatus_level`; Linux `MemAvailable`).
- **Budget.** Local models may take `local.memory_gib`, else two thirds of physical memory. That leaves room for the system, the agents and the user's own programs; it is also what Ollama reports as GPU memory on an 18 GiB Apple-silicon Mac.
- **Reserve.** A model load must leave 2 GiB free, or a tenth of memory when that is more.
- **Estimate.** What a model takes is estimated before it loads: its weights, plus its context cache at the server's context and cache type, plus working room. The cache's shape comes from `/api/show`: K/V heads per layer, which hybrid models such as Qwen3.5 give layer by layer, and the key and value widths.
- **Sliding windows and loops.** A model whose metadata spells out each layer's kind (`layer_pattern`: `*` full attention, `S` a sliding window, `-` no cache) has its sliding-window layers counted at their window plus one 512-token batch, as llama.cpp keeps them, not at the whole context. A looped model such as NVIDIA's Dory runs its recurrent layers `num_loops` times, and in `per_loop` cache mode each pass keeps a cache of its own. For Irie's llama-server, the `--override-kv` settings in `local.llama_args` count, since that is how the model loads. Dory at a 64k `q8_0` context: 5.6 GB with two loops sharing a cache, 7.9 GB with its file's four loops each keeping one, against 14.5 GB when every one of its 72 layers was counted at the whole context.
- **Measurement.** Once the model is loaded, its size from `/api/ps` replaces the estimate, kept in `local.json`. Against Ollama 0.40.6, a 9B Qwen3.5-family model at a 64k `q8_0` context was estimated at 4.9 GB and took 4.1 GB, so the estimate errs high. `/api/ps` leaves out the runner's prompt cache and checkpoints, which the runner caps below bound: a Qwen3.5 4B that `/api/ps` put at 4.3 GB held 5.2 GB in use, inside the 2 GiB reserve.

**The spawn list.** Tab after `+` lists the profiles, then each local model an agent can run on (tools, a long enough context, within the budget) as `+ollama:<model>`, cheapest in memory first. With one candidate, its note goes to the status strip:

| Profile | Note |
|---|---|
| `+claude` | `opus-5-5 max · plan 5h 62% · ~$1.40 a session · ~640 MB a session` |
| `+local` | `qwen3.5:9b · local, $0 · ~8.4 GB`. A measured size drops the `~`; `in memory` once loaded; `too big` past the budget; `short of memory` when what is free now cannot take it |
| A generic profile | Its command and `cost` |

The status strip shows a session's cost (`$1.23 ctx 41% 640 MB`, or `local` for a local model). The header shows `mem low` when less than the reserve is free, and the plan's five-hour use (`5h62%`).

**Irie's own Ollama server.**
- **Start.** The first time a local profile needs a server, the core starts `irie runtime -- ollama serve` in window `irie-ollama` of `irie-agents`. A need is a spawn, Tab after `+`, `irie setup local` or `irie ctl local`; Tab starts it in the background and never waits for it.
- **Settings.** It runs with `OLLAMA_HOST=127.0.0.1:<local.port>`, `OLLAMA_CONTEXT_LENGTH=<local.context>` and `OLLAMA_KV_CACHE_TYPE=<local.kv_cache>`, with flash attention, which a quantised cache needs. It has one slot and one loaded model, then `local.env`.
- **Runners kept small.** Ollama 0.40 runs each model it loads in llama.cpp's llama-server, which reads its settings from the environment it inherits. By default that runner keeps up to 8 GiB of earlier prompts in memory (`--cache-ram`) and 32 checkpoints of a hybrid model's state per slot (`--ctx-checkpoints`, 50 MiB each for a Qwen3.5 4B). Measured on an 18 GiB M3 Pro on 2026-11-14, the runner of a Qwen3.5 4B at a 64k `q8_0` context grew from 3.0 GB after loading to 10.8 GB over ten Claude Code tasks in a row. So the server also gets `LLAMA_ARG_CACHE_RAM=512` and `LLAMA_ARG_CTX_CHECKPOINTS=8`. With those, FrogNano 4B, a model of the same architecture, held at 5.1 GB through ten tasks, and Qwen3.5 4B at 5.2 GB through three. Neither lost speed or passed fewer tasks. `local.env` overrides the caps. They reach only llama-server, so a model on Ollama's MLX engine is refused (§6.1).
- **Nothing changes for the user.** It runs the user's own `ollama` binary and shares their model store, so nothing downloads twice, and no setting of the user's Ollama changes. The reasoning is the same as for the private tmux server.
- **One model at a time.** That keeps its memory to one model and one cache. Loading another model unloads the first, and the check warns when a live session runs on the one that would go.
- **Stopping.** `irie runtime` keeps the server in a process group of its own and turns a hangup into SIGTERM for that group. Ollama unloads its runners on SIGTERM. A hangup, which tmux sends when it goes, would end Ollama at once and leave each runner holding its model's memory; whatever of the group is left after the server exits is stopped too.

**`irie setup local`.**
1. **Install.** Installs Ollama by its official route when it is missing, only after a yes. On macOS that is Homebrew's `ollama` formula when `brew` is there, else the app from ollama.com; on Linux, Ollama's install script.
2. **Start and weigh.** Starts the server, then shows the machine's memory and each model weighed against it.
3. **Pick.** The best suggested model that fits the budget, which the server has or the disk can take while keeping 10 GiB free. Else the largest model there that fits.
4. **Pull.** Pulls the model, after a yes. A pull that would leave the disk less than 10 GiB is refused.
5. **Check.** Checks it as a spawn would, and makes it `+local`'s.

`irie ctl local` prints the same table, with the disk space free for models and the model `+local` runs, and `irie ctl preflight` runs the check alone.

**Suggestions.** The models Irie suggests, best first: Bonsai 2 27B, Qwen3.8 27B, Qwen3.5 27B, Bonsai 2 9B, Qwen3.5 9B, Bonsai 2 4B, Qwen3.5 4B. Quantization sets much of the order. Bonsai 2 (PrismML, 2026) stores a Qwen model's weights as ternary values, −1, 0 or +1, at about two bits a weight: its 27B takes 8.3 GB where Qwen's own 4-bit 27B takes 17.7 GB, and keeps 98% of Qwen3.8-27B's benchmark average. A ternary model comes before a 4-bit one of the same base, which takes twice the memory and decodes at about half the speed for about a point; a larger base comes before a smaller one; below 4B a model is too weak for an agent's tool loop. So an 18 GiB Mac runs a 27B model at a 64k context (about 11 GB), and an 8 GiB laptop a 9B one (about 4.9 GB). Ollama's library serves Bonsai 2 in its `Q2_0` packing; the smaller `PTQ1_0` and `PQ2_0` packings need PrismML's own llama.cpp, which Irie runs as its llama-server (§6.5). NVIDIA's Nemotron models are left out, as of 2026-10-31. Nemotron 3.5 Lightning, a 30B mixture of experts with 3B active, takes at least 18.8 GB at any quantization, more than Qwen3.8 27B at 4 bits, and scored 24 on Artificial Analysis's index at its launch, where Qwen3.6 35B-A3B scored 32. NVIDIA's 4B models, Nemotron 3 Nano 4B and 3.5 Bolt E4B, are made for voice assistants and game characters, and in NVIDIA's own table trail Qwen3.5 4B on τ²-bench, 29 and 55 to 75.

**Not done, and why.**
- **Bundling Ollama.** Its macOS app is about 650 MB and has its own security updates, and the user's own install is one command away.
- **One session split between cloud and local**, such as an Opus orchestrator with local subagents. Claude Code sends every request to one `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`, so that needs a proxy that carries the user's Claude credentials, which §9 rules out. Separate sessions and `irie ctl spawn +local` delegation (§6.2) get the same effect.
- **Free cloud tiers built in.** They change too often to ship as defaults. A user adds one as a profile with a `cost` label. As of 2026-10-26:
  - Gemini CLI's free Google sign-in moved to Antigravity CLI for unpaid users on 2026-06-18.
  - Qwen Code's free OAuth tier was reported closed in April 2026.
  - OpenRouter's free models allow 50 requests a day (1,000 after $10 of credits), and an agent's task spends dozens.
  - Ollama's cloud lists no free use on its pricing page (2026-10-31): a free account pays per token, three requests at a time, and prompts are never logged or trained on. Nemotron 3 Super costs $0.015 a million tokens in and $0.60 out.
  - NVIDIA's API catalog, build.nvidia.com, serves its Nemotron models and others, Kimi K2.6 and GLM-5.3 Flash among them, free at about 40 requests a minute (2026-10-31). It speaks only OpenAI's API, so Claude Code would need a translating proxy. OpenRouter's free Nemotron models (3 Ultra, 3.5 Super VL, 3.5 Lightning) are NVIDIA's own endpoints under the same trial terms. Those allow testing and evaluation only, never production (§1.2, §1.4), forbid confidential data (§2.6), let NVIDIA use prompts and outputs to improve its models (§3.3), and forbid distributing what the model writes (§4.2), such as code pushed to a public repository.

### 6.4 Routing by cost per success

A cheap model that gets a task wrong costs a retry and the user's attention, so the router does not pick the cheapest profile. It picks the cheapest **per successful task**, and only among profiles accurate enough for the task. Hard work, orchestration included, stays on the strongest model, where Opus orchestrators give the best results; mechanical work goes where it costs least.

**The rule.** `spawn.default_profile` is `auto`, so a task with no `+profile` is routed; `+auto` routes explicitly, and any other `+profile` overrides.
1. **Class.** The task gets a class, `small`, `medium` or `hard`, from its words (`route.words`) and its size. Hard words count three, medium words one, and small words minus two; a long task or one with a list of steps counts more. Hard words include `design`, `refactor`, `debug`, `review`, `orchestrat`, `milestone` and `why`; small ones include `typo`, `rename`, `docstring`, `summarize` and `README`. Once the user has given 30 or more tasks their classes, every class among them, the class of the one nearest the task decides instead, unless the words call it hard (below).
2. **Accuracy.** For each profile in `route.profiles`, its accuracy on that class is the mean of a beta distribution: the starting guess (`route.accuracy`), counted as `route.prior_weight` outcomes, plus the user's own outcomes on that profile and class.
3. **Cost.** Cost per attempt is relative to the strongest profile: the starting guess (`route.cost`), replaced by what its sessions of that class cost, from their status lines, once three or more say.
   - A local model costs no tokens, only a hundredth of an Opus task in memory weight when its model takes the whole budget. It can be chosen only when Irie's Ollama server has a model for it and the machine can hold that model now (§6.3).
   - Profiles on the user's Claude plan cost up to five times more as its five hours pass `route.pressure` percent, and none can run at a window's limit.
4. **Choice.** The cheapest cost per success (cost ÷ accuracy) among usable profiles at or above their floor wins, the more accurate on a tie. The floor is `route.min_accuracy`, and `route.local_min_accuracy` for a local model's profile: a local model costs no tokens, so a miss costs only a retry, and the lower floor hands it more of the work. If none reaches its floor, the most accurate usable profile runs it.

With the starting guesses and floors of 85% and 60%, small work goes to `+local` when it is ready (else `+haiku`), medium work to `+sonnet`, and hard work to `+claude`; medium work goes local too once a local model's record there passes 60%, which a bench (§6.6) measures. To keep medium work on Opus, raise `route.min_accuracy` to 0.9 or more.

**Measured on an 18 GiB Mac (2026-11-14).** The starting guesses were checked with ten small subtasks of the kinds `irie ctl delegate` hands on:
- two summaries;
- two renames across files;
- two docstring passes;
- two tests, each of which must fail on broken versions of the code;
- a typo fix;
- a commit message.

Claude Code ran each one as a delegated session runs, with ten minutes for each, on an M3 Pro beside its owner's work:
- **Haiku** passed all ten, in a median of 17 s, at about $0.004 a task.
- **Dory 4B** passed 8, but took a median of 7.5 minutes a task, and twice ran into the ten minutes.
- **Qwen3.5 4B** passed 7, in a median of 95 s, holding 5.2 GB with the runner caps (§6.3).
- **FrogNano 4B** (Qwen3.5 4B trained further on coding agents' work) passed 6.
- **Bonsai 2 9B** passed both summaries, then ran out of time on both renames, and was stopped there.

A caller has to catch the local models' misses:
- functions left out of a docstring pass;
- code changed that the task said to keep;
- a test with wrong expected values;
- a commit subject copied from the template;
- a summary of a file the model never opened.

So measured, a 4B-class model belongs at 60–80% for small work in `route.accuracy`, where the starting guess for `local` is 85%.

As judges they gained little. On 72 pairs of the user's jots, the follow-up rule (§4.2) was right on 90%. The best local model scored 92%, Haiku 81%, and any one of them allowed only to overrule the rule's "yes" lifted it to 94%. On the class of 153 of the user's tasks, the rule was right on 50%, Bonsai 2 9B on 61% and Haiku on 68%.

**Classes from labelled tasks (measured 2026-11-15).** A small embedding model gets the class right more often than every local chat model measured. Each of the same 153 tasks took the class of the one nearest it among the other 152, by the cosine of their embeddings:
- **embeddinggemma-2 270M** (378 MB, 0.35 GB loaded, about 0.1 s a task) was right on 71%, as often as Haiku within the set's ±7 points. With every neighbour at a cosine of 0.9 or more left out, for jots sent twice, it was right on 72%. It called 3 of the 67 hard tasks small.
- **qwen3-embedding 0.6B** was right on 59%.
- **Neighbours voting.** The three to nine nearest voting were right on 64–67%, so the one nearest decides.
- **The words' hard stands.** Calling hard work easier is the costly mistake, since it puts the work on a weaker model. The nearest alone did that on 20 of the 153 tasks, the words alone on 44. Letting the words' "hard" stand, and the nearest decide the rest, was right on 72.5% and did it on 11. Taking the harder of the two did it on 8, but was right on only 66%.
- **Follow-ups.** The cosine of two jots says nothing about whether the second is more for the first: the rule with it as a veto stayed at 90%.

**Measured again (2026-11-21).** No other way of sorting the same 153 tasks, scored the same way, beat the nearest with the words' "hard" standing (72.5%):
- **Other ways over the same embeddings.** The three to seven nearest voting were right on 64–67%, and the nearest class average on 67%. Logistic regression was right on 63–75% across eight variants; its best is inside the ±7 points.
- **The model's classification prompt** (`task: classification | query: `) lowered it to 66%, so tasks go in plain.
- **Fewer numbers.** The first 512 of the 768 numbers sorted exactly as all 768 did; 256 lost 2 points and 128 five. Storing each as int8 lost one, and as one bit six.
- **No labels.** A decision model asked to pick small, medium or hard (laya, 421M, on Ollama's `/v1/systemone`) was right on 43%.
- **A probe.** A ridge classifier on Qwen3-1.7B's own states, averaged over the task's tokens, was right on 72.5% at its best block, the 24th of 28 (75% before the words' "hard"), and called hard work easier 14 times. That ties the 0.35 GB model with a 1.8 GB one.

So the router is at the ceiling of what its labels can teach, and more labels, not another model, would raise it. A 2026 study of 21 routers found the same plateau: they "converge to a narrow performance range", and more data, bigger encoders and fine-tuning together added 1.24 points on average.

So `route.embed` sorts tasks this way:
- **The labels.** `irie ctl route --label small|medium|hard TASK` gives one task its class, and `--import FILE` many: JSON lines, each with `text` and `class` (or a bench's eval set, with `fields.text` and `label`). They are kept in `route-labels.jsonl` in the state folder, never in a repository; a task given again takes its new class.
- **The model.** It runs on the user's own Ollama at `route.embed.url`, since Irie's own server holds one model, which an embedding would push out. The core embeds the labelled tasks in the background and keeps the embeddings in `route-vectors.json`, so a restarted core embeds only the task.
- **A task.** A task its words call hard is hard, with no embedding. For any other, a spawn waits at most 3 s for its task's embedding. The class of the nearest labelled task decides when its cosine is at least `route.embed.near` (0.75); this model's cosines run from about 0.7 up, so only a task unlike every labelled one is left to `route.words`, as is every task while fewer than 30 are embedded or a class has none. A server that fails is left alone for a minute, and the words decide meanwhile. `irie ctl route` says which decided: `like “…”, 0.82`, or the words.

**Learning.** Outcomes are folded from the journal, like every row's state, over its 30 days:

| Outcome | Counts as |
|---|---|
| The user rates a session (`+` or `-` on its row, `:rate good\|bad`, `irie ctl rate`) | good or bad; the latest rating stands |
| The same task (its words, ignoring case and spacing) is spawned within two hours on a profile with a higher starting accuracy for its class | bad for the earlier session (`outcome.escalated`), unless rated good |
| A turn ended, 30 minutes have passed, and neither of the above happened | good |
| A turn stopped at a usage limit, or no turn ended | nothing |

With a weight of 10, a profile's own record outweighs the guess after about ten outcomes. `irie ctl route "<task>"` and `:route <task>` show the class, each profile's accuracy with its number of outcomes, its floor, cost and cost per success, and the choice.

**Delegation.** A Claude Code session on a profile with cheaper ones in `route.profiles`, which was not itself delegated, is told at session start (SessionStart `additionalContext`) that it can delegate.
- **The call.** `"$IRIE_BIN" ctl delegate "<subtask>"` routes the subtask among the profiles cheaper than the caller's. A profile with no starting cost counts as the dearest.
- **In time.** The caller waits `--timeout` (10 minutes) for the answer, so a profile that takes longer than that to answer such a task is passed over (`answers in about 25m, past the 10m wait`). How long a profile takes is the median of its latest nine delegated subtasks of the class, from spawn to the end of the first turn, or of every class when the class has none, since a model's speed is mostly its own; a subtask its caller gave up on counts as taking at least the wait. Only delegations count: a session the user started may work for hours on a task of the same class. With no one waiting, as for a task spawned on `auto`, time does not count. `irie ctl route` shows each profile's time, as `≥2m` when the median is a wait its sessions ran past (`takes 2m or more`), and `--wait D` weighs a task as a subtask waited D for.
- **Where it runs.** In the caller's directory, with `route.delegate_mode` (`acceptEdits`), so it can edit without asking; a command still needs the user.
- **The result.** The call waits until the session's turn ends and prints the agent's last message: Stop's `last_assistant_message`, which the shim passes whole and the core keeps in `result.md`.
- **Handed on.** A routed session still at work at `--timeout` is stopped (`delegate.timed_out`; Irie closes it, so reopen brings it back), and the subtask goes to the next profile accurate enough, in the same directory and under the same name, with a note that its work so far is there to check. The call waits for that one as long, once. Running the same task again this way says nothing against the first session's accuracy: it was slow, not wrong.
- **Exit codes.** 3 when no cheaper profile is accurate enough ("do it yourself"); 4 when the session is still at work at `--timeout` and no other profile can take it over, or the one that did is still at work too, where it goes on as a row marked `↳ delegated`.
- **Choosing what to hand on.** The note asks for self-contained, mechanical subtasks on files the caller is not editing, and keeps planning, design and review with the caller. It names the local profiles among those it may hand work to, which cost no tokens, and asks it to prefer them for every subtask that fits, such as reading and summarising files, searches, renames, docstrings and simple tests, and to check what comes back.

**Fallback models.** Beside the plan's session and weekly limits, which every model shares, a model family can have a usage limit of its own, as Fable has. Claude Code stops the turn with `rate_limit` and says whose limit it was ("You've reached your Fable limit. Run /usage-credits to continue or switch models with /model."), and a model outside that family keeps working. A profile with `fallback_model` makes that switch on its own, in the same session:
- **The switch.** A turn whose message names its own model's family (`<family> limit`, for fable, opus, sonnet or haiku) makes the core type `/model <fallback_model>` into the agent's prompt, as continue types its word: only into the agent's own prompt with nothing typed in it, and Enter only once the prompt shows it. Its PreModelSwitch hook allows that switch, so it goes without the confirmation Claude Code asks while the prompt cache is warm. The agent's process goes on, with its work in the background and its teammates; the next request reads the whole conversation again on the new model.
- **Carrying on.** Once PostModelSwitch reports the new model (`model.switched`), the core waits 2 s and types the agent a note to carry on from where it stopped, unless Claude Code carried on by itself, as it does when the switch ends its own wait for a reset. The card shows the model it runs now, and a fork carries that model on.
- **The user's default.** `/model <name>` also saves the name as the default for new sessions, in the `model` key of the user's `settings.json`. The core reads the key before it types and puts it back as it was afterwards, or takes it out when there was none, changing that value alone, byte for byte, and keeping a link to the file. A session keeps the model it switched to when the key changes under it (checked with Claude Code 2.1.325). One switch runs at a time, so none puts back a default another saved.
- **New sessions.** For an hour after a model's own limit last stopped a turn (`limitHold`), a launch of a profile with a fallback starts on the fallback, with a warning that says why; a model asked for by name, as for a fork, stays. The journal's `route.fallback` events carry the hour over a restart. After it, a session that finds the limit still there switches in place.
- **When it can't.** A session started with `--disable-slash-commands` has no `/model`, so a profile with `fallback_model` may not pass it, and an older session waits for the reset. So does a session whose settings file, written at its launch, lacks the PreModelSwitch and PostModelSwitch hooks, as one from before Irie had them, or on a Claude Code older than 2.1.295: its `/model` would stop at Claude Code's confirmation. Reopening it gives it them. So does one whose prompt holds something typed, whose terminal is scrolled back, or whose Claude Code reports no new model within 30 s; its `route.fallback` says why. The plan's own limits switch nothing: they are shared, so failover (below) and the trod bot handle them. A switch never buys usage credits.

**Failover.** With `route.failover`, a turn that stops with `rate_limit` makes the core route the task again among profiles cheaper than the session's, keeping its class. A turn its model's own limit stopped, on a profile with a fallback, switches models instead.
- **Carrying on.** A profile accurate enough carries it on in the same directory. Its task opens with a note that the earlier session stopped at its limit and that its work so far is there to check.
- **Waiting.** When none is accurate enough, as for hard work after Opus's limit, the task waits for the reset, as Claude Code does, and the session's `route.failover` event says so.
- **When the plan is out.** At a window's limit, every profile on the plan is unusable, so only a local model can carry the task on.
- **Once.** A session fails over once.

**In the sidebar.** `:setup` runs `irie setup local` as a tool session, shown in the slot with the focus to answer its questions; `:models` runs `irie ctl local`. A tool session takes no task and is at work from its start. The status strip shows a routed session's class and profile (`small→local`), `↳ delegated`, and its rating.

### 6.5 Big models in little memory: llama-server, quantization and streaming

A coding agent wants the largest model it can get, and a laptop's memory is the limit: about two thirds of it, less whatever else is open. Two ways around the limit came to laptops in 2026 (research of 2026-10-31):

- **Quantization below four bits.** Bonsai 2 (PrismML) stores Qwen3.8-27B's weights as ternary values in 5.95 GB (`PTQ1_0`, 1.75 bits a weight), 7.21 GB (`PQ2_0`) or 7.63 GB (`Q2_0`), and keeps 98% of the model's benchmark average, where Qwen's own 4-bit build takes 17.6 GB. Ollama's library has only the `Q2_0` packing (`bonsai-2`, which §6.3 suggests first); the two smaller ones need PrismML's build of llama.cpp. At a 64k `q8_0` context a 27B in `PTQ1_0` takes about 8.8 GB, where Ollama's `bonsai-2:27b` takes about 11.4 GB with its vision projector.
- **Streaming weights from disk.** A mixture-of-experts model uses a few of its experts for each token, so an engine can keep the experts on the SSD and read the ones a token needs, with the hot ones cached in memory: flash-moe runs a 397B on a 48 GB Mac at 4–6 tokens a second, slipstream (a llama.cpp fork) 35B–480B coding models on 16–36 GB, slotstream a 125B on 16 GB. Each is its own build, each needs a model of its own layout, and every token waits on the SSD. Stock llama.cpp and Ollama do not stream: on Apple silicon a model larger than the GPU's working set fails. Measured on an 18 GiB M3 Pro with Homebrew's llama.cpp build 10180: a 16.8 GB 27B with 24 of its 64 layers on the GPU and the rest memory-mapped loaded past Metal's recommended working set and failed every request with a compute error.
